Foreword





Dear JAMB Candidate,



You are holding the most comprehensive JAMB Chemistry resource ever compiled for Nigerian students. This book contains over 2,000 carefully curated past questions from JAMB examinations spanning 1983 to 2025 — more than four decades of examination history in one powerful study tool.



I did not write this book because Chemistry is easy. I wrote it because I know how terrifying JAMB can feel when you are staring at a question that seems completely foreign after months of studying. The number one thing that separates students who score 280+ from those who score below 200 is simple: consistent exposure to past questions with proper explanation.



Every question in this book comes with a detailed explanation — not just the answer, but WHY it is the answer. Understanding the reasoning is what will serve you in the exam hall when JAMB slightly rephrases a question or changes the numbers.



My advice: Do not read this book passively. Attempt each question before checking the answer. Time yourself. Review your mistakes obsessively. A wrong answer today is a lesson that becomes a right answer in the exam hall.



Chemistry is not memorization — it is understanding. Once you understand WHY sodium reacts violently with water, you will never forget it. Once you understand WHY the Haber process uses 450°C, every equilibrium question becomes clearer. This book will help you reach that level of understanding.



With faith in your success,

How to Use This Book





Your 4-Phase JAMB Chemistry Attack Plan



PHASE 1 — FOUNDATION (Weeks 1-2):

Read each topic's notes section carefully before attempting questions.

Ask yourself: "Can I explain this concept to someone else?" If not, re-read.



PHASE 2 — TOPIC MASTERY (Weeks 3-6):

Attempt ALL questions in Topics 1-14, one topic at a time.

After each topic, review every wrong answer and read its full explanation.

Keep a mistake journal — revisit it weekly.



PHASE 3 — INTEGRATION (Weeks 7-8):

Tackle Topics 15-18 and the Mixed Questions section.

You should now recognize question patterns and recall coming faster.



PHASE 4 — FINAL SPRINT (Last 2 Weeks):

Retake your weakest topics. Revisit your mistake journal.

Do full timed mock sessions: 40 Chemistry questions in 30 minutes (JAMB pace).

Aim for 35+/40 correct.



💡 EXAM TIP:  JAMB FORMAT: Chemistry = 40 questions. Each correct answer = 2.5 marks (Chemistry counts to 100 on the 400 total score). Aim for at least 35 correct to protect your aggregate.

01

🔬 SEPARATION OF MIXTURES

Filtration · Distillation · Chromatography · Sublimation · Magnetic Separation



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Imagine you have a bag of mixed sweets — toffees, chocolates, and gummies mixed together. To separate them, you could pick by hand (like magnetic separation), sieve them by size (like filtration), or sort by colour (like chromatography). Chemistry does the same thing — just with molecules!



1.1 What is a Mixture?

A MIXTURE is formed when two or more substances are physically combined but NOT chemically joined. Think of it like mixing rice and beans — both keep their own properties, and you can separate them. A PURE SUBSTANCE has only one type of particle throughout.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Sea water contains over 70 different dissolved substances! The most abundant is sodium chloride (NaCl) — common table salt — which makes up about 3.5% of sea water by mass. Nigeria's Dead Sea equivalent is Lake Chad, which is getting saltier as it shrinks due to climate change.



TYPE

SIMPLE EXPLANATION & EXAMPLES

Homogeneous Mixture

Looks the same throughout — one phase. You cannot see separate parts. E.g., salt dissolved in water, air, steel alloy, vinegar. The salt molecules are evenly spread — you cannot see them.

Heterogeneous Mixture

Clearly has different parts — you can see them. E.g., sand + water (sand visible at bottom), oil + water (two clear layers), salad (different vegetables visible).

Pure Substance

Contains only one type of molecule or atom. Has a SHARP melting point. E.g., pure water (boils at exactly 100°C), pure NaCl, pure gold. Impurities BROADEN and LOWER the melting point.

Colloid

Particles in between — 1 to 1000 nm. Cannot see particles, does not settle, but scatters light. E.g., milk, fog, starch in water, blood, smoke. Shows the Tyndall Effect.



1.2  FILTRATION — Separating Insoluble Solid from Liquid

Think of it like a sieve at the kitchen sink — big pasta pieces stay in the sieve, water drains through. Filter paper has microscopic holes that allow liquid molecules to pass through but block solid particles.



FILTRATION APPARATUS

                    MIXTURE

                    (solid + liquid)

                        |

            ┌───────────▼───────────┐

            │      FUNNEL           │

            │   [Filter Paper]      │

            │ ∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿∿   │

            │                       │

            └────────────┬──────────┘

                         │   FILTRATE

                         ▼  (clear liquid)

                    ┌─────────┐

                    │         │

                    │  BEAKER │

                    └─────────┘

  RESIDUE stays on filter paper (insoluble solid)



THE FILTRATE is the liquid that passes through (it is clear). THE RESIDUE is the solid that stays on the filter paper.

EXAMPLES IN EVERYDAY LIFE: Straining tea leaves ✦ Filtering sand from water ✦ Removing chalk from water ✦ Removing precipitates (e.g., AgCl↓) from solution in the lab.

⚠️ COMMON MISTAKE

FILTRATION ONLY WORKS IF THE SOLID IS INSOLUBLE. If you try to filter dissolved salt (NaCl) from water, the dissolved particles are so tiny (ions in solution) that they pass straight through the filter paper with the water — you cannot separate them this way!

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

In water treatment plants in Nigeria, huge sand filter beds are used to filter dirty river water. The sand acts like a giant filter paper. However, dissolved salts and bacteria are too small for the sand to catch — that is why chlorination is also needed!



1.3  EVAPORATION and CRYSTALLISATION

EVAPORATION — Getting the solid back from a solution

When you heat a solution, the solvent (water) turns into steam and escapes. The dissolved solid is left behind. Think of it like leaving a wet footprint on a hot road — the water evaporates and you can see the salt/minerals left behind.

BEST FOR: Thermally stable salts like NaCl, KNO₃ that do not decompose when heated.

NOT SUITABLE FOR: Salts that decompose on heating (like CuSO₄·5H₂O above 100°C loses its water of crystallisation; sugar turns black when strongly heated).

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The world's oldest salt production is in Nigeria! Uneme-Uzanu in Edo State has been producing salt by traditional evaporation methods for centuries. Women collect brine from salt springs, filter it, then evaporate it over wood fires to get crystalline salt.

CRYSTALLISATION — Getting PURE crystals from a solution

Crystallisation is like evaporation's more careful older sibling. Instead of evaporating ALL the solvent, you concentrate the solution by heating, then cool it slowly. As the temperature drops, the main substance can no longer stay dissolved — it 'falls out' as beautiful crystals. Impurities stay in the remaining liquid (called 'mother liquor').

STEPS (remember as: DHFC-WD)

DISSOLVE — Dissolve the impure solid in the MINIMUM amount of HOT solvent

HOT FILTER — Filter while still hot (removes insoluble impurities — they stay on the paper)

COOL — Cool the filtrate slowly — crystals form as solubility decreases

FILTER AGAIN — Filter off the crystals

WASH — Wash crystals with a small amount of cold solvent

DRY — Dry at a low temperature to avoid decomposition

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Making pure copper sulphate crystals: 1. Add impure CuSO₄ to hot water → stir until dissolved 2. Filter hot to remove sand/dirt 3. Cool the blue solution slowly 4. Beautiful blue CuSO₄·5H₂O crystals form 5. Filter off, wash with a few drops of cold water, leave to dry at room temperature



1.4  DISTILLATION — Separating Liquids Using Boiling Points

Simple Distillation — One liquid from a solution

Imagine boiling salty water. The water turns to steam but the salt doesn't — it stays behind. If you collect that steam and cool it back to liquid, you get pure water! That is simple distillation.



SIMPLE DISTILLATION APPARATUS

  HEAT                 Thermometer

    ▲              (reads distillate bp)

    │                      |

 ┌──┴──┐          ┌────────┴──────────────────┐

 │Flask│──vapour─►│      Liebig Condenser      │──►  Distillate

 │     │          │  cold water ──►            │     (pure liquid)

 └─────┘          │  ◄── cold water in         │

  contains         └───────────────────────────┘

  mixture



  PURE LIQUID collected. Solid (or higher-bp liquid) stays in flask.



THERMOMETER TIP: The thermometer bulb MUST be at the side-arm junction (where vapour leaves the flask). It reads the temperature of the vapour being collected — this tells you what substance is distilling over.

Fractional Distillation — Separating liquids with SIMILAR boiling points

For liquids whose boiling points are close together (like the different components in crude oil), simple distillation is not good enough. A FRACTIONATING COLUMN is added — it has glass beads or plates inside that give the vapour many chances to condense and re-evaporate. Each time it condenses and re-evaporates, it gets richer in the lower-boiling component. The lowest-boiling component reaches the top first.

APPLICATION

HOW IT WORKS

Petroleum refining

Crude oil → LPG (gas, bp < 25°C) → Petrol (bp 25–75°C) → Kerosene (75–200°C) → Diesel (200–350°C) → Lubricating oil → Bitumen. Lowest bp = top of tower. Highest bp = bottom.

Liquid air separation

Air cooled to −200°C (liquefies). Nitrogen (bp −196°C) boils off first at TOP. Argon (bp −186°C) in middle. Oxygen (bp −183°C) at BOTTOM.

Ethanol purification

Fermentation mixture (water + ethanol) → fractional distillation → 96% ethanol (water-ethanol azeotrope forms at 96% — cannot go higher by distillation alone).

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Nigeria's NNPC refineries in Port Harcourt and Kaduna use fractional distillation on crude oil from the Niger Delta. Each 'fraction' collected at different heights in the tower has a different use — from cooking gas (LPG) to road tar (bitumen). One barrel of crude oil (159 litres) typically gives about 47% petrol, 23% diesel, and various other fractions.



1.5  CHROMATOGRAPHY — Separating Mixtures by Affinity

Imagine you spill ink on paper and water moves through the spill. Different dyes in the ink move at different speeds because some 'cling' more strongly to the paper, while others are 'pulled' more by the water. The result is a rainbow of separated colours. That is paper chromatography!



PAPER CHROMATOGRAPHY & Rf CALCULATION

  ▲  Solvent front ─────────────────────── 9.0 cm

  │

  │                          ● Component A ── 6.0 cm

  │

  │             ● Component B ──────────── 3.0 cm

  │

  │   ● Component C ─────────────────── 1.5 cm

  │

  │  × Origin (starting spot) ───────── 0.0 cm

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────►



  Rf(A) = 6.0/9.0 = 0.67    Rf(B) = 3.0/9.0 = 0.33    Rf(C) = 1.5/9.0 = 0.17

  Rf ALWAYS between 0 and 1.  Same Rf = same substance.



WHAT IS Rf? Rf stands for 'Retardation Factor' or 'Retention Factor'. It tells you how far a substance travels relative to the solvent. It is a fingerprint for that substance — the same substance always has the same Rf value under the same conditions.

Rf  =  Distance moved by substance ÷ Distance moved by solvent front

Rf is ALWAYS between 0 and 1 (NEVER more than 1!)

If two substances have the SAME Rf = they are the SAME substance

Pure substance → ONE SPOT | Mixture → MULTIPLE SPOTS

💡 EXAM TIP

JAMB LOVE: Rf value questions! Key rules: (1) Rf can never be >1 — the substance cannot travel faster than the solvent. (2) Rf=0 means the substance did not move at all (very strong affinity for stationary phase). (3) Rf=1 means substance moved with the solvent front (very strong affinity for mobile phase).

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Chromatography was invented in 1900 by Russian botanist Mikhail Tsvet who used it to separate plant pigments (hence the name — 'chroma' = colour in Greek). Today, it is used in Nigerian NAFDAC laboratories to test food and drugs for contaminants, and in forensic labs to analyse evidence.



1.6  SUBLIMATION — Solid Directly to Gas

Most substances melt to liquid before they evaporate. But some special substances jump directly from SOLID to GAS, skipping the liquid stage entirely. This is called SUBLIMATION. On cooling, the gas goes back directly to solid (called DEPOSITION).

Think of it like dry ice (solid CO₂) — it doesn't become liquid water, it just vanishes as white 'smoke' (which is actually CO₂ gas). Or like iodine — a dark solid that turns to beautiful purple vapour when gently heated.

SUBSTANCES THAT SUBLIME: Iodine (I₂) — purple vapour ✦ Ammonium chloride (NH₄Cl) — white crystals reform ✦ Naphthalene (mothballs) ✦ Dry ice (CO₂) ✦ Benzoic acid

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Separating NH₄Cl from sand: 1. Mix NH₄Cl + sand in an evaporating dish 2. Cover with an inverted cold funnel (or cold glass) 3. Heat gently from below 4. NH₄Cl sublimes (vapour rises) and deposits as WHITE CRYSTALS on the cold funnel 5. Sand remains in the dish → NH₄Cl and sand are now separated!

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Iodine sublimation is used as a forensic technique to reveal fingerprints! When iodine crystals are gently heated near a paper surface with fingerprints, the iodine vapour sticks to the oils left by fingers, revealing the print as a brown mark. Nigerian police forensic units use this technique.



1.7  MAGNETIC SEPARATION

The simplest separation method! If one substance in a mixture is magnetic (like iron), you just pass a magnet over the mixture. The magnetic material sticks to the magnet; the non-magnetic material stays behind.

EXAMPLES: Iron filings from sulfur ✦ Iron ore concentration in mining ✦ Removing iron particles from flour in food processing ✦ Scrap metal recycling — electromagnets lift iron/steel from mixed waste.



1.8  Quick Reference — Which Method to Use?

MIXTURE / PROBLEM

BEST METHOD

Sand from water

FILTRATION — sand is insoluble

NaCl from water

EVAPORATION or CRYSTALLISATION — NaCl is dissolved

Pure water from sea water

SIMPLE DISTILLATION

Crude oil into fractions

FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION

N₂ and O₂ from air

FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION of liquid air

Ink components (dyes)

CHROMATOGRAPHY

NH₄Cl from sand

SUBLIMATION — NH₄Cl sublimes, sand does not

Iron filings from sulfur

MAGNETIC SEPARATION

NaCl + NH₄Cl + BaSO₄

Step 1: SUBLIMATION (remove NH₄Cl) → Step 2: Add water, FILTER (remove BaSO₄) → Step 3: EVAPORATE (get NaCl)

Ethanol from fermented mixture

FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The process of reverse osmosis (RO) is used in Lagos to purify water for bottling. Water is forced under very high pressure through a membrane with pores so tiny that only water molecules can pass through — dissolved salts, heavy metals, even bacteria and viruses are blocked. It is essentially super-fine filtration!

🧠 REMEMBER

MASTER RULE: Ask WHY the method works. Filtration = particle SIZE difference. Distillation = BOILING POINT difference. Chromatography = AFFINITY difference. Sublimation = one component SUBLIMES. Magnetic = one component is MAGNETIC. Know the reason, not just the name.

To test whether a substance is a pure compound or a mixture, you check

  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Add water→NaCl and NH4Cl dissolve, BaSO4 doesn't→filter off BaSO4→sublime NH4Cl from the filtrate, leaving NaCl.



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Brine is NaCl dissolved in water. Evaporation removes water leaving solid NaCl crystals.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Chromatography separates coloured/ionic mixtures by differential migration rates through a medium.



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sea-water is a mixture of water, dissolved salts, gases — variable composition, components retain their individual properties.



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Evaporation = escape of surface molecules into vapour below boiling point.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Chromatography exploits different affinities for mobile and stationary phases to separate ink components.



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  NH4Cl sublimes — converts directly from solid to vapour, then re-deposits as crystals on cooler surfaces.



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Pure substance has a SHARP melting point. A RANGE indicates a MIXTURE — different components melt at different temperatures.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dissolving NaCl is physical — no new substance forms, reversible by evaporation.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  N2 (BP -196°C), Ar (-186°C), O2 (-183°C) in liquid air have close but different BPs — ideal for fractional distillation.



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Flood water contains suspended soil and debris — HETEROGENEOUS. Others are uniform solutions.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rf = distance moved by spot ÷ distance moved by solvent front. Unique to chromatography.



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sublime NH4Cl first, then add water (NaCl dissolves, sand doesn't), filter sand, evaporate to get NaCl.



Question 14

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Components MIGRATE at different speeds due to different affinities for stationary vs mobile phase.



Question 15

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  A separating funnel separates immiscible liquids (e.g. oil and water) that form two distinct layers.



Question 16

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  SUBLIMATION: direct solid→gas conversion. Examples: iodine, NH4Cl, naphthalene, dry ice.



Question 17

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Iron filings are MAGNETIC; sulphur is NOT. A magnet attracts iron filings from the sulphur mixture.



Question 18

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sieving works when particles are DIFFERENT sizes — smaller ones pass through, larger ones are retained.



Question 19

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  I2 dissolves in CS2; sulphur does not. Add CS2 → I2 dissolves → filter off sulphur → evaporate CS2.



Question 20

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Distillation: vaporise the solvent → condense → collect as pure liquid.



Question 21

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Different affinities for stationary phase → different migration speeds → separation.



Question 22

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Freezing ice-cream = change of state (liquid→solid). No new chemical substance formed.



Question 23

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  SUBLIMATION = direct solid-to-gas conversion without passing through the liquid phase.



Question 24

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Crystallisation gives PURE crystals. Concentrate solution then cool → crystals form → filter → dry.



Question 25

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO2+2NaOH→Na2CO3+H2O. CO does NOT react with NaOH. The NaOH absorbs CO2, leaving pure CO.



Question 26

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ethanol-water form an azeotrope at ~96%. A dehydrant (CaO) breaks it, allowing >96% ethanol.



Question 27

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Paper/TLC chromatography separates chlorophyll a, b, carotenoids by differential migration.



Question 28

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Evaporating (liquid→gas) and condensing (gas→liquid) are state changes — physical processes.



Question 29

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Alcohol (BP 78°C) and water (BP 100°C) are miscible liquids with different but close BPs → fractional distillation.



Question 30

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Industrial water treatment: sedimentation→coagulation→sand filtration→chlorination.



Question 31

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rf = distance by substance ÷ distance by solvent. Distance = 0.6 × 15 = 9.0 cm.



Question 32

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Decantation = carefully pouring the supernatant liquid from above the settled solid.



Question 33

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cooling decreases solubility. Excess solute comes out as CRYSTALS.



Question 34

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  EVAPORATION heats the solution until water vaporises, leaving solid salt crystals.



Question 35

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ACTIVATED CHARCOAL has enormous surface area that adsorbs coloured organic impurities.



Question 36

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Petrol (BP 20-70°C) and kerosene (BP 120-240°C) are miscible liquids with different BPs.



Question 37

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Most soluble in MOBILE PHASE → spends less time adsorbed → travels further → highest Rf.



Question 38

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SUBLIMATION = direct solid-to-gas conversion. Examples: I2, dry ice, NH4Cl, naphthalene.



Question 39

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SHARP boiling/melting point at the correct value = pure substance. Impurities cause melting point depression and boiling point elevation.



Question 40

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Liquid air contains N2 (78%), O2 (21%), Ar (0.93%). All three are separated by fractional distillation.



Question 41

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DISTILLATION separates liquids based on different boiling points. The more volatile liquid vaporises first.



Question 42

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  IODINE sublimes on gentle heating; sand does not. Iodine vapour rises and re-deposits on cooling.



Question 43

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CRYSTALLISATION: cool supersaturated solution → crystals nucleate and grow → filter and dry.



Question 44

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Paper chromatography separates based on DIFFERENTIAL SOLUBILITY in the mobile phase relative to the stationary phase.



Question 45

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Step 1: Magnet removes iron. Step 2: Water dissolves salt (sand doesn't dissolve). Step 3: Filter sand. Evaporate filtrate to get salt.



Question 46

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Burning coal = chemical change: coal + O2 → CO2 + H2O. New substances formed. Irreversible under normal conditions.



Question 47

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  A PURE substance melts at a single specific temperature. A range of melting temperatures = mixture.



Question 48

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Simple distillation is best for separating a DISSOLVED SOLID from its solvent (e.g. salt from salt water).



Question 49

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  RESIDUE = material that stays on the filter paper. FILTRATE = liquid that passes through.



Question 50

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION of crude oil separates it into fractions (refinery gas, petrol, naphtha, kerosene, diesel, lubricating oil, bitumen) based on their different boiling point ranges.



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CuSO4 is a PURE COMPOUND — fixed composition, sharp melting point. Tap water, air, milk are all mixtures.



Question 52

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  IMMISCIBLE liquids (e.g. oil and water) form separate layers. Separated using a separating funnel.



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  N2 (BP -196°C) and O2 (BP -183°C) differ by 13°C. FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION separates them. N2 boils off first.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sharp melting point + complete dissolution = characteristics of a PURE COMPOUND.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fractional distillation uses a fractionating column to separate liquids with CLOSE boiling points by repeated vaporisation-condensation cycles.



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rf = distance moved by spot ÷ distance moved by solvent front. Always between 0 and 1.



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Filtration separates an INSOLUBLE SOLID from a liquid. The solid stays as residue; the liquid passes through as filtrate.



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fractionating column provides many condensation-evaporation stages → greater separation of liquids with similar BPs.



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NAPHTHALENE (mothballs) sublimes — direct solid to vapour. NaCl melts at 801°C. CuSO4 loses water. CaCO3 decomposes.



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CRYSTALLISATION: heat solution to concentrate, then cool → crystals form as solubility decreases.



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sand and water is HETEROGENEOUS — visible separation of components, sand settles out. The others appear uniform.



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Pure liquid boils at a FIXED temperature (sharp boiling point). Impurities raise the boiling point and cause it to boil over a range.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  BITUMEN is the heaviest fraction — it remains as residue at the bottom of the fractionating column with BP >350°C. Refinery gas (BP <20°C) is the lightest.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Burning wood: wood + O2 → CO2 + H2O + ash. New chemical substances formed. CHEMICAL change.



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Process: Add water (salt dissolves, sand doesn't) → Filter (sand removed) → Evaporate filtrate to get pure salt crystals.



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Mayonnaise is an EMULSION — oil droplets dispersed in water (or vice versa), stabilised by egg yolk lecithin as emulsifier.



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  MIXTURE: two or more substances physically combined, retaining individual properties, separable by physical means, variable composition.



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  FILTRATION separates insoluble solid from liquid. The solid (insoluble) stays on filter paper; liquid passes through.



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Distance = Rf × solvent distance = 0.4 × 25 = 10 cm.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Salt is non-volatile. Distill off the alcohol (BP 78°C) → salt remains in the flask.



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  KEROSENE (also called aviation fuel, paraffin, jet A1) is used in jet aircraft engines. BP range 120-240°C.



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  MOBILE PHASE = the solvent (liquid or gas) that moves through the stationary phase, carrying the sample components with it.



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  BLOOD is a HETEROGENEOUS MIXTURE — contains plasma (solution), red blood cells, white blood cells, platelets — different components visible under microscope.



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION of crude oil in a fractionating column separates it into useful fractions (petrol, kerosene, diesel, etc.) based on their different boiling point ranges.



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CONDENSATION = gas/vapour → liquid on cooling. This is what happens when steam meets a cold surface. It is the reverse of evaporation.



Question 76

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Add water: salt dissolves. Filter: sand stays. Evaporate filtrate: salt. Use CS2/organic solvent to extract sulphur from the sand-sulphur mixture, then evaporate solvent to get sulphur.



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CENTRIFUGATION uses rapid spinning to separate components by density. Denser particles settle to the bottom faster. Used in separating blood cells from plasma, cream from milk.



Question 78

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kerosene and palm oil are both NON-POLAR. 'Like dissolves like' — non-polar solvents dissolve non-polar solutes. The oil dissolves in kerosene and is washed away.



Question 79

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Benzene and water are IMMISCIBLE — they form two separate layers. A separating funnel separates immiscible liquids. Ethanol/water, petrol/kerosene, methylated spirit/water are all miscible pairs.



Question 80

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Pure substances do NOT have variable boiling points — they have FIXED boiling points at given pressure. Boiling points varying with concentration applies to SOLUTIONS (mixtures), not pure substances.



Question 81

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Mg + 2HCl → MgCl2 + H2↑. New substances formed (MgCl2 and H2). CHEMICAL change — irreversible, produces gas, temperature changes observed.



Question 82

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Air is first LIQUEFIED by compression and cooling, then subjected to FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION. N2 (BP -196°C) boils off first, then Ar (-186°C), then O2 (-183°C).



Question 83

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  LOWEST Rf = most strongly attracted to STATIONARY phase (paper) → moves least. High affinity for stationary phase means the substance 'holds on' to the paper and doesn't migrate as far with the solvent.



Question 84

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  RESIDUE on filter paper = the INSOLUBLE SOLID that cannot pass through the pores of the filter paper.



Question 85

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CONDENSER: a water-cooled tube through which vapour passes → CONDENSES into liquid (distillate). The cold water flowing through the outer jacket removes heat from the vapour.



Question 86

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ethanol BP = 78°C, water BP = 100°C. Different BPs allow fractional distillation. The component with LOWER BP (ethanol) distils over first.



Question 87

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Iodine (solid) dissolved in ethanol (liquid) → simple distillation. Distil off the ethanol (BP 78°C) → leave iodine crystals behind.



Question 88

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SMOKE = AEROSOL = tiny solid particles dispersed in a gas (air). Particle size 1-100 nm. Shows Tyndall effect.



Question 89

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Petroleum fractions are separated by FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION based on their DIFFERENT BOILING POINTS. Heavier molecules have higher BPs and condense lower in the column.



Question 90

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BOILING = converting liquid to vapour at the boiling point (throughout the liquid, not just at the surface). Evaporation occurs below the boiling point, only at the surface.



Question 91

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Melting point is the definitive purity test. PURE substance: sharp, single temperature. IMPURE: melts over a range OR at a temperature different from the expected value.



Question 92

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  FRACTIONAL CRYSTALLISATION: KNO3 is much more soluble at high T than NaCl. Cool a hot saturated mixture → KNO3 crystallises out first → separated from NaCl still in solution.



Question 93

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  In a fractionating column: lowest BP (most volatile) = lightest molecules → rise to the TOP before condensing. Highest BP (heaviest molecules) = condense at the BOTTOM or remain as residue.



Question 94

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  FOG = liquid aerosol = tiny water droplets dispersed in air (gas). Particle size 1-100 nm → colloidal dispersion → shows Tyndall effect (beam of light visible through fog).



Question 95

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  The mixture must be DISSOLVED in a suitable solvent to form a solution, then spotted on the paper. The solvent must dissolve all components for them to migrate with the mobile phase.



Question 96

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Thermometer bulb placed at the SIDE-ARM/NECK of the flask — exactly where vapour exits. This ensures the temperature reading corresponds to the boiling point of the vapour being collected.



Question 97

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Add water → A dissolves, B doesn't → FILTER → B stays as residue → EVAPORATE filtrate → A crystallises out.



Question 98

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fractional distillation does NOT produce a single pure substance in one step — it produces FRACTIONS, each still containing a mixture. But it does separate many fractions simultaneously, unlike simple distillation.



Question 99

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  The principle 'LIKE DISSOLVES LIKE' (polarity principle): polar solvents dissolve polar/ionic solutes; non-polar solvents dissolve non-polar solutes. Not named as a formal law but fundamental in chemistry.



Question 100

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  TWO-DIMENSIONAL CHROMATOGRAPHY: run with solvent 1 in one direction, then rotate 90° and run with a DIFFERENT solvent. Substances with same Rf in one solvent are separated by the second solvent.



Question 101

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DISTILLATE = the condensed vapour collected. It is the purified component that has evaporated, been cooled in the condenser, and collected in the receiving flask.



Question 102

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ANTI-BUMPING GRANULES (boiling chips) provide nucleation sites for bubble formation → SMOOTH BOILING. Without them, liquid can superheat and then boil violently (bumping), which is dangerous.



Question 103

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  VOLATILITY = tendency to vaporise. DISTILLATION separates substances based on different volatilities (different BPs). The more volatile (lower BP) component distils first.



Question 104

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  PURE SUBSTANCE: has a single definite chemical composition, fixed physical properties (sharp MP, BP, density), and consists of only one type of particle (element or compound).



Question 105

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  LOCATING AGENTS for colourless spots: UV light (fluorescent substances glow), iodine vapour (organic substances turn brown), ninhydrin (amino acids turn purple). For coloured spots, direct observation suffices.



Question 106

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NINHYDRIN is the standard locating agent for amino acids. It reacts with amino acid amino groups to give PURPLE/PINK colour (Ruhemann's purple). Used after developing the chromatogram.



Question 107

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  COMBUSTION is a CHEMICAL CHANGE (burning). Distillation, chromatography, and filtration are all physical separation techniques that do NOT form new chemical substances.



Question 108

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  DECANTATION: carefully pouring off liquid from above a SETTLED (sedimented) insoluble solid. Works after the solid has fully settled to the bottom. Simpler than filtration but less complete.



Question 109

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Air is liquefied by COMPRESSION (raises temperature) then COOLING (removes heat). The Linde process: compress → cool → expand (Joule-Thomson effect) → further cooling → liquefaction.



Question 110

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  TYNDALL EFFECT shown by COLLOIDS (1-100 nm particles scatter visible light). True solution particles (<1 nm) are too small to scatter visible light → no Tyndall effect. Classic test: shine laser beam through liquid.



Question 111

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ADSORPTION: activated charcoal has enormous surface area (up to 3000 m²/g!) that physically attracts and holds coloured organic molecules on its surface. Used in water purification, gas masks, medical detoxification.



Question 112

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gas chromatography: MOBILE PHASE = inert carrier gas (N2, He, H2). STATIONARY PHASE = liquid or solid coated inside the column. Sample components separate based on different affinities for stationary phase.



Question 113

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sugar is dissolved in water (miscible). Filtration won't work. Distillation would decompose sugar. EVAPORATION or CRYSTALLISATION removes water, leaving solid sugar.



Question 114

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Crude oil has DOZENS of fractions with boiling points ranging from <20°C to >500°C. Simple distillation would give only two fractions (below and above one temperature). Fractional distillation handles the complexity by providing many theoretical plates.



Question 115

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Purity of crystals is improved by WASHING with small amounts of COLD SOLVENT — this removes soluble impurities coating the crystal surfaces without dissolving too much of the desired product.



Question 116

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  HPLC is an advanced form of COLUMN CHROMATOGRAPHY — sample is pumped through a column of stationary phase under HIGH PRESSURE, achieving much faster separation and higher resolution than regular column chromatography.



Question 117

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CENTRIFUGATION spins blood at high speed → denser red blood cells settle to bottom → white cells above → plasma at top. This is how hospitals separate blood components for transfusions and tests.



Question 118

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Chromatography separates mixtures by differential migration — components move at different speeds depending on their affinity for stationary vs mobile phase.



Question 119

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rf = distance moved by solute / distance moved by solvent front. Each compound has a characteristic Rf at given conditions.



Question 120

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Simple distillation: separates a dissolved solid from a liquid (solvent evaporates and is collected). For two close-boiling miscible liquids, fractional distillation is needed.



Question 121

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  A separating funnel separates immiscible liquid layers (e.g. oil and water) by allowing each layer to drain separately.



Question 122

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sublimation: substances like iodine, naphthalene, and ammonium chloride sublime — used to separate them from non-subliming components.



Question 123

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  In the fractionating column, temperature decreases from bottom to top. Substances with lowest b.p. (most volatile) collect at the top.



Question 124

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Iron is magnetic; sulphur is not. A magnet attracts iron filings, separating them from sulphur powder.



Question 125

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Centrifugation uses centrifugal force to accelerate settling of fine particles/cells that are difficult to filter.



Question 126

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Chromatography separates a mixture of dyes in ink based on their different affinities for the mobile and stationary phases.



Question 127

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Solvent extraction: the desired compound dissolves in an appropriate solvent while impurities do not, then the solution is filtered.



Question 128

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Decantation: carefully pouring off the liquid (supernatant) from above a settled solid precipitate.



Question 129

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Crystallisation: a hot saturated solution is cooled; excess solute (above solubility at lower T) crystallises out pure.



Question 130

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Components with greatest affinity for mobile phase (and least for stationary phase) travel furthest and have highest Rf values.



Question 131

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fractional distillation separates miscible liquid mixtures (e.g. ethanol/water, crude oil fractions) using their different boiling points.



Question 132

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Crude oil (a complex mixture of hydrocarbons with different boiling points) is separated by fractional distillation in oil refineries.



Question 133

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Separating funnel requires the two liquids to be immiscible (don't dissolve in each other) and have different densities so they form separate layers.



Question 134

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rf is characteristic for a compound under specific conditions (solvent, temperature, stationary phase). It helps identify compounds.



Question 135

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  In evaporation, the solvent is evaporated to air (not collected). In distillation, the vapour is condensed and collected.



Question 136

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Desiccants (e.g. CaCl2, silica gel, P2O5) absorb water/moisture — used to dry gases or store moisture-sensitive materials.



Question 137

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Chlorination (adding Cl2 or NaOCl) kills bacteria and pathogens in drinking water. Filtration removes particles but not all bacteria.



Question 138

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Steam distillation allows heat-sensitive substances (like essential oils) to be distilled below 100°C by using steam to carry the vapour.



Question 139

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fractional crystallisation: one solid dissolves much more in hot solvent than cold; on cooling, it crystallises out leaving the more soluble one in solution.



Question 140

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fractional distillation of ethanol/water mixture gives ~95% ethanol azeotrope. The two boiling points are close enough to require fractionation.



Question 141

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Activated charcoal has enormous surface area (~1000 m²/g) that adsorbs dissolved organics, chlorine, colours, and tastes from water.



Question 142

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Recrystallisation: dissolve impure solid in hot solvent → filter hot (remove insoluble impurities) → cool to crystallise pure compound → filter and dry.



Question 143

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gas chromatography separates volatile compounds by passing a gas mixture through a heated column containing stationary phase — components elute at different times.



Question 144

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Salt is soluble in water; sand is not. Add water → filter (removes sand) → evaporate filtrate to recover salt.



Question 145

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Zone refining: a molten zone moves along a rod; impurities concentrate in the melt and are swept to one end, leaving pure crystal behind.



Question 146

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  TLC uses a thin layer of silica gel (or alumina) on a glass/aluminium plate as stationary phase. Mobile phase is the solvent.



Question 147

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Decanting simply pours the liquid from above a settled solid. Filtering passes liquid through filter paper to remove even suspended fine particles.



Question 148

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sea water is evaporated in large shallow pans using solar heat, leaving crystalline salt (NaCl + other minerals) behind.



Question 149

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Distillation: heat liquid to boil → vapour rises, passes through condenser → cools back to pure liquid. Purifies by exploiting different boiling points.



Question 150

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  In paper chromatography, the stationary phase is water adsorbed on cellulose fibres of the paper. The mobile phase is the organic solvent.



Question 151

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  High Rf: compound moves far with solvent front → strong affinity for mobile phase, weak for stationary phase.



Question 152

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Magnetic separation exploits the magnetic property of iron (and Fe-containing minerals) to separate them from non-magnetic materials.



Question 153

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Crude oil is fractionated in a continuous distillation column. Different fractions collect at different levels based on boiling point ranges.



Question 154

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  A pure substance has a sharp, precise melting/boiling point. Impurities lower and broaden the melting point range.



02

⚗️ CHEMICAL COMBINATION & THE MOLE

Laws · Avogadro · Stoichiometry · Empirical Formula · Titration



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

The MOLE is the chemist's 'dozen'. Just as a baker says '12 eggs = 1 dozen', a chemist says '6.022 × 10²³ particles = 1 mole'. It's just a counting unit — a very large one, because atoms are incredibly tiny!



2.1  Laws of Chemical Combination

LAW

MEANING IN SIMPLE ENGLISH

Conservation of Mass (Lavoisier, 1789)

Matter cannot be created or destroyed in a chemical reaction. If you start with 100 g of reactants, you always get 100 g of products. 'Nothing comes from nothing.'

Definite Proportions (Proust, 1797)

Every pure compound always has the same elements in the same fixed mass ratio, no matter where it comes from. Water (H₂O) is ALWAYS 11.1% H and 88.9% O by mass — whether from a Nigerian river or a European glacier.

Multiple Proportions (Dalton, 1803)

When two elements form MORE than one compound, the ratios of masses are in simple whole numbers. E.g., CO (C:O=3:4) and CO₂ (C:O=3:8). The oxygen ratio 4:8 = 1:2 (simple ratio).

Gay-Lussac's Combining Volumes

Gases combine in simple whole-number VOLUME ratios at the same T and P. 1 volume N₂ + 3 volumes H₂ → 2 volumes NH₃ (same ratios as the equation coefficients).

Avogadro's Hypothesis

Equal volumes of gases at the same T and P contain equal numbers of molecules. This is why volume ratios = mole ratios for gases.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Antoine Lavoisier, who discovered the Law of Conservation of Mass, was beheaded during the French Revolution in 1794 at age 50. The judge reportedly said 'The Republic has no need of scientists.' However, his scientific legacy lives on — every chemistry calculation you do today is based on his work!



2.2  The Mole — Understanding Avogadro's Number

ONE MOLE = 6.022 × 10²³ particles. This number is called Avogadro's constant (Nₐ). The particles can be atoms, molecules, ions, electrons — whatever the formula represents.

HOW BIG IS 6.022 × 10²³? If you had 6.022 × 10²³ grains of rice, they would cover the entire surface of the Earth to a depth of about 75 metres! That is how many particles are in just ONE mole of a substance.

MOLE CALCULATION TRIANGLE

Moles = Mass (g) ÷ Molar Mass (g/mol)

Mass = Moles × Molar Mass

Molar Mass = Mass ÷ Moles

Number of particles = Moles × 6.022 × 10²³

MOLAR MASS = the mass of one mole of a substance in g/mol. It equals the relative atomic/molecular mass numerically. MEMORISE THESE: H=1, C=12, N=14, O=16, Na=23, Mg=24, Al=27, S=32, Cl=35.5, K=39, Ca=40, Fe=56, Cu=63.5, Zn=65

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

How many moles are in 36 g of water (H₂O)? Molar mass of H₂O = 2(1) + 16 = 18 g/mol Moles = 36 ÷ 18 = 2 mol Number of molecules = 2 × 6.022×10²³ = 1.204×10²⁴ molecules

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

What is the mass of 0.5 mol of NaCl? Molar mass NaCl = 23 + 35.5 = 58.5 g/mol Mass = 0.5 × 58.5 = 29.25 g



2.3  Molar Volume of Gases

At the same conditions, one mole of ANY gas takes up the same volume (because the molecules are far apart). This is Avogadro's hypothesis in action.

At STP (0°C, 273 K, 1 atm):   1 mole of gas = 22.4 dm³

At RTP (25°C, 298 K, 1 atm):   1 mole of gas = 24.0 dm³

Moles = Volume (dm³) ÷ 22.4  [at STP]

Moles = Volume (dm³) ÷ 24.0  [at RTP]

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

What volume does 44 g of CO₂ occupy at STP? [C=12, O=16] Moles CO₂ = 44 ÷ 44 = 1 mol Volume = 1 × 22.4 = 22.4 dm³



2.4  Stoichiometry — Using Mole Ratios

The coefficients (big numbers) in a balanced equation tell you the MOLE RATIOS of substances reacting and produced. This is called STOICHIOMETRY.

GOLDEN RULE: (1) Write balanced equation → (2) Convert given to MOLES → (3) Use mole ratio → (4) Convert to what the question asks.

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

What mass of CaCO₃ reacts with excess HCl to produce 11.2 dm³ of CO₂ at STP? CaCO₃ + 2HCl → CaCl₂ + H₂O + CO₂ Moles CO₂ = 11.2 ÷ 22.4 = 0.5 mol Ratio CaCO₃:CO₂ = 1:1 → Moles CaCO₃ = 0.5 mol Mass CaCO₃ = 0.5 × 100 = 50 g



2.5  Limiting Reagent

Imagine making sandwiches. If you have 10 slices of bread but only 3 fillings, you can only make 3 sandwiches — the filling is the LIMITING REAGENT (it limits how many sandwiches you can make). The bread is in EXCESS.

TO FIND THE LIMITING REAGENT: Convert each reactant to moles → divide each by its coefficient in the equation → the smallest result = limiting reagent.

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃.   You have 28 g N₂ and 6 g H₂. Moles N₂ = 28÷28 = 1 mol. Moles H₂ = 6÷2 = 3 mol. Divide by coefficients: N₂: 1÷1=1.0 | H₂: 3÷3=1.0 Both equal — they are in the EXACT stoichiometric ratio! Moles NH₃ produced = 2×1 = 2 mol. Mass = 2×17 = 34 g.

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Same reaction but 28 g N₂ and 3 g H₂: Moles H₂ = 3÷2 = 1.5. H₂÷coefficient: 1.5÷3 = 0.5 N₂÷coefficient: 1÷1 = 1.0 Smallest = 0.5 → H₂ is LIMITING REAGENT Moles NH₃ = 2 × 0.5 = 1 mol. Mass = 17 g.



2.6  Empirical and Molecular Formulae

EMPIRICAL FORMULA = the SIMPLEST whole-number ratio of atoms in a compound. MOLECULAR FORMULA = the ACTUAL number of atoms per molecule. Think of empirical formula as 'how many of each letter per word' (e.g., 'aab') and molecular formula as the actual word ('aabaab').

STEP

WHAT TO DO

EXAMPLE (glucose)

Step 1

Get % of each element

C=40%, H=6.7%, O=53.3%

Step 2

Divide by atomic mass

C:40÷12=3.33 | H:6.7÷1=6.7 | O:53.3÷16=3.33

Step 3

Divide by SMALLEST

C:1 | H:2.01≈2 | O:1

Step 4

Write empirical formula

CH₂O

Step 5

Use Mr to find n

n = Mr÷empirical mass = 180÷30 = 6 → C₆H₁₂O₆

💡 EXAM TIP

ROUNDING RULE: After dividing by the smallest, if you get 1.5 → multiply ALL by 2. If you get 1.33 or 1.67 → multiply ALL by 3. NEVER round 1.5 to 1 or 2 — 1.5 × 2 = 3 (whole number). JAMB often tests this!

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Find empirical formula of compound with 80% C, 20% H. [C=12, H=1] C: 80÷12=6.67 | H: 20÷1=20 Divide by smallest (6.67): C: 1 | H: 3 Empirical formula = CH₃

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The molecular formula of aspirin (acetylsalicylic acid) is C₉H₈O₄, but its empirical formula is C₉H₈O₄ — they happen to be the same in this case! However, glucose (C₆H₁₂O₆) and formaldehyde (CH₂O) have the same EMPIRICAL formula (CH₂O) but completely different structures and properties.



2.7 Solution Concentration

Concentration (mol/dm³)  =  Moles  ÷  Volume (dm³)

1 dm³ = 1 litre = 1000 cm³       So: cm³ ÷ 1000 = dm³

Dilution formula: C₁V₁ = C₂V₂   (amount of solute unchanged)

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

What is the concentration of 4 g NaOH dissolved in 500 cm³ of solution? [Na=23, O=16, H=1] Molar mass NaOH = 23+16+1 = 40 g/mol Moles = 4 ÷ 40 = 0.1 mol Volume = 500 ÷ 1000 = 0.5 dm³ Concentration = 0.1 ÷ 0.5 = 0.2 mol/dm³



2.8 Titration Calculations

TITRATION is like a balancing act — you slowly add one solution to another until the reaction is exactly complete (the EQUIVALENCE POINT, shown by an INDICATOR colour change = END POINT). From the volumes used, you can calculate concentrations.

TITRATION FORMULA:   nₐ × Cₐ × Vₐ  =  n_b × C_b × V_b

n = mole ratio | C = concentration (mol/dm³) | V = volume (cm³ — same units both sides)

HCl + NaOH (1:1 ratio): C₁V₁ = C₂V₂

H₂SO₄ + 2NaOH (1:2 ratio): 1×Cₐ×Vₐ = 2×C_b×V_b

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

25.0 cm³ of NaOH solution required 20.0 cm³ of 0.100 M HCl to neutralise it. HCl + NaOH → NaCl + H₂O  (ratio 1:1) C₁V₁ = C₂V₂ → 0.100 × 20.0 = C(NaOH) × 25.0 C(NaOH) = 2.00 ÷ 25.0 = 0.0800 mol/dm³

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

What volume of 0.200 M H₂SO₄ neutralises 30.0 cm³ of 0.150 M NaOH? H₂SO₄ + 2NaOH → Na₂SO₄ + 2H₂O  (ratio 1:2) Moles NaOH = 0.150 × (30÷1000) = 0.0045 mol Moles H₂SO₄ needed = 0.0045 ÷ 2 = 0.00225 mol Volume = 0.00225 ÷ 0.200 = 0.01125 dm³ = 11.25 cm³

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Titration is used in the quality control of Nigerian food products. The NAFDAC laboratory uses acid-base titration to check the vitamin C content of orange juice, and redox titration to test iron content in food supplements. The same principles you learn in JAMB are used in industry every day!

💡 EXAM TIP:  TITRATION: C1V1 = C2V2 for a 1:1 acid-base reaction. For different ratios: n1C1V1 = n2C2V2 where n1 and n2 are the mole coefficients from the balanced equation.

🧠 MNEMONIC:  Moles = Mass / Molar Mass. Think: "Men Marry More Men" — always divide Mass by Molar Mass.

Q154.  (JAMB 1983)  

What is the volume at STP of 8g of oxygen (M=32)?

A.  22.4 dm³

B.  5.6 dm³

C.  11.2 dm³

D.  2.8 dm³







  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  GAY-LUSSAC'S LAW: at constant T and P, combining gas volumes are in simple whole-number ratios.



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NH4NO2→N2+2H2O. M=64. Moles=3.20/64=0.05. Moles N2=0.05. Volume=0.05×22.4=1.12 dm³.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Moles CaCO3=10/100=0.1. CaCO3+2HCl→CaCl2+H2O+CO2. Moles CO2=0.1. Molecules=0.1×6.02×10²³=6.02×10²².



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Avogadro's number (6.02×10²³) = number of atoms in EXACTLY 12 g of carbon-12. The definition of the mole.



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  C=40/12=3.33; H=6.7; O=53.3/16=3.33. Divide by 3.33: C:H:O=1:2:1. Empirical formula=CH2O.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  24 g Mg=1 mol. 32 g O2=32/32=1 mol. Both contain 6.02×10²³ particles.



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe2O3 in ore=56 kg. M(Fe2O3)=160. Moles=56000/160=350. Fe per mol=2×56=112 g. Mass Fe=350×112/1000=39.2 kg.



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Moles NaOH=0.1×0.5=0.05. Mass=0.05×40=2.0 g.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  2KClO3→2KCl+3O2. Ratio 2:3. Moles O2=2.5×(3/2)=3.75 mol.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Moles CH4=0.5→11.2 dm³ (1 mol=22.4). Moles C3H8=22/44=0.5. Volume=0.5×22.4=11.2 dm³.



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2SO4+2NaOH: moles NaOH=0.002. Moles H2SO4=0.001. V=0.001/0.5=0.002 dm³=2.0 cm³.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  O%=39.16. K=0.818; Cl=0.815; O=2.448. Divide by 0.815: K:Cl:O=1:1:3. KClO3.



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Moles=0.25×2=0.5. M(KOH)=56. Mass=0.5×56=28 g.



Question 14

This matches none perfectly. Best JAMB answer=C (N2O, VD=22) or recalculate. Correct: if VD=19, M=38, but this doesn't match standard NO series. JAMB 2020 answer=C based on pattern.

Question 42

In 0.1 mol = 0.1×6.02×10²³×50 = 3.01×10²⁴. JAMB typically asks differently — if asking for moles of molecules: 0.1×6.02×10²³=6.02×10²². Closest answer A.



Question 43

Question 132

Question 133

Question 134

Question 135

📝 EXPLANATION:  Stoichiometry uses balanced equations to calculate amounts (moles, masses, volumes) of reactants and products.



Question 136

✅ ANSWER: B

Question 137

Question 138

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The mole bridges the gap between the invisible atomic scale and the laboratory scale by connecting mass (measurable) to number of particles.



Question 139

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  3.01×10²³ atoms = 0.5 mol; mass = 0.5 × 12 = 6g.



Question 140

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The limiting reagent is fully consumed first. It limits the amount of product formed. Excess reagent remains when reaction stops.



Question 141

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  4g H2 = 2 mol; 32g O2 = 1 mol. Ratio needed = H2:O2 = 2:1. 2 mol H2 needs 1 mol O2; available: 1 mol O2 = just right for 2 mol H2. Actually neither is limiting here — they're in exact stoichiometric ratio.



Question 142

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Percentage yield = (actual yield obtained / theoretical maximum yield) × 100%. Less than 100% due to side reactions, incomplete reaction, or product loss.



Question 143

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ca(OH)2: 40 + 2(16+1) = 40 + 2(17) = 40 + 34 = 74 g/mol.



Question 144

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Avogadro's Law: equal volumes of different gases at same temperature and pressure contain the same number of molecules.



Question 145

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  n = 117/58.5 = 2 moles of NaCl.



Question 146

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Simplest ratio: C:H:O = 6:12:6 = 1:2:1 → CH2O.



Question 147

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  From pV = nRT at constant T and P: V ∝ n (moles). More moles = more volume (Avogadro's Law).



Question 148

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  12g C = 1 mol C; C + O2 → CO2; 1 mol CO2; at STP = 22.4 dm³.



Question 149

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  At STP: M (g/mol) = density (g/dm³) × 22.4 (dm³/mol). The molar volume links density to molar mass.



Question 150

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  0.5 mol × 6.02×10²³ formula units/mol = 3.01×10²³ formula units.



Question 151

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Mass of 1 molecule = molar mass / NA = 18 / (6.02×10²³) = 2.99×10⁻²³ g.



Question 152

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  0.5 mol A needs 2×0.5 = 1 mol B; available 2 mol B → excess B = 1 mol. B is in excess.



Question 153

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  The combined gas law (P1V1/T1 = P2V2/T2) applies to ideal gases. Real gases deviate at high P and low T.



Question 154

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  8g O2 / 32 g/mol = 0.25 mol; Volume = 0.25 × 22.4 = 5.6 dm³.

























03

💨 GAS LAWS

Boyle's · Charles's · Combined · Ideal Gas · Dalton's · Graham's



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Think of gas molecules as tiny footballs bouncing around inside a room at high speed. If you make the room smaller (reduce volume), the balls hit the walls more often — pressure goes up (Boyle's Law). If you heat the room (raise temperature), the balls move faster and hit harder — if walls are fixed, pressure goes up; if walls can expand (like a balloon), volume goes up (Charles's Law).



GAS LAWS — VISUAL SUMMARY

  BOYLE'S LAW (Constant T):          CHARLES'S LAW (Constant P):

  P ↑ → V ↓   (Inverse)             T ↑ → V ↑   (Direct)

  P ↓ → V ↑                         T ↓ → V ↓

  P₁V₁ = P₂V₂                       V₁/T₁ = V₂/T₂

  Graph: P vs V = hyperbola          Graph: V vs T(K) = straight line



  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │ COMBINED: P₁V₁/T₁ = P₂V₂/T₂   │  IDEAL: PV = nRT          │

  │ (when BOTH P and T change)      │  n=moles, R=8.314 J/mol·K │

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘



  ⚠️  ALWAYS convert temperature: T(K) = T(°C) + 273

  STP: 0°C (273 K), 1 atm → 1 mol gas = 22.4 dm³

  RTP: 25°C (298 K), 1 atm → 1 mol gas = 24.0 dm³



3.1  Kinetic Molecular Theory — Why Gases Behave the Way They Do

All gas behaviour can be explained by the KINETIC MOLECULAR THEORY (KMT):

Gas molecules are in CONTINUOUS, RANDOM, RAPID MOTION in all directions

The volume of the molecules themselves is NEGLIGIBLE compared to the container

There are NO INTERMOLECULAR FORCES between molecules (except during brief collisions)

Collisions are PERFECTLY ELASTIC — no kinetic energy is lost overall

Average kinetic energy ∝ Absolute Temperature: KE = ½mv² ∝ T(K)

At the same temperature, ALL gases have the SAME average KE — lighter molecules move FASTER

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The fastest gas molecule ever recorded was a hydrogen molecule moving at about 3,800 metres per second — faster than a speeding bullet! At room temperature, the average H₂ molecule moves at about 1,700 m/s. Heavier O₂ molecules average about 480 m/s — still faster than the speed of sound (340 m/s).



3.2  Boyle's Law — Pressure and Volume at Constant Temperature

BOYLE'S LAW (Temperature constant)

P₁V₁  =  P₂V₂

Pressure and Volume are INVERSELY proportional



SIMPLE RULE: If pressure doubles, volume halves. If pressure triples, volume becomes one-third. They always multiply to give the same number (at constant T).

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

A gas occupies 400 cm³ at pressure 3 atm. Find volume when pressure is reduced to 1.5 atm (temperature constant). P₁V₁ = P₂V₂ 3 × 400 = 1.5 × V₂ V₂ = 1200 ÷ 1.5 = 800 cm³ (Pressure halved → volume doubled ✓ — makes sense!)

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Your lungs use Boyle's Law! When your diaphragm contracts and ribs expand, the volume of your chest cavity INCREASES. By Boyle's Law, pressure inside DECREASES (below atmospheric), so air rushes INTO your lungs. Breathing in = increasing volume = decreasing pressure = air flows in. Breathing out = opposite.



3.3  Charles's Law — Volume and Temperature at Constant Pressure

CHARLES'S LAW (Pressure constant)

V₁/T₁  =  V₂/T₂

Volume and Temperature are DIRECTLY proportional — T MUST be in KELVIN!



CRITICAL RULE: ALWAYS convert Celsius to Kelvin before using. T(K) = T(°C) + 273

Common Kelvin values to memorise: 0°C=273K, 25°C=298K, 27°C=300K, 100°C=373K, 127°C=400K, 227°C=500K, 327°C=600K

⚠️ COMMON MISTAKE

This is the #1 JAMB calculation error — forgetting to convert to Kelvin! 0°C ≠ 0 K. Using 0°C in the formula would make V₂/0 = undefined (infinity). ALWAYS add 273.

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

A gas occupies 300 cm³ at 27°C. Find the volume at 127°C at constant pressure. T₁ = 27+273 = 300 K    T₂ = 127+273 = 400 K V₁/T₁ = V₂/T₂ → 300/300 = V₂/400 V₂ = 400 cm³ (Temperature increased by 1/3 → volume increased by 1/3 — makes sense!)



3.4  Combined Gas Law

COMBINED GAS LAW (Fixed amount of gas)

P₁V₁ / T₁   =   P₂V₂ / T₂

Use when BOTH pressure AND temperature change



📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

A gas: 500 cm³ at 27°C and 2 atm. Find volume at 127°C and 1 atm. T₁=300K, T₂=400K, P₁=2, P₂=1, V₁=500 V₂ = (P₁×V₁×T₂)÷(T₁×P₂) = (2×500×400)÷(300×1) = 400,000÷300 = 1333 cm³



3.5  Ideal Gas Law

IDEAL GAS LAW

PV = nRT

P=pressure | V=volume | n=moles | R=8.314 J/mol·K | T=Kelvin



Use when MOLES are involved or when you need ABSOLUTE values (not just comparing before/after).

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

What is the volume of 2 mol of ideal gas at 27°C and 1 atm pressure? PV = nRT → V = nRT/P = (2 × 8.314 × 300) / 101325 (if P in Pa) OR using R=0.0821 dm³·atm/mol·K: V = (2 × 0.0821 × 300)/1 = 49.26 dm³ Note: Also = 2 × 24.0 = 48.0 dm³ at RTP (close enough at 300K vs 298K)



3.6  Dalton's Law of Partial Pressures

P_total  =  P₁ + P₂ + P₃ + ...

Partial pressure of gas A = mole fraction of A × Total pressure

Mole fraction of A = moles of A ÷ total moles of all gases

GAS COLLECTED OVER WATER: If gas is collected by displacing water, the collected gas is saturated with water vapour. The DRY GAS pressure = Total pressure − Water vapour pressure (look up in tables).

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Container holds 2 mol N₂ and 3 mol O₂ at total pressure 5 atm. P(N₂) = (2/5) × 5 = 2 atm P(O₂) = (3/5) × 5 = 3 atm Check: 2 + 3 = 5 atm ✓



3.7  Graham's Law of Diffusion

GRAHAM'S LAW

r₁ / r₂  =  √(M₂ / M₁)

Lighter gas diffuses FASTER. For same volume: t₁/t₂ = √(M₁/M₂)



WHY? At the same temperature, all gases have the same average kinetic energy (½mv²). So if molecule A is lighter (smaller m), it must move FASTER (larger v) to have the same KE. Like comparing a bicycle (light) and a lorry (heavy) — if both have the same kinetic energy, the bicycle must be moving much faster!

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

How many times faster does H₂ (M=2) diffuse than O₂ (M=32)? r(H₂)/r(O₂) = √(M(O₂)/M(H₂)) = √(32/2) = √16 = 4 H₂ diffuses FOUR TIMES FASTER than O₂

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

NH₃/HCl tube experiment: NH₃ (M=17) released from left end, HCl (M=36.5) from right end simultaneously. Where does white NH₄Cl smoke form? r(NH₃)/r(HCl) = √(36.5/17) = √2.15 = 1.47 NH₃ moves 1.47× faster → ring forms CLOSER TO THE HCl END (NH₃ has travelled farther)

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Graham's Law is used in the separation of uranium isotopes for nuclear power. Natural uranium is mostly ²³⁸U but nuclear reactors need ²³⁵U. UF₆ gas is passed through thousands of tiny holes. The lighter ²³⁵UF₆ molecules diffuse very slightly faster than ²³⁸UF₆. After thousands of repetitions (called stages), the uranium is enriched in ²³⁵U. Nigeria has uranium deposits in Plateau and Cross River states.



3.8  Gay-Lussac's Law of Combining Volumes

Gas volumes combine in the SAME ratio as the COEFFICIENTS in the balanced equation (at constant T and P).

REACTION

VOLUME RATIOS

H₂ + Cl₂ → 2HCl

1 vol + 1 vol → 2 vol

N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃

1 vol + 3 vol → 2 vol

2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O (steam)

2 vol + 1 vol → 2 vol steam

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

20 cm³ H₂ reacted with 20 cm³ O₂, cooled to 25°C. Residual volume? 2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O Ratio H₂:O₂ = 2:1. For 20 cm³ H₂, need 10 cm³ O₂. All H₂ used. O₂ left over = 20−10 = 10 cm³. Water condensed (liquid at 25°C, negligible volume). Residual gas = 10 cm³ O₂

💡 EXAM TIP:  ALWAYS convert temperature to Kelvin: T(K) = T(°C) + 273. Never use Celsius in gas law equations!

🧠 MNEMONIC:  BCC: Boyle (constant T), Charles (constant P), Combined (both change). Then PV=nRT for ideal gas.
