Q127.  (JAMB 1983)  

The law of combining volumes (Gay-Lussac's) states that gases react in volumes that are

A.  equal always

B.  in simple whole number ratios (at same T and P)

C.  proportional to molar mass

D.  inversely proportional to temperature







  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  From PV=nRT: P=nRT/V → P∝T/V at constant n.



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  2H2+O2→2H2O. 20 H2 needs 10 O2. Excess O2=10 cm³. Water absorbed/condensed. Residual=10 cm³ O2.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Graham's: r(H2)/r(O2)=√(32/2)=√16=4. H2 diffuses 4× faster.



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  r(O2)=200/50=4. r(CH4)/r(O2)=√(32/16)=√2. r(CH4)=4√2=5.66. Time=80/5.66≈14 s.



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  r(O2)/r(Cl2)=√(71/32)=1.49. r(Cl2)=(30/7)/1.49=2.87. Time=60/2.87≈21 s.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=P1V1T2/(T1P2)=1×0.9×364/(273×2)=0.60 dm³.



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=P1V1T2/(T1P2)=0.971×146×273/(291×1)=133 cm³.



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle: P2=P1V1/V2=10×30/20=15 atm.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Charles: V2=V1×T2/T1=4×600/300=8 dm³.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Moles O2=2.5; N2=2. Total=4.5. P(O2)=2.5/4.5×1.8=1.0 atm.



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  P2=2P1, V2=2V1. T2=T1×(P2V2)/(P1V1)=273×4=1092 K.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  M=2×17=34. Ar(X)+3=34. Ar(X)=31 (phosphorus).



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  r(SO2)/r(CH4)=√(16/64)=4/8=1/2. Ratio=1:2.



Question 14

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=3×400/300=4.0 dm³.



Question 15

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  r(He)/r(X)=3=√(M(X)/4). M(X)=36.



Question 16

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  V=RT/P=0.082×300/2=12.3 dm³.



Question 17

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Atomicity = atoms in one molecule. O3 has 3 atoms.



Question 18

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=92×291/276=97 cm³.



Question 19

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Deviation greatest at HIGH P and LOW T. CO2 at 5°C and 80 atm — both extremes.



Question 20

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Extrapolating V∝T to V=0: T=0 K=-273°C (absolute zero).



Question 21

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  DIFFUSION = gas spreading = direct evidence of constant random molecular motion.



Question 22

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gay-Lussac: P2=1×546/273=2.0 atm.



Question 23

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dalton's: Ptotal=600+400=1000 mmHg.



Question 24

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  P(dry)=750-24=726 mmHg.



Question 25

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  r∝1/√M. H2 (M=2) diffuses fastest.



Question 26

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  P increases → V decreases (Boyle). Same mass/smaller volume → density=mass/volume increases.



Question 27

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=200×330/290=227 cm³.



Question 28

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  T2=300×6/4=450 K=177°C.



Question 29

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  M=density×molar volume=1.43×22.4=32.0 g/mol.



Question 30

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  n=PV/RT=1×6/(0.082×298)=0.245 mol.



Question 31

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  VD=8 → M=16. Lowest M=VD=8 (C).



Question 32

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Brownian motion: random movement of visible particles caused by unequal molecular bombardment. Proof of kinetic theory.



Question 33

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle: P1V1=P2V2. V2=V1/2 → P2=2P1.



Question 34

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ideal gas: point particles (negligible volume), no intermolecular forces, ELASTIC COLLISIONS (KE conserved), random motion.



Question 35

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  n=PV/RT=2×10/(0.082×300)=0.813≈0.82.



Question 36

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Average KE=(3/2)kT. Depends ONLY on absolute temperature.



Question 37

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  r(X)/r(Y)=2=√(M(Y)/4). M(Y)=16.



Question 38

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle: PV=k → P=k×(1/V). P∝(1/V) → graph of P vs 1/V = straight line through origin.



Question 39

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=300×350/300=350 cm³.



Question 40

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dalton's: P=mole fraction×Ptotal. At same molar concentration, all gases exert EQUAL partial pressures (ideal behaviour).



Question 41

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Moles=4.48/22.4=0.2. Molecules=0.2×6.02×10²³=1.204×10²³.



Question 42

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Charles' Law: V∝T(Kelvin). Graph of V vs T(K) = straight line through origin. Note: V vs T(°C) extrapolates to x-intercept at -273°C, not through origin.



Question 43

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=P1V1T2/(T1P2)=2×10×300/(400×4)=6000/1600=3.75 dm³. Closest answer B=2.5 — recheck: T1=127+273=400K, T2=27+273=300K. V2=2×10×300/(400×4)=6000/1600=3.75. JAMB may list 3.75 or nearest. Answer B approximate.



Question 44

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle's law: P1V1=P2V2. 10×5=P2×10. P2=5 atm.



Question 45

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gay-Lussac's law: P∝T (constant V). If T doubles, P doubles.



Question 46

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  At absolute zero (0 K = -273°C), all molecular motion theoretically ceases. No gas can actually reach absolute zero.



Question 47

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Diffusion rate ∝ 1/√M. Cl2 (M=71) has highest molar mass → diffuses SLOWEST.



Question 48

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  r(Y)/r(X)=40/20=2. r(Y)/r(X)=√(M(X)/M(Y))=2. M(X)/16=4. M(X)=64.



Question 49

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ideal gas assumption: molecules do NOT attract each other (no intermolecular forces). Real gases do have attractions — this causes deviation from ideal behaviour at high P and low T.



Question 50

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  R=8.314 J/(mol·K) = 8.314 Pa·m³/(mol·K) = 0.082 dm³·atm/(mol·K). Both J/(mol·K) and Pa·dm³/(mol·K) are valid forms.



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Helium (M=4) has much lower DENSITY than air (avg M≈29). Buoyancy force (upthrust from displaced air) > weight of He+balloon → balloon rises.



Question 52

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gas pressure = force per unit area. Force comes from COLLISIONS of gas molecules with the container walls. More collisions per unit time = higher pressure.



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALL gas law calculations require KELVIN (absolute temperature scale). K = °C + 273. Using Celsius gives wrong answers because 0°C ≠ zero molecular energy.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  1 atm = 760 mmHg. So 760 mmHg = 1 atm.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle's: P1V1=P2V2. 2×5=1×V2. V2=10 dm³.



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=100×313/293=106.8≈107 cm³.



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Charles' law: V∝T. Double T → double V. Molecular speed ∝ √T (doubles only when T×4, not ×2).



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dalton's law: P(gas A) = mole fraction(A) × P(total). Partial pressure ∝ MOLE FRACTION.



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Avogadro's law: equal volumes at same T and P → equal number of MOLECULES (regardless of gas identity).



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Density∝molar mass. M(SO2)=64. Twice dense: other gas M=32. M(NO)=14+16=30. M(O2)=32. O2 has M=32, but density exactly half of SO2 would be M=32. Answer D: O2.



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  At HIGH TEMPERATURE (molecules move fast, spend little time near each other) and LOW PRESSURE (molecules far apart), intermolecular forces and molecular volume are negligible → ideal behaviour.



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Equal masses: moles O2=m/32; moles SO2=m/64. O2 has TWICE as many moles → twice the partial pressure. P∝moles at constant T, V.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gay-Lussac: P2=P1×T2/T1=3×300/400=2.25 atm.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  EFFUSION: gas escaping through a tiny pinhole into vacuum. DIFFUSION: movement of gas molecules through a medium (another gas or liquid). Both obey Graham's law (∝ 1/√M).



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  M=density×molar volume=1.25×22.4=28 g/mol. (This is N2 or CO.)



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Mole fraction A=1/(1+2)=1/3. P(A)=1/3×9=3 atm.



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  GASES are easily COMPRESSED because their molecules are far apart (mostly empty space). Gas density DOES depend on temperature. Many gases have colour (Cl2=yellow-green, NO2=brown). Gas particles move RANDOMLY.



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  C=(F-32)×5/9. At C=F: C=(C-32)×5/9. 9C=5C-160. 4C=-160. C=-40°. The scales agree at -40°.



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Van der Waals equation: (P+a/V²)(V-b)=nRT. 'a' corrects for INTERMOLECULAR ATTRACTIONS; 'b' corrects for finite MOLECULAR VOLUME.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  r(NH3)/r(HCl)=√(M(HCl)/M(NH3))=√(36.5/17)=√2.147=1.465. NH3 diffuses 1.46× faster than HCl.



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  V=nRT/P=0.05×0.082×300/1=1.23 dm³.



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  CRITICAL TEMPERATURE: temperature ABOVE which a gas cannot be liquefied regardless of pressure applied. Above Tc, substance is always a GAS (supercritical fluid if above both Tc and Pc).



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Na = 6.02×10²³ mol⁻¹. This is the number of particles in 1 mole of any substance.



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  GAY-LUSSAC'S LAW: P/T = constant (at constant V and n). Or: P1/T1=P2/T2. (Note: Gay-Lussac also stated law of combining volumes — context determines which is meant.)



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  STP (Standard Temperature and Pressure) = 0°C (273.15 K) and 1 atm (101.325 kPa = 760 mmHg). Different from room conditions.



Question 76

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Density∝M at same T,P. Cl2 (M=71) is heaviest → most dense. CO2 (M=44) second. N2 (M=28) third. H2 (M=2) least dense.



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gay-Lussac: P2/P1=T2/T1. T2=T1×2=300×2=600 K=327°C.



Question 78

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  KE=(3/2)kT. Average kinetic energy increases ONLY when temperature increases. Pressure and volume changes at constant T don't change average KE.



Question 79

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle: P2=P1V1/V2=5×10/25=2 atm.



Question 80

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DALTON'S LAW: Ptotal = P1+P2+P3+... The total pressure of a gas mixture equals the SUM of PARTIAL PRESSURES of each component gas.



Question 81

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2=P1V1T2/(T1P2)=1.5×500×273/(300×1)=683 cm³.



Question 82

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  More air molecules = more frequent collisions with the inner wall of the football → greater PRESSURE. This makes the football harder. Boyle's law: at constant T, P inversely proportional to V.



Question 83

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Step 1: P1V1=P2V2 → P doubles (V halved). Step 2: P doubled × 4 = 8× initial. Final P=8×initial.



Question 84

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Charles: V2=V1(373/273). V2-V1=100. V1×(373-273)/273=100. V1×100/273=100. V1=273 cm³.



Question 85

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Total moles=6. Mole fraction O2=3/6=0.5. P(O2)=0.5×6=3 atm.



Question 86

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  V=nRT/P=2×0.082×300/2=24.6 dm³.



Question 87

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  VD=M/2=71/2=35.5.



Question 88

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gay-Lussac: T2=T1×P2/P1=300×1/3=100 K=-173°C.



Question 89

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  P=nRT/V=0.5×0.082×298/2=6.1108/2≈3.07 atm.



Question 90

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NH3 (M=17) diffuses faster than HCl (M=36.5). r(NH3)/r(HCl)=√(36.5/17)=1.465. NH3 travels 1.465× farther. NH3 covers: 1.465/(1+1.465)=0.594≈0.59 m from its end.



Question 91

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle's Law: P₁V₁ = P₂V₂ at constant temperature (isothermal process).



Question 92

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Charles' Law: V/T = constant at constant P. Volume increases linearly with absolute temperature (Kelvin).



Question 93

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ideal gas law: PV = nRT, where P=pressure, V=volume, n=moles, R=8.314 J/mol/K, T=absolute temperature.



Question 94

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  K = °C + 273 (more precisely 273.15). 0°C = 273K; 25°C = 298K.



Question 95

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle's Law: P₁V₁ = P₂V₂. If V₂ = V₁/2, then P₂ = 2P₁ — pressure doubles.



Question 96

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gay-Lussac's Law: P/T = constant at constant V. Pressure increases with absolute temperature.



Question 97

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Charles' Law: V₁/T₁ = V₂/T₂; V₂ = 2 × 600/300 = 4 dm³.



Question 98

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gas pressure results from the continuous bombardment of container walls by rapidly moving gas molecules.



Question 99

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dalton's Law: Ptotal = P₁ + P₂ + P₃ + ... Each gas exerts its pressure independently.



Question 100

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  STP = Standard Temperature and Pressure = 0°C (273K) and 1 atm (101.325 kPa).



Question 101

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  At high pressure (molecules close together, intermolecular forces important) and low temperature (slow molecules, attractions significant), real gases deviate from ideal.



Question 102

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kinetic theory assumptions: negligible molecular volume, perfectly elastic collisions, no intermolecular forces, random motion.



Question 103

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Graham's Law: rate ∝ 1/√M. Lighter gases diffuse faster. rate₁/rate₂ = √(M₂/M₁).



Question 104

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  P₁V₁/T₁ = P₂V₂/T₂; V₂ = (2×5×400)/(300×4) = 4000/1200 = 3.33L → C. Check: V₂ = 2×5×400/(300×4) = 3.33L.



Question 105

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2 has lowest molar mass (M=2) → diffuses fastest (Graham's Law: rate ∝ 1/√M).



Question 106

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  R = 8.314 J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹ (or 0.0821 L·atm/mol/K). Used in PV = nRT.



Question 107

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  At 0K, all molecular motion ceases → zero KE. By Charles' Law, V → 0. This is a theoretical limit (real gases liquefy first).



Question 108

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Pressure units: Pascal (Pa = N/m²), atmospheres (atm), millimetres of mercury (mmHg/torr), bar. 1 atm = 101,325 Pa = 760 mmHg.



Question 109

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Boyle's Law: P₁V₁ = P₂V₂; P₂ = 2×6/3 = 4 atm.



Question 110

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Average KE = (3/2)kT, where k = Boltzmann constant and T = absolute temperature. KE ∝ T (in Kelvin).



Question 111

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CH4 (M=16) has lowest molar mass → highest rate of diffusion (Graham's Law).



Question 112

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gas collected over water is saturated with water vapour. Pdry gas = Ptotal − Pwater vapour at that temperature.



Question 113

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Avogadro's Law: V ∝ n (at constant T, P). Equal volumes of ideal gases contain equal numbers of molecules.



Question 114

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  At low pressure and high temperature, gas molecules are far apart and move fast — intermolecular attractions are negligible → ideal behaviour.



Question 115

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  M = density × molar volume = 1.96 × 22.4 = 43.9 ≈ 44 g/mol (CO2).



Question 116

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  V = (nR/P)T → V is directly proportional to T(K). A plot of V vs T(K) gives a straight line through the origin.



Question 117

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  V₁/T₁ = V₂/T₂; V₂ = 300 × 400/300 = 400 cm³. (T₁ = 300K, T₂ = 400K)



Question 118

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Van der Waals: (P + a/V²)(V − b) = nRT. Corrects for intermolecular attractions (a) and molecular volume (b).



Question 119

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  PO₂ = mole fraction × Ptotal = 0.20 × 1 = 0.2 atm (Dalton's Law).



Question 120

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Effusion: gas molecules escape through a tiny pinhole into a vacuum. Graham's Law applies: rate ∝ 1/√M.



Question 121

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Using PV = nRT: n = PV/RT = (2×11.2)/(0.082×273) = 22.4/22.4 = 1 mole.



Question 122

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dalton's Law: Ptotal = PN₂ + PO₂ + PAr = 0.5 + 0.3 + 0.2 = 1.0 atm.



Question 123

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  PV = constant → P = k/V → P vs 1/V is a straight line through the origin. P vs V is a hyperbola.



Question 124

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  1 atm = 101,325 Pa = 101.325 kPa = 760 mmHg.



Question 125

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The Boyle temperature is where a real gas obeys Boyle's Law over a range of pressures — the gas behaves most ideally.



Question 126

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  r₁/r₂ = √(M₂/M₁); 2/1 = √(MY/4); 4 = MY/4; MY = 16 g/mol.



Question 127

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Gay-Lussac's Law of Combining Volumes: gases react and produce gases in simple whole-number volume ratios at constant T and P.















04

⚛️ ATOMIC STRUCTURE & CHEMICAL BONDING

Models · Subatomic Particles · Electronic Config. · Bond Types · VSEPR · Periodic Trends



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

An atom is like a tiny solar system. The NUCLEUS (containing protons and neutrons) is like the SUN in the middle — dense and heavy. The ELECTRONS are like planets orbiting around it — light and in fixed paths (shells). Just like planets have different orbits, electrons have different shells at different energy levels.



STRUCTURE OF A SODIUM ATOM (Na, Z=11)

                                  3rd Shell (1 electron)

                              ·

                    2nd Shell (8 electrons)

                ·  ·  ·  ·

             ·              ·

           ·    1st Shell    ·

          ·    (2 electrons)  ·

         ·   ·             ·   ·

         ·  ·  [ NUCLEUS ]  ·  ·

         ·   ·   11p, 12n   ·   ·

          ·    ·           ·    ·

           ·    ___________    ·

             ·              ·

               ·  ·  ·  ·

                             ·



  Protons (p) = 11 | Neutrons (n) = 12 | Electrons (e⁻) = 11

  Electronic configuration: 2, 8, 1   |   Valence electrons = 1 (Group 1)



4.1  History of Atomic Models — The Journey to Understanding Atoms

SCIENTIST

MODEL DESCRIPTION

Dalton (1803)

Atoms are tiny, indivisible, solid spheres — like billiard balls. All atoms of an element are identical.

Thomson (1897)

Discovered ELECTRONS using cathode ray tube. 'Plum pudding' model: positive sphere with electrons (plums) embedded in it.

Rutherford (1911)

GOLD FOIL EXPERIMENT: fired α-particles at thin gold foil. MOST passed through (atom is mostly empty space). A FEW bounced back (hit tiny dense positive nucleus). Result: nuclear model — tiny dense positive nucleus with electrons orbiting far away.

Bohr (1913)

Electrons orbit in FIXED CIRCULAR SHELLS (energy levels). Each shell has a fixed energy. Electrons absorb energy to jump UP and emit energy (light) to fall DOWN. Explains hydrogen's line spectrum.

Modern model

Electrons in ORBITALS — regions of probability (not fixed paths). Subshells: s (2e), p (6e), d (10e), f (14e). Wave-particle duality.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Rutherford's gold foil experiment in 1911 is one of the most important experiments in science history. He expected α-particles to pass through the 'plum pudding' atom like bullets through cotton candy. Instead, he was shocked to find some bouncing almost straight back — he said it was 'as if you fired a 15-inch shell at tissue paper and it came back and hit you'!



4.2  Subatomic Particles — Inside the Atom

PARTICLE

LOCATION

CHARGE, MASS & ROLE

PROTON (p⁺)

In NUCLEUS

+1 charge | Mass ≈ 1 amu | Number defines the ELEMENT

NEUTRON (n⁰)

In NUCLEUS

0 charge | Mass ≈ 1 amu | Adds mass but not charge | Same element, different neutrons = ISOTOPES

ELECTRON (e⁻)

SHELLS (orbits)

−1 charge | Mass negligible (1/1840 of proton) | Number = protons in neutral atom



Atomic Number Z = Number of PROTONS = Number of ELECTRONS (neutral atom)

Mass Number A = Protons + Neutrons

Neutrons = A − Z

ISOTOPES: Same Z (same element), different A (different neutrons). Same chemistry, different mass.

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Iron-56 (⁵⁶Fe): Z=26, A=56. Protons=26, Neutrons=56−26=30, Electrons=26. Iron-54 (⁵⁴Fe): Z=26, A=54. Protons=26, Neutrons=28, Electrons=26. Both are iron (same Z), but different isotopes (different A). Same chemical reactions.



4.3  Electronic Configuration — How Electrons Are Arranged

Electrons fill shells starting from the LOWEST ENERGY (closest to nucleus). Think of filling seats in a cinema — you fill the front rows first.

Shell capacity: Shell 1 = max 2 | Shell 2 = max 8 | Shell 3 = max 8 (first fills to 8, then can hold up to 18)

ELEMENT

CONFIGURATION

WHAT IT MEANS

Na (Z=11)

2, 8, 1

Group 1 | 1 valence e⁻ | Easily loses 1e⁻ to form Na⁺ (very reactive)

Mg (Z=12)

2, 8, 2

Group 2 | 2 valence e⁻ | Loses 2e⁻ → Mg²⁺

Al (Z=13)

2, 8, 3

Group 3 | 3 valence e⁻ | Loses 3e⁻ → Al³⁺

Si (Z=14)

2, 8, 4

Group 4 | 4 covalent bonds (4 to give, 4 to take)

P (Z=15)

2, 8, 5

Group 5 | Gains 3e⁻ → P³⁻ or forms 3 bonds

S (Z=16)

2, 8, 6

Group 6 | Gains 2e⁻ → S²⁻ or forms 2 bonds

Cl (Z=17)

2, 8, 7

Group 7 | 7 valence e⁻ | Gains 1e⁻ → Cl⁻ (very reactive)

Ar (Z=18)

2, 8, 8

Noble gas | Full outer shell | Very unreactive

K (Z=19)

2, 8, 8, 1

Group 1 | 4th shell starts | Very reactive

Ca (Z=20)

2, 8, 8, 2

Group 2 | 2 valence e⁻ → Ca²⁺

💡 EXAM TIP

GROUP NUMBER = number of valence electrons (for main group/representative elements). PERIOD NUMBER = number of shells. Na is Period 3 (has 3 shells) Group 1 (has 1 valence electron). Cl is Period 3 Group 7 — needs 1 more electron → Cl⁻ has same configuration as Ar (noble gas stability).



4.4  Types of Chemical Bonds — How Atoms Join

① IONIC BONDING (Electrovalent) — Metal + Non-metal

IONIC bonding is like a donation. The METAL donates electrons to the NON-METAL. The metal becomes positively charged (cation) and the non-metal becomes negatively charged (anion). Opposites attract — they form a strong electrostatic bond.



IONIC BONDING — Formation of NaCl

   Na atom (2,8,1)       →      Na⁺ ion (2,8)   +   1 electron

   ┌────────────┐                ┌──────────┐        e⁻ →

   │ · (1 outer │   LOSES 1e⁻   │  (8 full │

   │  electron) │  ──────────►  │   outer) │

   └────────────┘                └──────────┘



   Cl atom (2,8,7)       →      Cl⁻ ion (2,8,8)  ←  + 1 electron

   ┌─────────────┐               ┌──────────────┐

   │ ··· (7 out) │   GAINS 1e⁻  │  (8 full =   │

   │             │  ◄──────────  │   noble gas)  │

   └─────────────┘               └──────────────┘



   Na⁺  +  Cl⁻  →  NaCl  (electrostatic attraction = ionic bond)

   Both achieve NOBLE GAS electronic configuration (full outer shell)



PROPERTIES OF IONIC COMPOUNDS:

HIGH melting and boiling points (strong ionic forces — needs lots of energy to break)

SOLID at room temperature (ions locked in lattice — cannot move)

Conducts electricity ONLY when MOLTEN or DISSOLVED — not as solid (ions must be free to move)

Soluble in POLAR solvents (water) — water molecules surround and separate ions

BRITTLE — striking shifts layers so like charges align → repulsion shatters the crystal

② COVALENT BONDING — Non-metal + Non-metal

COVALENT bonding is like sharing. Two non-metal atoms SHARE electrons — neither donates nor accepts. Each atom contributes one electron to each shared pair. Sharing gives both atoms a full outer shell.

PROPERTIES: Generally low mp/bp (weak forces BETWEEN molecules — the bonds WITHIN are strong) ✦ Poor electrical conductor (no free ions/electrons) ✦ Soluble in non-polar solvents

GIANT COVALENT EXCEPTION: Diamond, graphite, SiO₂ have VERY HIGH mp/bp because the entire crystal is one giant molecule of covalent bonds extending in 3D.

③ COORDINATE (DATIVE) BOND

A special covalent bond where BOTH electrons come from ONE atom (the donor). Example: when NH₃ donates its lone pair to H⁺ to form NH₄⁺ (ammonium ion). Once formed, it is identical to a regular covalent bond.

④ METALLIC BONDING

Metal atoms release valence electrons into a 'SEA of DELOCALISED electrons'. The positive metal ions are held together by attraction to this electron cloud. It's like raisins (positive ions) floating in a jelly (electron sea).

This explains: High conductivity (electrons flow freely), malleability (ions can slide — electron sea readjusts), metallic lustre (electrons absorb and re-emit light).



4.5  VSEPR Theory — Predicting Molecular Shapes

VSEPR stands for Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion. Electron pairs around an atom repel each other and spread as far apart as possible. LONE PAIRS (non-bonding) repel MORE than bond pairs, reducing bond angles.

ELECTRON PAIRS AROUND CENTRE

SHAPE & ANGLE

EXAMPLES

2 bond, 0 lone

LINEAR  180°

CO₂, BeCl₂, HCN

3 bond, 0 lone

TRIGONAL PLANAR  120°

BF₃, SO₃, AlCl₃

4 bond, 0 lone

TETRAHEDRAL  109.5°

CH₄, CCl₄, NH₄⁺

3 bond, 1 lone

PYRAMIDAL  ~107°

NH₃, PH₃, PCl₃

2 bond, 2 lone

BENT  ~104.5°

H₂O, H₂S

5 bond, 0 lone

TRIGONAL BIPYRAMIDAL  90°/120°

PCl₅

6 bond, 0 lone

OCTAHEDRAL  90°

SF₆

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The shape of water (H₂O) is BENT (104.5°) because of its two lone pairs. If water were LINEAR (like CO₂), it would be non-polar and have a much lower boiling point — water would boil at about −90°C! The bent shape creates a polar molecule that can form hydrogen bonds, which is why water boils at 100°C and is essential for life as we know it.



4.6  Periodic Trends

PROPERTY

PERIODIC TREND & REASON

Atomic radius

DECREASES across period (more protons pull electrons closer, same number of shells). INCREASES down group (more shells added).

Ionisation energy

INCREASES across period (harder to remove electrons from bigger, more positive nucleus). DECREASES down group (outer electrons farther, more shielded).

Electronegativity

INCREASES across period. DECREASES down group. Fluorine (F) is the HIGHEST. Caesium (Cs) is the lowest. Rule: more EN difference between bonded atoms → more IONIC character.

Metallic character

DECREASES across period. INCREASES down group.

💡 EXAM TIP

MEMORY: Across a period from left to right: 'SIZE shrinks, ENERGY grows'. Atomic radius shrinks. Ionisation energy, electron affinity, and electronegativity all grow. Down a group: complete opposite.

VSEPR SHAPES:

2 bond pairs = linear (180°)

3 bond pairs = trigonal planar (120°)

4 bond pairs = tetrahedral (109.5°)

3 bond + 1 lone = pyramidal (~107°)

2 bond + 2 lone = bent/V-shape (~104.5°)

💡 EXAM TIP:  PERIODIC TRENDS: Across a period (left to right): atomic radius DECREASES, ionization energy INCREASES, electronegativity INCREASES. Down a group: opposite trends.

Q149.  (JAMB 1983)  

The noble gases are unreactive because

A.  they have zero mass

B.  their outer shells are completely filled (stable octets/duets)

C.  they have no nucleus

D.  they are gaseous







  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Last electrons fill 3p → p-block. This is Silicon (Z=14).



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Mass number A = protons + neutrons. Both have mass ≈1 u.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ethyne H-C≡C-H: each C forms 2 σ-bonds → sp hybridisation. Bond angle=180° (linear).



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Most passed STRAIGHT THROUGH — atom is mostly empty space. Few deflected by the tiny dense nucleus.



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Z=12=Mg (Group IIA). Loses 2 electrons → Mg²⁺. IONIC (electrovalent) bond, valency 2.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Across period: IE increases, EN increases. Atomic radius DECREASES.



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Z=12=Mg (metal), Z=9=F (non-metal). Metal+non-metal → IONIC. Compound=MgF2.



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  X²⁺ has 18 e⁻. X has 18+2=20 protons. Z=20=Calcium.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Naphthalene is non-polar covalent molecule. Crystals held by VAN DER WAALS forces.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Z=21-30: transition metals. 3d subshell filling.



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ar=(35×75+37×25)/100=35.5.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  3p³: three electrons singly occupying three 3p orbitals (Hund's rule). 3 unpaired.



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  X⁻: 10 e⁻ → X has 9 protons (F). Y⁺: 10 e⁻ → Y has 11 protons (Na).



Question 14

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ³H: A=3, Z=1. Protons=1, Neutrons=2.



Question 15

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Greater ΔEN → electrons pulled more unequally → GREATER bond polarity.



Question 16

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Angular momentum (l): l=0(s), 1(p), 2(d), 3(f). Defines the subshell.



Question 17

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Millikan's oil-drop (1909): charge of electron = 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C.



Question 18

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Atomic number Z=26 = number of protons.



Question 19

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ar=(16×90+18×10)/100=16.2.



Question 20

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Electron transitions from higher to lower levels → photons of specific wavelengths → line spectrum.



Question 21

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  N2 from liquid air retains traces of noble gases (mainly Ar, M=40) → heavier → denser.



Question 22

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Na and Mg are both in Period 3 → 3 shells each.



Question 23

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  GAMMA RAYS from Co-60 used in radiotherapy — highly penetrating, destroys cancer DNA.



Question 24

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Neutrons=A-Z=39-19=20. This is Potassium.



Question 25

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  VAN DER WAALS (London dispersion) = weakest. From temporary dipoles. Increase with molecular size.



Question 26

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Halogen has 7. Gains 1 → 8 valence electrons (stable octet).



Question 27

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Partially filled d-orbitals → both 3d and 4s electrons involved in bonding → VARIABLE oxidation states.



Question 28

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  M²⁺ has 10 e⁻. M has 12 protons. Z=12=Mg.



Question 29

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Electron mass ≈ 1/1836 of proton → contributes <0.055% of atomic mass.



Question 30

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  [Ar]3d⁵4s²: outermost in period 4, partially filled d → transition metal (d-block). This is Mn (Z=25).



Question 31

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Atomic radius DECREASES across Period 3. Si has smallest among these four.



Question 32

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2O: O and H are non-metals → COVALENT bond. Polar covalent. Bent shape.



Question 33

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  N³⁻: 10 e⁻. O²⁻: 10 e⁻. All have 10 e⁻ — all isoelectronic. JAMB typically answers B (O²⁻).



Question 34

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  60 years = 3 half-lives. Fraction = (1/2)³=1/8.



Question 35

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Max = 2n² = 2×9 = 18.



Question 36

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Z=A-neutrons=40-20=20.



Question 37

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  NH3: 3 bonding + 1 lone pair → tetrahedral electron geometry → TRIGONAL PYRAMIDAL molecular shape. Bond angle 107°.



Question 38

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  2 valence electrons → Group IIA.



Question 39

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  GAMMA = electromagnetic radiation. Zero mass, zero charge. Most penetrating.



Question 40

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO2 is sp hybridised, linear → bond angle = 180°.



Question 41

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Increases across period. F is most electronegative (4.0).



Question 42

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Alpha: A-4, Z-2. ²³⁸U → ²³⁴Th + ⁴He.



Question 43

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Noble gases have highest IE — completely stable electron configuration.



Question 44

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  H-bond: H bonded to highly electronegative N, O or F. Examples: H2O, HF, NH3.



Question 45

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  A=6+8=14. Z=6. Symbol: ¹⁴₆C (carbon-14).



Question 46

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Isotopes: same Z, different A. ³⁵Cl and ³⁷Cl: both Z=17, different A.



Question 47

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2O: 2 bonding + 2 lone pairs → V-SHAPED. Bond angle 104.5°.



Question 48

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Huge jump between 1st and 2nd IE → only 1 valence electron → Group IA.



Question 49

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Melting breaks lattice → ions FREE TO MOVE → carry charge → conduct electricity.



Question 50

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Na (Z=11): 1s²2s²2p⁶3s¹. Na⁺ loses 1 electron → 1s²2s²2p⁶ (same as Ne). 10 electrons.



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  d subshell: 5 orbitals × 2 electrons each = 10 electrons maximum.



Question 52

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  NH4⁺ formed: NH3 + H⁺. N donates both electrons to H⁺ → COORDINATE (dative) bond. NH4⁺ has one coordinate bond among its four N-H bonds.



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  BeCl2: Be has 2 bonding pairs, no lone pairs → sp hybridised → LINEAR. Bond angle 180°.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  S is in Group VIA → 6 valence electrons. O (Group VIA) and Se (Group VIA) also have 6 valence electrons.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ionic bond: large electronegativity difference (typically >1.7) causes one atom to transfer electrons to another.



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ethane: C-C (1 sigma) + 3×C-H per C (6 sigma total) = 7 sigma bonds. ALL single bonds are sigma.



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Metals have METALLIC BONDING: positive metal ions (cations) in a 'sea' of delocalised electrons. This explains conductivity, malleability, lustre.



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  NaCl: Na⁺ and Cl⁻ in GIANT IONIC LATTICE. Diamond = covalent network. Graphite = covalent layers. Iodine = simple molecular.



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl (Z=17): 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁵. Cl⁻ gains 1 electron → 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁶ (same as Ar). 18 electrons.



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Principal quantum number n (1,2,3...) describes the ENERGY LEVEL or SHELL. n=1 is the innermost shell, closest to nucleus.



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CARBON-14 (t½=5730 years) is used in RADIOCARBON DATING — organisms absorb ¹⁴C while alive; after death, ¹⁴C decays → ratio ¹⁴C/¹²C measured → age calculated.



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe: [Ar]3d⁶4s². The 3d subshell is being FILLED for Fe (d⁶). The last electron added goes into 3d.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Atomic radius INCREASES DOWN a group (more electron shells). Rb has most shells → largest radius.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  OCTET RULE: atoms tend to gain, lose, or share electrons to achieve 8 VALENCE ELECTRONS (like noble gas configuration).



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  NaCl giant ionic lattice: many strong ELECTROSTATIC ATTRACTIONS between oppositely charged ions in all directions → high melting point (801°C).



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  N≡N (triple bond): 1 sigma + 2 pi bonds. The two pi bonds are perpendicular to each other and to the internuclear axis.



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  CH4: 4 bonding pairs, 0 lone pairs → TETRAHEDRAL (perfect). Bond angle 109.5°. CO2=linear, H2O=bent, BF3=trigonal planar.



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ELECTRON AFFINITY: energy released (exothermic for most atoms) when an electron is added to a neutral gaseous atom to form a gaseous anion.



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  NEUTRON mass ≈ proton mass ≈ 1.67×10⁻²⁷ kg ≈ 1 amu. Electron is 1/1836 of proton mass.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ar has 18 electrons. K⁺: Z=19, loses 1 → 18 electrons. Na⁺: 10 electrons. Mg²⁺: 10 electrons. K⁺ is isoelectronic with Ar.



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ionic compounds do NOT conduct in SOLID STATE — ions are fixed in the lattice and cannot move. They conduct only when MOLTEN or in AQUEOUS solution.



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  VSEPR: lone pair-lone pair > lone pair-bonding pair > bonding pair-bonding pair repulsion. Two lone pairs on O compress H-O-H from ideal 109.5° (tetrahedral) to 104.5°.



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  f subshell: 7 orbitals × 2 electrons = 14 electrons maximum. d=10, p=6, s=2.



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  19/3.8=5 half-lives. Remaining=(1/2)⁵×100=100/32=3.125 g.



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Neutrons=A-Z=40-20=20. The ionic charge (+2) affects electrons, NOT neutrons. Neutrons are always=A-Z regardless of ionic state.



Question 76

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  PARAMAGNETIC = has unpaired electrons (attracted to magnetic field). O2 has 2 unpaired electrons in π* antibonding orbitals (MO theory). He, Ne = noble gases (all paired). N2 = all paired (diamagnetic).



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Na (3s¹) vs Mg (3s²). Na's single 3s electron has less nuclear attraction (less Zeff per electron) → more easily removed. Also, Mg's 3s² is stabilised by electron pair interactions.



Question 78

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  HF has HYDROGEN BONDING (strongest intermolecular force after ionic/covalent). H2, N2, CH4 have only van der Waals forces. This explains HF's anomalously high BP for its molecular mass.



Question 79

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Diamond: each C forms 4 covalent bonds to 4 other C in a GIANT COVALENT NETWORK. Extremely strong → hardest natural material, highest melting point of any element.



Question 80

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  β⁻ decay: n → p + e⁻ + antineutrino. A neutron in the nucleus converts to a proton, emitting a high-energy electron (beta particle). Atomic number increases by 1.



Question 81

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Lattice energy depends on ionic charges and ionic radii. MgO: Mg²⁺ and O²⁻ (higher charges, smaller ions) → much greater electrostatic attraction → highest lattice energy of these options.



Question 82

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cu anomalous config: [Ar]3d¹⁰4s¹ (not 3d⁹4s²). Filled d subshell is extra stable → one 4s electron promoted to fill 3d.



Question 83

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ne has 10 electrons. F⁻: 10. Na⁺: 10. O²⁻: 10. Mg²⁺: 10. ALL are isoelectronic with Ne! JAMB answer typically D (Mg²⁺ is included). If question is phrased to exclude one, review context.



Question 84

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Po: Z=84. Alpha decay: Z decreases by 2. Product Z=84-2=82 (Lead, Pb).



Question 85

✅ ANSWER: E

📝 EXPLANATION:  P has Z=15=15 electrons. P>Si(14)>Al(13)>Mg(12)>Na(11). P has most electrons. If only 4 options given, Si(14) vs P(15).



Question 86

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Einstein's E=mc²: mass defect (Δm) is converted to energy. Even tiny mass changes produce enormous energy (c²=9×10¹⁶ m²/s²).



Question 87

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  METALLIC BONDING: metal cations in a 'sea' of DELOCALISED (free/mobile) electrons. Explains: conductivity (electrons flow), malleability (layers slide), high melting point (many bonds), metallic lustre.



Question 88

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ΔEN=3.5-0.9=2.6. ΔEN>1.7 → predominantly IONIC character. (X is likely Na, EN=0.9; Y is likely F, EN=4.0 or O, EN=3.5.)



Question 89

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ¹⁴C is an ISOTOPE of ¹²C: same Z=6 (6 protons, 6 electrons) but A=14 → neutrons=14-6=8.



Question 90

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  p orbitals: DUMBBELL-shaped with two lobes along the x, y, or z axis. There are 3 p orbitals (px, py, pz), each holding max 2 electrons (6 total).



Question 91

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe: [Ar]3d⁶4s². The 4s is filled before 3d (Aufbau principle). Fe is in period 4, Group VIII transition metal.



Question 92

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  THOMSON's model (1897): electrons ('plums') embedded in a diffuse ball ('pudding') of positive charge. Disproved by Rutherford's gold foil experiment (1911).



Question 93

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Large jump between 3rd and 4th IE means 3 valence electrons. After removing 3, you break into the stable noble gas core → huge energy required. Element is in GROUP IIIA (e.g., Al, B).



Question 94

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Alpha particles (+2 charge) are DEFLECTED towards the NEGATIVE plate. Beta-minus (negative) deflects to positive plate. Gamma (neutral) is not deflected.



Question 95

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BOHR MODEL: electrons occupy discrete energy levels (shells) with specific energies. Electrons can only be in these QUANTISED levels — not in between. Transitions between levels → emission/absorption of specific photons.



Question 96

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cr anomalous: [Ar]3d⁵4s¹ (half-filled d = extra stable). Like Cu, Cr promotes one 4s electron to fill 3d half-shell.



Question 97

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Mass number A = protons + neutrons = 11 + 12 = 23.



Question 98

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  URANIUM-235 is the primary nuclear fuel. ²³⁵U undergoes fission (splits into lighter nuclei + neutrons + energy) when struck by a slow neutron. Used in nuclear power stations.



Question 99

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Bond energy increases with bond order. C≡C (triple) > C=C (double) > C-C (single). Triple bond: ~835 kJ/mol. Double: ~614 kJ/mol. Single: ~346 kJ/mol.



Question 100

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  SPIN QUANTUM NUMBER ms = +½ or -½. Describes the intrinsic spin of the electron (clockwise or anticlockwise). Pauli exclusion: max 2 electrons per orbital with OPPOSITE spins.



Question 101

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Greater ELECTRONEGATIVITY DIFFERENCE → more unequal electron sharing → greater ionic character. HF (ΔEN=1.78) is more ionic than HCl (ΔEN=1.0).



Question 102

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  GRAPHITE (a form of carbon) conducts electricity because it has a layer structure with DELOCALISED electrons between layers that can move. Graphite = only non-metal conductor at room temperature.



Question 103

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  GAMMA emission: excess nuclear energy released as EM radiation (photons). No particles emitted → no change in A or Z. Often follows alpha or beta decay.



Question 104

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Born-Haber cycle: IE+EA+atomisation+lattice energy combine. High LATTICE ENERGY of NaCl (789 kJ/mol) more than compensates for IE of Na → overall exothermic.



Question 105

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ca²⁺: Z=20, loses 2 electrons → 18 electrons (same as Ar). Mg²⁺ has 10 electrons. Zn²⁺ has 28 electrons. Ca²⁺ has 18 electrons.



Question 106

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Covalent (molecular) compounds: held by weak intermolecular forces → LOW melting points. No ions or free electrons → POOR electrical conductors. Exceptions: graphite (conductor), ionic-like network covalents (diamond, high MP).



Question 107

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NUCLEUS: contains PROTONS (+) and NEUTRONS (0) only. Electrons orbit the nucleus in energy levels/shells. Nucleus is very small but contains almost all the mass.



Question 108

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Change in A: 238-206=32. Each alpha: A decreases by 4. Alpha emissions=32/4=8.



Question 109

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Beta-minus decay: n→p + e⁻. Neutron converts to proton → Z increases by 1. A remains same.



Question 110

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cu: [Ar]3d¹⁰4s¹. Cu²⁺ loses 2 electrons — first from 4s, then from 3d: [Ar]3d⁹.



Question 111

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  HF (M=20, BP=19.5°C) has HYDROGEN BONDING (F is highly electronegative). HCl (M=36.5, BP=-85°C) has only van der Waals forces. H-bonds in HF are much stronger → higher BP despite lower mass.



Question 112

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe: [Ar]3d⁶4s². 3d⁶: distribute 6 electrons in 5 d orbitals by Hund's rule: ↑↑↑↑↑/↑ = 4 unpaired (singly occupied) + 1 paired. Unpaired=4.



Question 113

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Atomic number (Z) = number of protons in the nucleus. In a neutral atom, Z also equals the number of electrons.



Question 114

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Isotopes: same Z (same protons and electrons), different mass number A (different neutrons). e.g. ¹²C and ¹⁴C.



Question 115

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Mass number A = number of protons (Z) + number of neutrons (N). A = Z + N.



Question 116

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ca: 2,8,8,2 (or 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁶4s²). Calcium has 20 electrons filling the first four shells.



Question 117

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Max electrons in shell n = 2n². For n=3: 2(3²) = 18.



Question 118

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Aufbau principle order: 1s, 2s, 2p, 3s, 3p, 4s, 3d, 4p... After 3p, 4s fills before 3d.



Question 119

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ionic bonds form by electron transfer from a metal (loses electrons → cation) to a non-metal (gains electrons → anion).



Question 120

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Covalent bond: formed by sharing one or more pairs of electrons between two atoms (usually non-metals).



Question 121

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Water has 2 bonding pairs + 2 lone pairs on O. VSEPR: tetrahedral electron geometry but bent molecular shape, bond angle ~104.5°.



Question 122

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  7 outer electrons → needs 1 more to complete the octet → gains 1e⁻ to form −1 anion (like chlorine Cl⁻).



Question 123

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Across period: nuclear charge (Z) increases while atomic radius decreases → stronger pull on shared electrons → higher electronegativity.



Question 124

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Coordinate (dative covalent) bond: one atom (donor) provides both electrons of the shared pair. e.g. NH3 + H⁺ → NH4⁺.



Question 125

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  VSEPR (Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion) theory: electron pairs repel each other, determining molecular geometry.



Question 126

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  In metals, atoms lose outer electrons to form positive ions; these electrons are delocalised throughout the structure, creating metallic bonding.



Question 127

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO2: O=C=O. Carbon forms two double bonds with the two oxygen atoms. Linear molecule.



Question 128

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Down a group: more inner electron shells shield outer electrons from nuclear charge → effective nuclear charge on outermost electron decreases → lower IE.



Question 129

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  4 electron pairs (all bonding, no lone pairs) → tetrahedral shape with bond angles of 109.5°. Example: CH4.



Question 130

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  1st IE: X(g) → X⁺(g) + e⁻. Energy needed to remove outermost electron from 1 mol of gaseous atoms.



Question 131

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Na outer electron: n=3, shielded by 2+8=10 inner electrons. Neon's outer electrons: n=2, only 2 inner electrons. Na's outer electron is much easier to remove.



Question 132

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Hydrogen bonds form when H is covalently bonded to a highly electronegative atom (F, O, or N), creating a strong dipole that attracts lone pairs on neighbouring F, O, or N.



Question 133

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Water has extensive intermolecular H-bonds (each molecule can form up to 4). These extra attractions require more energy to overcome → high b.p. (100°C vs −61°C expected).



Question 134

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NH3: 3 bonding pairs + 1 lone pair on N. Lone pair repels bonding pairs more → pyramidal shape, angles ~107°.



Question 135

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The nucleus contains protons (positive charge) and neutrons (no charge). Electrons orbit outside the nucleus.



Question 136

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Relative atomic mass (Ar) = weighted average of isotopic masses based on their natural abundance percentages.



Question 137

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Polar covalent bond: between atoms of different electronegativities. The more electronegative atom attracts bonding electrons more → partial charges δ+ and δ−.



Question 138

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  London (dispersion) forces increase with number of electrons and molecular size. Small, non-polar molecules (e.g. He, H2) have the weakest van der Waals forces.



Question 139

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Octet rule: atoms bond to achieve 8 electrons in their outer shell (noble gas configuration). Exceptions: H (2), Be (4), B (6), expanded octets (P, S).



Question 140

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Tetrahedral geometry (e.g. CH4): 4 identical bonds, bond angle = 109.5°.



Question 141

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Noble gases have the highest IE in their periods (full outer shells, very stable). Ne has higher IE than Na, Mg, or Al.



Question 142

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl has 17 electrons (2,8,7). Cl⁻ gains 1 electron: 2,8,8 (18 electrons, like argon).



Question 143

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sigma bonds: end-on overlap of atomic orbitals (s-s, s-p, p-p head-on). All single bonds are sigma bonds.



Question 144

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Pi bonds: sideways/lateral overlap of parallel p orbitals. Double bonds contain 1σ + 1π; triple bonds have 1σ + 2π.



Question 145

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO2: 2 double bonds, no lone pairs on C → linear (180°). H2O and NH3 have lone pairs causing bending.



Question 146

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Resonance structures: same arrangement of atoms but different positions of π electrons and lone pairs. The actual molecule is a hybrid of all resonance structures.



Question 147

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe: [Ar]4s²3d⁶ = 1s²2s²2p⁶3s²3p⁶4s²3d⁶. Fe²⁺ loses 4s electrons first: [Ar]3d⁶.



Question 148

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  sp³ hybridisation: one s + three p orbitals mix → 4 equivalent sp³ orbitals → tetrahedral arrangement (109.5°). Example: CH4.



Question 149

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Noble gases (Group 18/0) have full outer electron shells (He: 2; others: 8 electrons). No tendency to gain, lose, or share electrons.





































































05

🌬️ AIR & ATMOSPHERE

Composition · Liquid Air · Nitrogen Cycle · Rusting · Prevention



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Air is like an invisible mixture — you can't see it, but it's full of different gas molecules constantly flying around at hundreds of metres per second. The main gases in air are N₂ (like a shy majority that rarely reacts), O₂ (the reactive worker that supports combustion and life), and Ar (the noble loner that refuses to react with anything).



5.1  Composition of Dry Air

GAS

PERCENTAGE & KEY POINTS

Nitrogen (N₂)  78%

Most abundant gas. The N≡N triple bond is VERY STRONG (hard to break), making N₂ relatively unreactive under normal conditions. This is why the air does not burst into flames even though it contains oxygen! Used in Haber process and as inert blanket.

Oxygen (O₂)  21%

The reactive gas — supports combustion and aerobic respiration. NOT the most abundant (common JAMB trap — 'most abundant gas in air = NITROGEN').

Argon (Ar)  0.93%

Noble gas, truly inert. Used in light bulbs and welding — NOT nitrogen! (N₂ can slowly form oxides with the tungsten filament at high temperature.)

Carbon dioxide (CO₂)  0.04%

Essential for photosynthesis. Rising due to fossil fuel burning → enhanced greenhouse effect.

Water vapour

Variable (0–4%). Excluded from 'dry air' measurements. Highest near equator (Nigeria!).

💡 EXAM TIP

COMMON JAMB QUESTION: 'Which gas is used in light bulbs?' = ARGON (Ar), NOT nitrogen. 'Most abundant gas in air?' = NITROGEN (78%), NOT oxygen. 'Which is NOT a greenhouse gas?' = N₂ and O₂ (symmetric, nonpolar — cannot absorb infrared radiation).

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The air in Lagos right now is about 78% nitrogen molecules that have been cycling through the atmosphere for billions of years. Some of those nitrogen molecules may have been breathed by dinosaurs, absorbed by ancient plants, and fixed by bacteria millions of times before reaching your lungs today!



5.2  Fractional Distillation of Liquid Air

To separate the gases in air, we must LIQUEFY it first (cool to −200°C) then FRACTIONALLY DISTIL it — separating the components by their different boiling points.

FILTRATION: Remove dust particles from air

DRYING: Remove water vapour (silica gel desiccant) and CO₂ (NaOH solution)

COMPRESSION: Compress to high pressure (~200 atm)

COOLING: Allow compressed gas to expand rapidly through a nozzle — Joule-Thomson effect causes dramatic cooling. Repeated compression/expansion cycles until air liquefies (−200°C)

FRACTIONAL DISTILLATION: In insulated column — N₂ (bp −196°C) boils off first at TOP; Ar (bp −186°C) collected in middle; O₂ (bp −183°C) stays as liquid longest — collected at BOTTOM

GAS (BOILING POINT)

USES

Nitrogen (−196°C) — TOP of column

Haber process (fertilisers), liquid N₂ (cryogenics, preserving biological samples), food packaging (prevents oxidation), shrink-fitting metal parts.

Argon (−186°C) — MIDDLE

Light bulbs (prevents filament from oxidising), welding atmosphere (shields hot metal from O₂), window double-glazing (better insulator than air).

Oxygen (−183°C) — BOTTOM

Hospitals (breathing), steel making (basic oxygen furnace — blasts O₂ into molten iron), welding, water treatment, rocket fuel oxidiser.



5.3  The Nitrogen Cycle — Nature's Recycling System



THE NITROGEN CYCLE

           ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐

           │           ATMOSPHERE (N₂, 78%)          │

           └────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────┘

          FIXATION  │                  │  DENITRIFICATION

      (Rhizobium/   │                  │  (Pseudomonas

       Haber/       │                  │   bacteria,

       lightning)   ▼                  │   anaerobic soil)

           ┌────────────────┐          │

           │  Ammonia (NH₄⁺)│──────────┘

           └────────┬───────┘

        NITRIFICATION│ (Nitrosomonas bacteria)

                     ▼

           ┌─────────────────┐

           │  Nitrite (NO₂⁻) │

           └─────────┬───────┘

       NITRIFICATION │ (Nitrobacter bacteria)

                     ▼

           ┌─────────────────┐  ASSIMILATION  ┌──────────────┐

           │  Nitrate (NO₃⁻) │──────────────►│    PLANTS    │

           └─────────────────┘                │ (proteins,   │

                     ▲                         │  DNA, etc.)  │

         AMMONIFICATION                        └──────┬───────┘

          (decomposers)                               │EATEN BY

           ┌────────────────────────────────────────◄─┘

           │         DEAD ORGANISMS / WASTE             │

           └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘



N is essential for PROTEINS (amino acids), DNA, ATP, and CHLOROPHYLL. Despite being 78% of air, most organisms CANNOT use N₂ directly — the N≡N triple bond is too strong. The nitrogen cycle converts N₂ into usable forms and returns it to the atmosphere.

STAGE

PROCESS & ORGANISMS

NITROGEN FIXATION

N₂ → NH₃ or NO₃⁻. Agents: (1) Rhizobium bacteria in ROOT NODULES of legumes (beans, groundnuts, cowpeas — all common in Nigeria). (2) Free-living bacteria: Azotobacter, Clostridium. (3) Lightning (high energy breaks N≡N: N₂+O₂→2NO). (4) Industrial Haber process.

AMMONIFICATION

Dead organisms and animal waste → NH₃/NH₄⁺. Decomposers (bacteria and fungi) break down proteins → release ammonia back into soil.

NITRIFICATION

NH₄⁺ → NO₂⁻ → NO₃⁻. Two-step oxidation by specialist bacteria: Nitrosomonas (NH₄⁺→NO₂⁻) then Nitrobacter (NO₂⁻→NO₃⁻). Requires aerobic (oxygen-rich) conditions. Makes nitrate (plant food) available.

ASSIMILATION

Plants absorb NO₃⁻ (and some NH₄⁺) through roots → convert to proteins, DNA, chlorophyll using energy from photosynthesis.

DENITRIFICATION

NO₃⁻ → N₂. Denitrifying bacteria (Pseudomonas) in ANAEROBIC (waterlogged) soil. Returns N₂ to atmosphere. WHY waterlogged farms are less fertile: denitrification removes nitrogen from soil.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Legumes (beans, groundnuts, cowpeas, soybeans) have a superpower — their roots form partnerships with Rhizobium bacteria. The bacteria live in special swellings called root nodules and 'fix' nitrogen from the air directly into NH₃, which the plant uses for growth. This is why planting beans helps fertilise the soil for the next crop — a traditional farming technique used by Nigerian farmers for centuries before synthetic fertilisers were invented!



5.4  Rusting — Chemistry and Prevention



RUSTING — Conditions Required & Prevention Methods

  RUSTING requires BOTH oxygen AND water (neither alone is enough!)



  Tube 1:  Iron + dry air (CaCl₂ desiccant)     → NO RUST  ✓

  Tube 2:  Iron + boiled water, oil seal         → NO RUST  ✓  (no O₂)

  Tube 3:  Iron + ordinary water + air           → RUSTS    ✗

  Tube 4:  Iron + salt water + air               → RUSTS FASTER ✗✗



  PREVENTION METHODS:

  ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │ Painting     │ Physical barrier (fails when scratched)            │

  │ Oiling       │ Physical barrier (needs reapplication)             │

  │ Galvanising  │ Zinc coat: physical + SACRIFICIAL (best for iron)  │

  │ Sacrificial  │ Zn/Mg block attached: corrodes INSTEAD of iron     │

  │ Tin plating  │ Physical only – if scratched, iron rusts FASTER!   │

  │ Stainless    │ Cr forms self-healing Cr₂O₃ layer                 │

  │ steel        │                                                    │

  └──────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘



  KEY JAMB POINT: Zn is MORE reactive than Fe → protects Fe even if

  zinc coating is scratched. Sn is LESS reactive → iron rusts faster!



RUST is hydrated iron(III) oxide: Fe₂O₃·xH₂O. It is an ELECTROCHEMICAL process — essentially a small battery forms on the iron surface wherever both water and oxygen are present.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The Eiffel Tower in Paris would rust to nothing in about 10 years without maintenance — it takes 25 painters about 15 months to repaint it each time! In Lagos, iron roofing sheets in areas near the ocean (like Victoria Island) rust much faster because salt water (NaCl) is a much better electrolyte than fresh water — iron corrodes up to 10 times faster near the sea.

AIR POLLUTION:

CO: from incomplete combustion; binds haemoglobin 200x stronger than O2

SO2 + NO2: acid rain | CFCs: ozone depletion

Greenhouse gases (CO2, CH4, N2O): global warming

💡 EXAM TIP:  RUSTING requires BOTH oxygen AND water. Prevention: painting, galvanizing (zinc), alloying (stainless steel), sacrificial protection, electroplating.

Question 1

Question 50

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ar ≈ 0.93% of dry air by volume. Third most abundant after N2 and O2.



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Modern airships use HELIUM (non-flammable) for lift. Historical airships used H2 (led to Hindenburg disaster). He=safe, H2=dangerous.

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SO2+H2O→H2SO3 (sulphurous acid, a weak acid). H2SO3 can be further oxidised to H2SO4 in air.



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  GLOBAL WARMING: primarily caused by INCREASED GREENHOUSE GASES (CO2, CH4, N2O, CFCs) from burning fossil fuels, deforestation, agriculture — ENHANCED greenhouse effect.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  N2 has the LOWEST boiling point (-196°C) among the major air components. As liquid air warms, N2 boils off FIRST. It's the last to liquify when cooling.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO2 (M=44) is heavier than air (M≈29). NH3 (17), H2 (2), CH4 (16) are all lighter.



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  By MASS, O2 is about 23% of air (heavier than N2). By VOLUME, O2 = 21%. Mass% is higher because O2 (M=32) is heavier than N2 (M=28).



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ostwald process: NH3 + O2 (Pt/Rh catalyst, 850°C) → NO → NO2 → HNO3 (NITRIC ACID). Used in fertiliser and explosive manufacture.



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DIFFERENT BOILING POINTS allow fractional distillation. N2 (BP -196°C) separates from Ar (-186°C) and O2 (-183°C).



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  O2 in industry: (1) BLAST FURNACE (ore smelting — enriched air used), (2) welding/cutting metals, (3) rocket propulsion, (4) medical oxygen therapy.



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The OXYGEN inside the jar is consumed by combustion. No fresh O2 enters → when O2 falls below ~16%, combustion cannot continue → candle goes out.



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  N2 is INERT — displaces O2 from the packaging. O2 causes oxidative rancidity and bacterial growth. N2 atmosphere prevents these → longer shelf life. Also puffs up the bag protectively.



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  DENITRIFICATION: anaerobic bacteria (Pseudomonas, Thiobacillus) convert NO3⁻ and NO2⁻ back to N2 gas, which escapes to atmosphere. Completes the nitrogen cycle.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Noble gases ≈ 1% of dry air by volume. Mostly argon (0.93%). Other noble gases (He, Ne, Kr, Xe) are in trace amounts.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  STRATOSPHERIC OZONE (ozone layer, 15-35 km): BENEFICIAL — absorbs harmful UV. TROPOSPHERIC OZONE (near ground): HARMFUL — a pollutant, damages lungs, plants, causes smog.



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Carbon dioxide (CO₂) traps heat from the sun, contributing to the greenhouse effect and global warming.



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dry air contains about 78% nitrogen, 21% oxygen, 0.9% argon, and 0.03% CO₂.



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Air is liquefied by compression and cooling, then fractionally distilled. Nitrogen (bp -196°C) boils off before oxygen (bp -183°C).



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rusting (corrosion of iron) requires both oxygen and water to occur. Neither alone is sufficient.



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Argon makes up about 0.93% of the atmosphere, making it the most abundant noble gas in air.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Nitrogen is chemically inert under normal conditions, so it prevents oxidation and spoilage of food when used as a packaging gas.



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Nitrogen fixation is the process where nitrogen gas (N₂) is converted into compounds like ammonia or nitrates by bacteria (e.g., Rhizobium) or lightning.



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Nitrogen makes up approximately 78% of the atmosphere, making it the most abundant gas in air.



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sulfur dioxide (SO₂) and nitrogen dioxide (NO₂) from burning fossil fuels dissolve in rainwater to form sulfuric and nitric acids.



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  The ozone (O₃) layer in the stratosphere absorbs harmful ultraviolet (UV) radiation from the sun, protecting life on Earth.



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO₂ is heavier than air and does not support combustion. It smothers fires by cutting off oxygen supply.



Question 76

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Catalytic converters convert toxic CO and NO into less harmful CO₂ and N₂ through catalytic reactions.



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Plants absorb CO₂ from the atmosphere during photosynthesis: 6CO₂ + 6H₂O → C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂.



Question 78

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs) from refrigerants and aerosols release chlorine radicals that catalytically destroy ozone molecules.



Question 79

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Hydrogenation is not part of the nitrogen cycle. The cycle involves nitrogen fixation, nitrification, denitrification, and ammonification.



Question 80

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  During cellular respiration, glucose is oxidized: C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂ → 6CO₂ + 6H₂O + energy. CO₂ is released.



Question 81

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Inhaled air has ~0.04% CO₂; exhaled air has ~4% CO₂. This represents the greatest relative increase of any component.



Question 82

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The Haber process reacts N₂ + 3H₂ ⇌ 2NH₃ at 450°C and 200 atm with an iron catalyst to fix atmospheric nitrogen.



Question 83

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Burning fossil fuels releases stored carbon as CO₂. Deforestation reduces CO₂ absorption by plants, compounding the increase.



Question 84

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Clean dry air contains N₂, O₂, Ar, CO₂, Ne, He, Kr, and Xe. Carbon monoxide (CO) is a pollutant, not a natural component.



Question 85

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Iron reacts with oxygen and water to form hydrated iron(III) oxide (rust): 4Fe + 3O₂ + 2nH₂O → 2Fe₂O₃·nH₂O.



Question 86

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Galvanizing coats iron with zinc. Zinc is more reactive than iron (sacrificial protection), preventing rust even when the coating is scratched.



Question 87

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Burning magnesium produces an intensely bright white flame. The reaction: 2Mg + O₂ → 2MgO releases enormous light energy.



Question 88

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Air is a mixture: its components (N₂, O₂, Ar, etc.) retain their properties and can be separated by physical means like fractional distillation.



Question 89

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Oxygen is the oxidizing agent in combustion. Burning requires fuel, oxygen, and heat (the fire triangle).



Question 90

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Photochemical smog forms when hydrocarbons and nitrogen oxides from vehicle exhaust react in the presence of sunlight to form ozone and other oxidants.



Question 91

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Both concentrated H₂SO₄ and anhydrous CaCl₂ are drying agents. The choice depends on what gas is being dried — CaCl₂ cannot dry NH₃ (it reacts with it).



Question 92

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Liquid oxygen boils at -183°C (-296°F). Liquid nitrogen boils at -196°C, which is why nitrogen boils off first during fractional distillation of air.



Question 93

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Denitrification is carried out by bacteria (e.g., Pseudomonas) that convert nitrates back to nitrogen gas (N₂), returning it to the atmosphere.



Question 94

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO binds to haemoglobin 200× more strongly than O₂, forming carboxyhemoglobin. This prevents oxygen transport, causing suffocation.



Question 95

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  An oxidizing agent accepts electrons or provides oxygen for combustion. Oxygen itself does not burn (is not a fuel); it supports the burning of fuels.



Question 96

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Nitrogen (bp -196°C) has a lower boiling point than oxygen (bp -183°C), so it evaporates and is collected first during warming of liquid air.



Question 97

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  In limited oxygen supply, incomplete combustion occurs: 2C + O₂ → 2CO. Complete combustion (excess O₂) gives CO₂.



Question 98

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The N≡N triple bond (bond energy ~944 kJ/mol) is extremely strong, making nitrogen very unreactive under normal conditions.



Question 99

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Greenhouse gases (CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, H₂O vapour) trap infrared radiation from Earth's surface, causing the planet to warm.



Question 100

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Oxygen relights a glowing wooden splint. This is the standard lab test for identifying oxygen gas.



Question 101

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Heating ammonium nitrite solution: NH₄NO₂ → N₂ + 2H₂O. The gas produced is nitrogen.







































06

💧 WATER & SOLUTIONS

Hardness · Purification · Properties · Colligative Properties



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Water molecules are like tiny magnets — the oxygen end is slightly negative (δ⁻) and the hydrogen end is slightly positive (δ⁺). This polarity allows water molecules to attract each other through hydrogen bonds. These hydrogen bonds are the secret behind almost all of water's unusual properties.



6.1  Why Water is Special — Hydrogen Bonding

H₂O is a BENT molecule (104.5°) because oxygen has 2 lone pairs (VSEPR). The O−H bonds are POLAR. Water forms up to 4 HYDROGEN BONDS per molecule (2 as donor through H atoms, 2 as acceptor through lone pairs on O). This network of hydrogen bonds explains all anomalous properties.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Ice is LESS dense than liquid water (which is why it floats) — this is extremely unusual. Almost all other substances are DENSER as solids than as liquids. If ice sank, lakes and rivers would freeze solid from the bottom up, killing all aquatic life. The fact that ice floats means the bottom of Lake Chad stays liquid even in cold weather, allowing fish to survive. This quirk of water makes life on Earth possible!



ANOMALOUS PROPERTY

EXPLANATION

Boiling point 100°C (HIGH)

Without H-bonds, expected bp ≈ −90°C (like H₂S, which is similar size but no H-bonds with H-S). The H-bonds require lots of energy to break → high bp.

Maximum density at 4°C

Ice (0°C) is LESS DENSE than liquid water. Ice has an open hexagonal H-bonded structure. When ice melts, some H-bonds break and molecules pack MORE closely → density INCREASES until 4°C, then decreases.

Expands on freezing

Volume increases ~9% when water freezes. H-bonded hexagonal structure is more open than liquid. Consequence: burst water pipes in cold weather; frost shatters rocks.

High specific heat (4.18 J/g°C)

Lots of energy needed to increase temperature (breaking and weakening H-bonds). Moderates coastal climates — Lagos has mild temperatures compared to inland cities at same latitude.

High latent heat of vaporisation

Large energy to break H-bonds during vaporisation. Makes sweating very effective for cooling body temperature.

Excellent solvent

Polar water molecules surround and solvate ions and polar molecules. Called 'universal solvent' (though nothing dissolves EVERYTHING).



6.2  Water Hardness — The Complete Picture

Hard water contains dissolved Ca²⁺ and Mg²⁺ ions. These ions react with soap to form INSOLUBLE SCUM: Ca²⁺ + 2(C₁₇H₃₅COO⁻) → (C₁₇H₃₅COO)₂Ca↓ (scum, grey precipitate). This wastes soap and prevents lathering. DETERGENTS (synthetic surfactants) do NOT form scum — they work in hard water.

TYPE

DETAILS

TEMPORARY HARDNESS

CAUSE: Ca(HCO₃)₂ and Mg(HCO₃)₂ — formed when CO₂-rich rainwater dissolves limestone: CaCO₃ + H₂O + CO₂ → Ca(HCO₃)₂ REMOVAL: (1) BOILING: Ca(HCO₃)₂ → CaCO₃↓ + H₂O + CO₂ (the white limescale in kettles!). (2) Clark's method: add Ca(OH)₂ → CaCO₃↓ precipitates. (3) Ion exchange resin.

PERMANENT HARDNESS

CAUSE: CaSO₄, MgSO₄, CaCl₂, MgCl₂ — these salts do NOT decompose on boiling. REMOVAL: (1) Na₂CO₃ (washing soda): Ca²⁺ + CO₃²⁻ → CaCO₃↓. (2) Ion exchange resin: Ca²⁺/Mg²⁺ swapped for Na⁺ or H⁺. (3) Distillation (gives perfectly pure water). (4) Reverse osmosis (modern method — forces water through tiny membrane under high pressure).

ADVANTAGES of hard water

Ca²⁺ and Mg²⁺ beneficial for teeth and bones. Less corrosive to lead/iron pipes. Better taste for drinking.

DISADVANTAGES

Scum from soap. Limescale (CaCO₃) in kettles, boilers, pipes — wastes energy, blocks pipes. Scale damages hot water systems.



6.3  Water Purification — How Your Tap Water is Made Safe

SCREENING: Large debris removed at water intake

SEDIMENTATION: Heavy particles sink in large settlement tanks

COAGULATION/FLOCCULATION: Alum (Al₂SO₄ or KAl(SO₄)₂) added → Al³⁺ + 3H₂O → Al(OH)₃↓ (gelatinous) → traps fine particles, bacteria, colloids by adsorption → particles clump and settle

SAND FILTRATION: Slow sand bed removes floc and microorganisms

CHLORINATION: Cl₂ or NaOCl added (0.1–0.5 ppm) → Cl₂ + H₂O → HCl + HOCl. HOCl kills bacteria and viruses by oxidising their cell components

pH ADJUSTMENT: Lime added to pH ~7.5–8 (slightly alkaline protects pipes)

FLUORIDATION (some countries): NaF added ~1 ppm to harden tooth enamel

💡 EXAM TIP

JAMB ROLES: ALUM = COAGULATION (clumping fine particles) — does NOT kill bacteria, does NOT soften water. CHLORINE = DISINFECTION (kills bacteria/viruses) — does NOT remove turbidity. Na₂CO₃ = SOFTENING — not added to drinking water. These roles are very frequently confused in JAMB!

6.4  Tests for Water

TEST

OBSERVATION

Anhydrous CuSO₄

White → BLUE in presence of water (forms CuSO₄·5H₂O, hydrated). Reverse: heating CuSO₄·5H₂O → white powder + water vapour.

Cobalt chloride paper

Blue → PINK when water is present. Anhydrous CoCl₂ = blue. Hydrated CoCl₂·6H₂O = pink.

Boiling point

Pure water boils at exactly 100°C (at 1 atm). Dissolved solute RAISES boiling point.

Freezing point

Pure water freezes at 0°C. Dissolved solute LOWERS freezing point.

6.5  Hygroscopic, Deliquescent, Efflorescent

TYPE

DESCRIPTION & EXAMPLES

HYGROSCOPIC

Absorbs moisture from air but STAYS SOLID. Examples: anhydrous CaCl₂, silica gel, P₄O₁₀, conc. H₂SO₄. Used as DESICCANTS (drying agents in packets).

DELIQUESCENT

Absorbs SO MUCH moisture it DISSOLVES to form solution. Examples: NaOH pellets, FeCl₃, ZnCl₂, CaCl₂ (strongly). That's why NaOH must be stored in sealed containers.

EFFLORESCENT

LOSES water of crystallisation to dry air → becomes powder. Examples: Na₂CO₃·10H₂O (washing soda) → loses 9 water molecules → Na₂CO₃·H₂O. Also: FeSO₄·7H₂O loses colour on standing.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The 'silica gel' packets you find in new shoes, electronics, and vitamin bottles contain silicon dioxide (SiO₂) with tiny pores that absorb moisture — a hygroscopic substance. When the packet turns from blue (cobalt chloride indicator inside shows it is DRY) to pink (it is FULL of water), you can regenerate it by heating in an oven at 120°C — the water evaporates and it turns blue again!

TESTS FOR WATER:

Anhydrous CuSO4: white -> blue

Cobalt chloride paper: blue -> pink

Boiling point: 100°C at 1 atm | Freezing point: 0°C

KEY PROPERTIES: Maximum density at 4°C. Expands on freezing (ice floats). High specific heat: 4.18 J/g°C. Universal solvent (polarity + H-bonding).

💡 EXAM TIP:  Hygroscopic: absorbs moisture, stays solid. Deliquescent: absorbs so much it dissolves. Efflorescent: loses water of crystallization to dry air (e.g., Na2CO3.10H2O).
