  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  MnO4-: Mn +7 to +2 (REDUCED). OXIDISING AGENT = accepts electrons.



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cu2++2e-=Cu (REDUCED). Cu2+ gains electrons. OXIDISING AGENT. Fe is reducing agent.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SO2 bleaches by REDUCTION. Unlike Cl2 which oxidises.



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe3O4=FeO+Fe2O3. Contains BOTH Fe2+ AND Fe3+ (mixed oxide).



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2 is oxidised (0 to +1). H2 is REDUCING AGENT. OIL RIG.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cr2O72-: Cr=+6. Product Cr3+=+3. Change: +6 to +3 (REDUCTION).



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  K=+1, 4O=-8. +1+x-8=0. x=+7.



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2+2NaOH=NaCl+NaOCl+H2O. Cl: 0 to -1(NaCl) AND 0 to +1(NaOCl). DISPROPORTIONATION.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  SHE: E0=0.00 V by definition. Reference for all E0 values.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Most negative E0 = strongest reducing agent. Ba (E0=-2.90V) most easily oxidised.



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2SO4: 2(+1)+x+4(-2)=0. x=+6.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe2+ easily oxidised to Fe3+ (loses e-). REDUCING AGENT.



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  REDUCING AGENT: LOSES electrons (is OXIDISED). OIL RIG.



Question 14

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  2x+3(-2)=0. x=+3.



Question 15

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  HOCl: H=+1, O=-2, Cl=x. +1-2+x=0. x=+1.



Question 16

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  KMnO4: Mn=+7. Product Mn2+=+2. Change: +7 to +2 (REDUCTION, gains 5e-).



Question 17

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  E0cell=E0cathode-E0anode=0.34-(-0.76)=1.10 V.



Question 18

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SO2: x+2(-2)=0. x=+4.



Question 19

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Na2S2O3 standard REDUCING AGENT in iodine titrations: 2S2O32-+I2=S4O62-+2I-.



Question 20

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cr: +6 to +3. Change=3. Each Cr gains 3 electrons.



Question 21

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO is a REDUCING AGENT (CO to CO2, C oxidised +2 to +4).



Question 22

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rusting: Fe to Fe2+ (OXIDATION). O2+2H2O+4e- to 4OH- (REDUCTION). Both = REDOX.



Question 23

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ca(OCl)Cl + H2SO4 = CaSO4+Cl2+H2O. Cl2 is bleaching agent (oxidising).



Question 24

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Spontaneous: E0cell > 0. DG0=-nFE0. E0>0 = DG0<0 = spontaneous.



Question 25

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  H3PO4: 3(+1)+x+4(-2)=0. x=+5.



Question 26

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2 (E0=+1.36V) > Br2 (E0=+1.07V). Cl2 more powerful oxidiser. Oxidises Br-.



Question 27

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ANODE: OXIDATION. CATHODE: REDUCTION. ANOX=ANOde=OXidation.



Question 28

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  IO3-: x+3(-2)=-1. x=+5.



Question 29

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2+2e-=2Cl- (REDUCED). Cl2 gains electrons. OXIDISING AGENT.



Question 30

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CORROSION PREVENTION: galvanisation, painting, cathodic protection, alloying.



Question 31

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  N2O: 2x+(-2)=0. x=+1.



Question 32

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  K2Cr2O7: 2(+1)+2x+7(-2)=0. x=+6.



Question 33

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CuO: Cu2+ (Cu is +2). O is -2. Sum=0. Cu is +2.



Question 34

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  REDUCTION: gain electrons, decrease OS. Cu2+(+2)+2e-=Cu(0). +2 to 0. Reduction.



Question 35

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  MnO4- GAINS electrons (5e-). Mn7+ to Mn2+ (REDUCTION). Accepts electrons. OXIDISING AGENT.



Question 36

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  NO3-: x+3(-2)=-1. x=+5.



Question 37

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2: 0 to +1 in H2O (OXIDISED). O2: 0 to -2 (REDUCED). H2 is reducing agent.



Question 38

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe2O3: 2x+3(-2)=0. x=+3.



Question 39

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Standard reduction potential (E0): measures tendency to GAIN ELECTRONS (be REDUCED). More positive = stronger oxidising agent.



Question 40

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Zn more reactive (more negative E0). OXIDISED. ANODE. Cu is cathode (reduced).



Question 41

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Zn loses electrons (Zn → Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻), so zinc is oxidised. It is also the reducing agent.



Question 42

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  K is +1, O is −2: +1 + Mn + 4(−2) = 0; Mn = +7.



Question 43

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Reducing agents lose electrons (are oxidised), thereby reducing other substances.



Question 44

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  2Cr + 7(−2) = −2; 2Cr = +12; Cr = +6.



Question 45

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Oxidation = loss of electrons: Fe²⁺ → Fe³⁺ + e⁻.



Question 46

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  In acidic solution: MnO4⁻ + 8H⁺ + 5e⁻ → Mn²⁺ + 4H2O. Mn goes from +7 to +2.



Question 47

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Disproportionation: a single species simultaneously undergoes both oxidation and reduction. e.g. 2H2O2 → 2H2O + O2.



Question 48

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  2(+1) + S + 4(−2) = 0; S = +6.



Question 49

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2 gains electrons (Cl2 + 2e⁻ → 2Cl⁻), so Cl2 is the oxidising agent.



Question 50

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Oxygen has oxidation state −2 in most compounds (except peroxides where it is −1, and OF2 where it is +2).



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Bleaching powder (Ca(OCl)Cl) releases nascent oxygen which oxidises and destroys coloured organic matter.



Question 52

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  OIL RIG: Oxidation Is Loss (of electrons); Reduction Is Gain (of electrons).



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2O2 oxidises Fe²⁺ to Fe³⁺, but reduces MnO4⁻ to Mn²⁺. It can do both — it's amphoteric in redox.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  4(+1) + N = +1; N = −3. Nitrogen is in its most reduced state in ammonium.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Na → Na⁺ + e⁻; Na loses an electron, so it is oxidised. Na is the reducing agent.



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Oxidising agents accept electrons from the substance being oxidised, so they are themselves reduced.



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Fe3O4 = FeO·Fe2O3; contains Fe²⁺ and Fe³⁺. Average = +8/3, but there are both states.



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Benedict's/Fehling's solution (contains Cu²⁺) is reduced to Cu⁺ (red precipitate) by reducing sugars.



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rusting: 4Fe + 3O2 + 6H2O → 4Fe(OH)3 → Fe2O3·H2O. Iron is oxidised from 0 to +3.



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Reduction = Gain of electrons (RIG from OIL RIG).



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The standard hydrogen electrode (SHE) is the reference electrode with E° = 0V by definition.



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Metals above hydrogen in the electrochemical series (like Zn, Fe, Mg) can displace H2 from dilute acids.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Zinc is above iron in reactivity series; it acts as a sacrificial anode, oxidising to protect the iron.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  The overall charge is 2−; Cr oxidation state = +6 (as calculated: 2Cr + 7(−2) = −2, Cr = +6).



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Elements in their standard state (e.g. O2, Fe metal, Na metal) have oxidation state 0.



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  KMnO4 is a powerful oxidising agent — used to oxidise alcohols, iron(II), and in titrations.



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SO2 has S in +4; SO3 has S in +6. This is an oxidation of sulphur.



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cu → Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻; copper is oxidised from 0 to +2. Silver is reduced from +1 to 0.



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  A galvanic/voltaic cell produces electricity from spontaneous redox reactions.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  In metal hydrides like NaH, hydrogen is −1 (hydride ion H⁻). Na is +1.



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  At the anode: oxidation always occurs (AN OX — Anode = Oxidation).



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  2H2 + O2 → 2H2O: H is oxidised from 0 to +1; O is reduced from 0 to −2. It's a redox reaction.



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Magnesium (or zinc) is more reactive than iron and corrodes preferentially, protecting the steel pipeline.



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2 is a strong oxidising agent. It destroys colour by oxidising the chromophores in dyes and organic matter.



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Corrosion is the oxidation of metals in the presence of moisture and O2 (or other oxidising agents).



Question 76

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Loss of hydrogen = oxidation (the hydrogen-electron model of oxidation). e.g. CH3CH2OH → CH3CHO (loss of H2).



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Sn²⁺ → Sn⁴⁺ + 2e⁻; tin loses electrons — it is oxidised. Iron is reduced from Fe³⁺ to Fe²⁺.





11

🔋 ELECTROLYSIS

Principles · Preferential Discharge · Faraday's Laws · Industrial Applications



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

A battery (galvanic cell) is like a waterfall — water naturally flows downhill and releases energy. Electrolysis is like a PUMP that forces water UPHILL — you must supply energy (electricity) to force a non-spontaneous chemical reaction to happen. The cathode is where positive ions are forced to accept electrons; the anode is where negative ions are forced to give up electrons.



ELECTROLYSIS CELL

                    BATTERY / D.C. SUPPLY

                   +│          │-

                    │          │

         ┌──────────┴──┐    ┌──┴──────────┐

         │  ANODE (+)  │    │ CATHODE (-) │

         │  OXIDATION  │    │  REDUCTION  │

         │  Anions ──► │    │ ◄── Cations │

         │  lose e⁻   │    │  gain e⁻   │

         └──────┬──────┘    └──────┬──────┘

                │                  │

                └────────┬─────────┘

                         │

              ┌──────────┴──────────┐

              │    ELECTROLYTE      │

              │  (molten or aq.)    │

              │  ions move freely   │

              └─────────────────────┘



  Memory: RED CAT (Reduction at Cathode) | AN OX (Oxidation at Anode)



11.1  Key Terms and Electrode Rules

TERM

EXPLANATION

CATHODE (−) — Reduction

Connected to NEGATIVE terminal of battery. Positive cations migrate HERE. CATHode → CATions arrive → gain electrons → REDUCED. Memory: RED CAT (Reduction at Cathode) Examples: Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu; 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂↑

ANODE (+) — Oxidation

Connected to POSITIVE terminal. Negative anions migrate HERE. ANode → ANions arrive → lose electrons → OXIDISED. Memory: AN OX (ANode = Oxidation) Examples: 2Cl⁻ → Cl₂ + 2e⁻; 2H₂O → O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻

Electrolyte

Ionic substance conducting electricity when MOLTEN or DISSOLVED in water. As solid: ions locked in fixed positions — CANNOT conduct. Examples: molten NaCl, CuSO₄(aq), dilute H₂SO₄, molten Al₂O₃.

Inert vs Active electrode

INERT: Does not react. Platinum (Pt) or graphite/carbon. ACTIVE: Dissolves during electrolysis. Example: copper anode in copper refining dissolves to replenish Cu²⁺ ions in solution.



11.2  Preferential Discharge of Ions

When several ions are present in solution, only the MOST EASILY DISCHARGED ion is released at each electrode. The order depends on the electrochemical series and concentration.

CATHODE — discharge order (most easily discharged FIRST):

Ag⁺ > Hg²⁺ > Cu²⁺ > H⁺ > Pb²⁺ > Ni²⁺ > Zn²⁺ > Fe²⁺ > Al³⁺ > Mg²⁺ > Na⁺ > K⁺

ANODE — discharge order (most easily discharged FIRST):

I⁻ > Br⁻ > Cl⁻ > OH⁻ > SO₄²⁻ > NO₃⁻

💡 EXAM TIP

CRITICAL ANODE RULE for JAMB: DILUTE halide solution: OH⁻ ions (from water) discharged preferentially → OXYGEN gas produced. CONCENTRATED halide solution: Cl⁻ (or Br⁻, I⁻) discharged preferentially → HALOGEN gas produced. Example: Dilute NaCl → O₂ at anode. Concentrated NaCl (brine) → Cl₂ at anode. This concentration effect is tested very frequently in JAMB!



11.3  Products of Electrolysis — Common Electrolytes

ELECTROLYTE

CATHODE PRODUCT

ANODE PRODUCT

Dilute H₂SO₄

Cathode: 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂↑

Anode: 2H₂O → O₂↑ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻

Dilute NaCl

Cathode: 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂↑

Anode: 2H₂O → O₂↑ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻

Conc. NaCl (brine)

Cathode: 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂↑

Anode: 2Cl⁻ → Cl₂↑ + 2e⁻

CuSO₄(aq), Pt electrodes

Cathode: Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu↓

Anode: 2H₂O → O₂↑ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻

CuSO₄(aq), Cu electrodes

Cathode: Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu↓ (grows)

Anode: Cu → Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ (dissolves)

Molten NaCl

Cathode: Na⁺ + e⁻ → Na (liquid)

Anode: 2Cl⁻ → Cl₂↑ + 2e⁻



11.4  Faraday's Laws of Electrolysis

FIRST LAW: Mass deposited ∝ Quantity of charge passed

Charge Q = I × t   (Coulombs = Amperes × seconds)

Mass = (M × I × t) ÷ (n × F)

M = molar mass (g/mol) | I = current (A) | t = time (s)

n = electrons transferred per ion | F = 96,500 C/mol (Faraday constant)

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

What mass of copper is deposited from CuSO₄ solution by a current of 2 A flowing for 30 minutes? [Cu = 63.5, n = 2] Q = I × t = 2 × (30 × 60) = 2 × 1800 = 3600 C Mass = (63.5 × 3600) ÷ (2 × 96500) = 228600 ÷ 193000 = 1.18 g

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

A current of 0.5 A flows through molten AlCl₃ for 1 hour. Find mass of Al deposited. [Al = 27, n = 3] Q = 0.5 × 3600 = 1800 C Mass = (27 × 1800) ÷ (3 × 96500) = 48600 ÷ 289500 = 0.168 g

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

What current deposits 1.975 g of Cu in 50 minutes? [Cu = 63.5, n = 2] Rearrange: I = (Mass × n × F) ÷ (M × t) = (1.975 × 2 × 96500) ÷ (63.5 × 3000) = 381275 ÷ 190500 = 2.0 A



11.5  Industrial Applications of Electrolysis

APPLICATION

DETAILS

CHLOR-ALKALI (Brine)

Electrolysis of concentrated NaCl solution. CATHODE: 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂ gas ANODE: 2Cl⁻ → Cl₂ gas + 2e⁻ SOLUTION: NaOH remains Products used: Cl₂ → PVC, bleach (NaOCl), disinfectants, HCl H₂ → fuel, margarine production, NH₃ (Haber) NaOH → soap, paper, food processing, Al extraction

ALUMINIUM — Hall-Héroult

Ore: BAUXITE purified to Al₂O₃ (Bayer process). Electrolyte: Al₂O₃ dissolved in molten CRYOLITE (Na₃AlF₆). WHY CRYOLITE? Lowers melting point from 2050°C to 900°C — saves enormous energy! CATHODE: Al³⁺ + 3e⁻ → Al (liquid metal, sinks to bottom) ANODE (carbon): 2O²⁻ → O₂ + 4e⁻ — O₂ burns the carbon anode: C + O₂ → CO₂ Anodes gradually consumed and must be REPLACED regularly.

COPPER REFINING

Impure Cu anode (dissolves) → pure Cu cathode (grows). Electrolyte: CuSO₄ solution. Cu transfers from impure → pure electrode. Impurities (Ag, Au, Pt) do NOT dissolve — fall as 'anode sludge' (valuable by-products!). Result: 99.99% pure Cu for electrical wiring.

ELECTROPLATING

Object to plate = CATHODE. Plating metal = ANODE (or inert anode if metal added to solution). Salt of plating metal = electrolyte. Silver cutlery: Ag anode, object cathode, AgNO₃ solution. Tin cans: Fe object cathode, Sn anode, SnSO₄ solution. Chromium on cars: inert anode, Cr₂(SO₄)₃ solution.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

About 50 million tonnes of aluminium are produced globally each year by the Hall-Héroult process. This requires an enormous amount of electricity — about 14 kWh per kg of aluminium produced. This is why aluminium smelters are always built near cheap electricity sources like hydropower dams. Recycling aluminium uses only 5% of the energy needed to produce it from ore — this is why recycling aluminium cans is so environmentally important.

PRINCIPLES: Electrolysis = decomposition by electric current (non-spontaneous).

Cathode (-): reduction, cations discharged (less reactive first)

Anode (+): oxidation, anions discharged

DISCHARGE ORDER (cathode): Cu2+ > Ag+ > H+ > Pb2+ > Zn2+ > Fe2+ > Al3+

(anode): Cl- > OH- > SO42- > NO3- (dilute: OH- discharged; concentrated halide: halide discharged)

FARADAY'S LAWS:

1st: Mass deposited proportional to charge passed (Q = I x t)

Mass = (M x I x t) / (n x 96500) where n = electrons transferred

💡 EXAM TIP:  INDUSTRIAL: Brine -> Cl2 (anode), H2 (cathode), NaOH (solution). Aluminium: Hall-Heroult (molten Al2O3 in cryolite). Copper refining: impure Cu anode, pure Cu cathode.

12

🔥 ENERGY CHANGES IN REACTIONS

Exo/Endothermic · Enthalpy · Hess's Law · Bond Energies · Born-Haber



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Think of energy in chemistry like a ball on a hillside. An EXOTHERMIC reaction is like a ball rolling DOWNHILL — it releases energy naturally, products are lower in energy than reactants (stable). An ENDOTHERMIC reaction is like PUSHING a ball UPHILL — you must put energy in, products are higher in energy (less stable). The ACTIVATION ENERGY (Ea) is the height of any hump the ball must go over first.



ENERGY PROFILE — Exothermic vs Endothermic

  EXOTHERMIC (ΔH < 0)          ENDOTHERMIC (ΔH > 0)

  Products LOWER energy         Products HIGHER energy



  Energy▲                       Energy▲

        │      ╭──╮                   │         ╭──╮

        │     /    \  Ea              │        /    \  Ea

   R ───┼────╯      \                 │   ────╯      \────── P

        │            \── P            │  R            (high)

        │          ΔH < 0             │       ΔH > 0

        └──────────────────►          └─────────────────────►

           Reaction progress              Reaction progress



  Catalyst LOWERS the Ea hill (──) but does NOT change R or P heights.

  Catalyst speeds up BOTH forward and reverse reactions equally.



12.1  Exothermic vs Endothermic Reactions

TYPE

DESCRIPTION & EVERYDAY EXAMPLES

EXOTHERMIC (ΔH < 0, negative)

Heat is RELEASED to surroundings. Surroundings temperature RISES (thermometer goes up). Products have LOWER energy than reactants. Bond FORMING releases more energy than bond BREAKING absorbs. EXAMPLES: Combustion of fuel, neutralisation (strong acid + strong base = −57 kJ/mol), respiration, rusting of iron, hand warmers, explosives.

ENDOTHERMIC (ΔH > 0, positive)

Heat is ABSORBED from surroundings. Surroundings temperature FALLS (thermometer goes down). Products have HIGHER energy than reactants. Bond BREAKING absorbs more energy than bond FORMING releases. EXAMPLES: Thermal decomposition (CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂), dissolving NH₄NO₃ in water (cold packs!), photosynthesis, electrolysis.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Cold packs for sports injuries use an endothermic reaction! When you squeeze the pack, it breaks an inner pouch mixing solid ammonium nitrate (NH₄NO₃) with water. The dissolving is strongly endothermic — it absorbs heat from your skin and the surrounding environment, making the pack feel ice-cold within seconds. Temperature can drop to about 2°C. This is the same compound used in some explosives when mixed with fuel oil!



12.2  Standard Enthalpy Changes — All Types

TYPE

DEFINITION & KEY NOTES

ΔHf° (Formation)

Enthalpy when 1 mol compound forms from ELEMENTS in standard states (25°C, 1 atm). ΔHf° of any pure element in its standard state = 0 (by definition). E.g., ΔHf°(H₂O, l) = −286 kJ/mol

ΔHc° (Combustion)

Enthalpy when 1 mol substance undergoes COMPLETE COMBUSTION in excess O₂ at standard conditions. ALWAYS EXOTHERMIC (negative) for organic compounds. Products must be CO₂(g) and H₂O(l) for standard combustion.

ΔH neutralisation

Enthalpy when acid + base → 1 mol H₂O. Strong acid + strong base = −57 kJ/mol (just H⁺ + OH⁻ → H₂O). Weak acid/base involved → less negative (ionisation energy also needed).

ΔH lattice energy

Energy to break 1 mol ionic lattice into isolated gaseous ions. ALWAYS endothermic. Larger ionic charges + smaller ions = more exothermic lattice formation (stronger attraction). MgO lattice energy (−3791 kJ/mol) gg NaCl (−787 kJ/mol) because Mg²⁺/O²⁻ charges are double.

ΔH atomisation

1 mol element → 1 mol isolated gaseous atoms. ALWAYS endothermic. E.g., ½Cl₂(g) → Cl(g)  ΔH = +121 kJ/mol

ΔH hydration

1 mol gaseous ions → hydrated ions in water. Always exothermic. Drives dissolution when large enough to overcome lattice energy.



12.3  Hess's Law — The Shortcut Principle

HESS'S LAW

The total enthalpy change for a reaction is the SAME regardless of the route taken.

(Enthalpy is a STATE FUNCTION — depends only on start and end, NOT on the path)



Using FORMATION enthalpies:  ΔH°rxn = ΣΔHf°(products) − ΣΔHf°(reactants)

Using COMBUSTION enthalpies:  ΔH°rxn = ΣΔHc°(reactants) − ΣΔHc°(products)  ← NOTE REVERSAL!

Using BOND ENERGIES:  ΔH = Σ(bonds BROKEN, reactants) − Σ(bonds FORMED, products)

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Find ΔH for C + 2H₂ → CH₄ using combustion enthalpies: ΔHc(C) = −393, ΔHc(H₂) = −286, ΔHc(CH₄) = −890 kJ/mol ΔHf(CH₄) = [(−393) + 2(−286)] − [(−890)] = −965 + 890 = −75 kJ/mol

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Bond energy calculation for H₂(g) + Cl₂(g) → 2HCl(g): Bond energies: H–H = 436, Cl–Cl = 242, H–Cl = 431 kJ/mol ΔH = (436 + 242) − 2(431) = 678 − 862 = −184 kJ/mol (exothermic ✓)

💡 EXAM TIP

BOND ENERGY RULE: BREAKING bonds ALWAYS costs energy (endothermic). FORMING bonds ALWAYS releases energy (exothermic). Overall reaction is exothermic if total energy RELEASED (forming) > energy ABSORBED (breaking). This is why combustion of fuels is so exothermic — very strong C=O bonds in CO₂ and O–H bonds in H₂O are formed.



12.4  Born-Haber Cycle (for Ionic Compounds)

The Born-Haber cycle applies Hess's Law to calculate LATTICE ENERGY, which cannot be measured directly. It traces the energy path from elements → ionic compound via a series of measurable steps.

For NaCl, the cycle includes: ΔHf° = atomisation(Na) + 1st IE(Na) + atomisation(½Cl₂) + 1st EA(Cl) + lattice energy(NaCl)

STEP

PROCESS & SIGN

Atomisation of Na

Na(s) → Na(g)    endothermic (sublimation energy)

1st Ionisation Energy of Na

Na(g) → Na⁺(g) + e⁻    endothermic

Atomisation of Cl

½Cl₂(g) → Cl(g)    endothermic (bond dissociation)

1st Electron Affinity of Cl

Cl(g) + e⁻ → Cl⁻(g)    exothermic

Lattice energy of NaCl

Na⁺(g) + Cl⁻(g) → NaCl(s)    exothermic (very large)

By Hess's Law

ΔHf° = sum of all above steps = −411 kJ/mol

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The lattice energy of magnesium oxide (MgO) is about −3791 kJ/mol — nearly 5 times larger than NaCl's (−787 kJ/mol). This is because Mg²⁺ has twice the charge of Na⁺, and O²⁻ has twice the charge of Cl⁻. By Coulomb's Law, doubling both charges quadruples the attraction. This is why MgO melts at 2852°C (used in furnace linings!) while NaCl melts at only 801°C.

EXOTHERMIC: dH < 0. Heat released to surroundings. Temperature RISES. Examples: combustion, neutralization, respiration.

ENDOTHERMIC: dH > 0. Heat absorbed from surroundings. Temperature FALLS. Examples: thermal decomposition, dissolving NH4Cl, photosynthesis.

ENTHALPY TYPES:

dHf: enthalpy of formation (1 mol from elements in standard states)

dHc: enthalpy of combustion (1 mol burns in excess O2)

dH neutralization: ~-57 kJ/mol for strong acid + strong base

dH lattice: ionic crystal -> gaseous ions

HESS'S LAW: Total enthalpy change = same regardless of route taken (energy conservation). Used to calculate dH for unmeasurable reactions.

💡 EXAM TIP:  BORN-HABER CYCLE calculates lattice energies of ionic compounds. Steps: atomization + ionization energy + electron affinity + lattice energy. dHf = sum of all steps.

The Hess's Law calculation: ΔH rxn = ΣΔHf(products) − ΣΔHf(reactants) is valid because

A.  enthalpy is a state function

C.  activation energy is fixed

D.  reactions are always exothermic







  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Moles H2=12/2=6. 2 mol = -571 kJ. 6 mol = 6/2 x 571 = -1713 kJ.



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DHf0 = enthalpy change forming 1 mole compound from elements in STANDARD STATES at 298K.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  HESS'S LAW: total enthalpy change same regardless of route. DH is a state function.



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  EXOTHERMIC: releases heat. DH < 0 (negative). Products lower enthalpy than reactants.



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  LATTICE ENERGY: energy released when gaseous ions form 1 mole of ionic solid. Exothermic.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BOND ENERGY: energy to BREAK 1 mole of bond in gaseous state. Endothermic process.



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DH = Sum(bond energies broken) - Sum(bond energies formed). Breaking=endothermic(+), forming=exothermic(-).



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Standard enthalpy of neutralisation (strong+strong) approximately -57 kJ/mol. H++OH-=H2O.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BORN-HABER CYCLE: Hess's Law for ionic compound formation. Calculates LATTICE ENERGY.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ACTIVATION ENERGY (Ea): MINIMUM ENERGY molecules need to react (overcome transition state energy barrier).



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Moles C=6/12=0.5. Heat=0.5 x (-393)=-196.5 kJ.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ENDOTHERMIC: DH>0. Reactants ABSORB heat. Products have MORE enthalpy than reactants.



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Catalyst DOES NOT change DH. Provides lower Ea pathway. Overall energy difference unchanged.



Question 14

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DH=bonds broken - formed=(436+244)-(2x431)=680-862=-182 kJ.



Question 15

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2SO4 dissolution strongly EXOTHERMIC. Temperature RISES.



Question 16

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  EVAPORATION: absorbs energy to break intermolecular forces. ENDOTHERMIC.



Question 17

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Negative DHsolution = EXOTHERMIC dissolution. Heat released. Temperature RISES.



Question 18

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  ENTROPY (S): measure of DISORDER/RANDOMNESS. Gases high entropy, crystals low.



Question 19

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  SPONTANEOUS: DG < 0. DG=0: equilibrium. DG>0: non-spontaneous.



Question 20

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Decompose: 2NH3=N2+3H2. DH=+92 kJ (reverse). Per mol NH3: +92/2=+46 kJ.



Question 21

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Endothermic dissolution absorbs heat from water. Water COOLS. Temperature FALLS. (Basis of cold packs.)



Question 22

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  q=mcDT: q=heat(J), m=mass(g), c=specific heat(4.18 J/gC for water), DT=temperature change.



Question 23

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CaCO3=CaO+CO2: solid to solid+gas. GAS formation increases entropy greatly.



Question 24

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Negative DHf0: compound has LOWER enthalpy than elements. MORE STABLE. Energy released when formed.



Question 25

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  DHf0=0 for ALL ELEMENTS in their STANDARD STATE. O2(g) is standard state. NaCl, H2O, CO2 are compounds.



Question 26

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  q=100x4.18x6.9=2884 J. Moles H2O=0.05. DH=2.884/0.05=-57.7 kJ/mol.



Question 27

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  EXOTHERMIC: REACTANTS are higher energy than PRODUCTS. Energy released = difference.



Question 28

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Arrhenius: k=Ae(-Ea/RT). Rate depends on TEMPERATURE and ACTIVATION ENERGY.



Question 29

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  After removing first electron: fewer electrons, same nuclear charge = HIGHER Zeff/electron = harder to remove next.



Question 30

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DG=DH-TDS=200-300=-100 kJ. Negative = SPONTANEOUS.



Question 31

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DHf=2(-393)+2(-286)-(-1411)=-786-572+1411=+53 approximately +52 kJ/mol.



Question 32

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  MgO: Mg2+ and O2- (higher charges, smaller ions). Strongest attraction. HIGHEST lattice energy.



Question 33

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Exothermic reaction. Ea(reverse)=Ea(forward)+|DH|=150+80=230 kJ.



Question 34

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DG=DH-TDS=-100,000-(300x50)=-100,000-15,000=-115,000 J. DG<0. SPONTANEOUS.



Question 35

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Born-Haber: (1)atomisation Na, (2)1st IE of Na, (3)atomisation Cl, (4)EA of Cl, (5)lattice energy of NaCl. Total=DHf0.



Question 36

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CRYSTAL LATTICE FORMATION: gaseous ions combine. Strong attractions. Energy RELEASED (exothermic).



Question 37

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ENTHALPY OF ATOMISATION: convert 1 mole element in standard state to gaseous atoms. Na(s) to Na(g).



Question 38

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Weak acid: energy needed to fully dissociate. Less exothermic than strong+strong (-57 kJ/mol).



Question 39

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Standard thermochemical conditions: 25C (298K) and 1 atm. Different from STP (0C and 1 atm) used in gas law calculations.



Question 40

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Calorimeter constant (Ccal): heat absorbed by the calorimeter walls and vessel must be accounted for. q(reaction)=-(qsolution + qcalorimeter). Accurate thermochemistry requires Ccal.



Question 41

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CO2 is already fully oxidised. BURNING CO2 is impossible - it cannot release more energy by combustion. CO2 is the PRODUCT of carbon combustion. DHcomb(CO2)=0 because it doesn't combust.



Question 42

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Hess's Law: the total enthalpy change is the same regardless of the route taken (conservation of energy).



Question 43

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  By definition, ΔHf° of any element in its standard state = 0 kJ/mol.



Question 44

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Energy must be supplied to break bonds. Bond breaking is endothermic (absorbs energy).



Question 45

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Energy is released when bonds form. Bond formation is exothermic.



Question 46

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Standard enthalpy of combustion: heat released when 1 mole of substance completely burns in excess oxygen at standard conditions.



Question 47

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Exothermic reactions release heat to surroundings, so ΔH is negative (products at lower energy than reactants).



Question 48

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  q = mcΔT = 100 × 4.2 × (30−20) = 100 × 4.2 × 10 = 4200J. Wait — 4200J → B.



Question 49

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  ΔHneutralisation ≈ −57 kJ/mol for any strong acid + strong base, as it's always H⁺ + OH⁻ → H2O.



Question 50

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  The Born-Haber cycle is a Hess's Law cycle used to calculate the lattice enthalpy of ionic compounds.



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Activation energy (Ea) is the minimum kinetic energy reactant molecules must have to collide successfully and react.



Question 52

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  MgO has Mg²⁺ and O²⁻ — higher charge and smaller ionic radii compared to NaCl → much higher lattice energy.



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Enthalpy of atomisation: energy to convert 1 mole of an element in its standard state to isolated gaseous atoms.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  A catalyst provides an alternative pathway with lower Ea, increasing rate. The overall ΔH of the reaction is unchanged.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NH4NO3 dissolves endothermically — absorbing heat from surroundings, cooling the solution (used in cold packs).



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Standard enthalpy of solution: the heat change when 1 mole of solute completely dissolves in excess solvent.



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Calorimetry measures heat exchange in chemical reactions using an insulated vessel (calorimeter).



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ΔHf° of CO2 = ΔHcomb° of C (since C + O2 → CO2 is both formation and combustion). = −394 kJ/mol.



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Endothermic reactions absorb energy, so products are at higher energy level than reactants; ΔH is positive.



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Enthalpy of vaporisation: energy needed to convert 1 mole of liquid to gas at constant pressure at its boiling point.



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Combustion releases large amounts of heat — strongly exothermic. The others are endothermic processes.



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  2g H2 = 2/2 = 1 mol; but ΔHf is for 1 mol H2 = −286 kJ; but H2(g) + ½O2(g) → H2O(l). 2g = 1 mol H2; heat = 286 kJ. — Check: 2g/2g/mol = 1mol → 286kJ. Answer should be A.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ΔHrxn = ΣΔHf°(products) − ΣΔHf°(reactants). This is the standard application of Hess's Law.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Bond dissociation energy: the energy needed to break 1 mole of a specific covalent bond in gaseous molecules.



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  A reaction is spontaneous if ΔG = ΔH − TΔS < 0. It can be spontaneous even if endothermic if ΔS is sufficiently positive.



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Enthalpy changes are expressed in kJ/mol (kilojoules per mole of reaction as written).



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Some energy is used to fully ionise the weak acid, leaving less heat for neutralisation. ΔHneut < 57 kJ/mol magnitude.



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Lattice energy (energy released when gaseous ions form a solid lattice) is always negative (exothermic process).



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  q = mcΔT (heat = mass × specific heat capacity × temperature change) applies to heat transfer in solutions and solids.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Positive ΔH means energy is absorbed from surroundings → endothermic reaction.



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Combustion is always exothermic (heat is released), so enthalpy of combustion is always negative.



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Combustion of methane releases ~−891 kJ/mol — far more than phase changes or dissolving.



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Entropy (S) measures disorder or randomness. Reactions that increase disorder have positive ΔS.



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ΔHf° = −286 kJ/mol means 286 kJ is released when 1 mol of H2O(l) forms from H2(g) and ½O2(g) at standard conditions.



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  In an endothermic energy profile, the products are at a higher energy level than the reactants; ΔH is positive.



Question 76

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Bond energy = energy needed to break 1 mole of that bond in the gaseous state. 436 kJ/mol is needed to break H-H.



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Exothermic reactions release heat to the surroundings (the solution in the calorimeter), causing a temperature rise.



Question 78

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Enthalpy is a state function — its change depends only on initial and final states, not the pathway (basis of Hess's Law).





13

⏱ RATES OF REACTION

Collision Theory · Factors · Activation Energy · Rate Law · Arrhenius



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Imagine a crowded Oshodi bus park. For two buses to COLLIDE and merge their passengers (react), they must: (1) actually MEET each other (collision), (2) be moving fast ENOUGH to merge (activation energy), and (3) be facing the right direction (correct orientation). More buses in the park (higher concentration), faster buses (higher temperature), and smaller park (higher pressure) all increase the chance of a successful merge per minute (higher reaction rate).



13.1  Collision Theory in Detail

For a chemical reaction to occur between two species, they must: (1) COLLIDE — come close enough to interact. (2) Collide with energy ≥ ACTIVATION ENERGY (Ea). (3) Collide with CORRECT ORIENTATION — reactive parts facing each other.

ACTIVATION ENERGY (Ea) = the minimum energy needed to start breaking bonds in the reactants, allowing new bonds to form. Even exothermic reactions need Ea to get started (like wood needs a match to start burning, even though burning releases far more energy).

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The activation energy concept explains why wood does not spontaneously catch fire even though combustion is highly exothermic! The C–H bonds in wood need energy to start breaking before the more stable C=O bonds in CO₂ can form and release large amounts of energy. Once started, the energy released is enough to activate more wood molecules — the fire sustains itself. In petrol engines, the spark plug provides the activation energy to ignite the fuel-air mixture.



13.2  Factors Affecting Rate of Reaction

FACTOR

MECHANISM & DETAILS

CONCENTRATION (solutions)

More molecules per unit volume → more frequent collisions → more chances for successful collisions → faster rate. Doubling concentration may double rate (1st order), quadruple it (2nd order), or have no effect (0th order) — depends on the reaction mechanism, NOT the equation coefficients.

PRESSURE (gases)

Higher pressure → same number of gas molecules squeezed into smaller volume → higher concentration → same effect as higher concentration for solutions. Halving volume doubles pressure AND doubles concentration.

TEMPERATURE

MAIN EFFECT: Raises the average kinetic energy. More molecules have energy ≥ Ea (Boltzmann distribution shifts right). Even a small temperature rise dramatically increases the fraction above Ea. SECONDARY EFFECT: Molecules move faster → more frequent collisions. RULE OF THUMB: ~10°C rise ≈ DOUBLES the rate for most reactions.

SURFACE AREA (solid reactants)

Grinding solid into powder dramatically increases surface area exposed to the other reactant. Same mass, many more collision sites. E.g., iron powder dissolves in HCl in seconds; iron nail takes minutes. Explosion risk: fine flour dust, coal dust, lycopodium powder all ignite explosively when dispersed in air.

CATALYST

Provides an ALTERNATIVE REACTION PATHWAY with LOWER ACTIVATION ENERGY. More molecules can now exceed the lower Ea barrier → much faster rate. The catalyst is NOT consumed (comes out unchanged). Does NOT change: ΔH, Kc, equilibrium position, or the final equilibrium yield. Just helps equilibrium be reached FASTER.

LIGHT (photochemical)

UV/visible light photons provide energy to INITIATE reactions. E.g., Cl₂ + CH₄: UV breaks Cl–Cl bond → Cl• radicals → chain reaction. Photosynthesis: visible light drives endothermic CO₂ fixation. Photographic film: AgBr → Ag (silver image forms in light).



13.3  Rate Law and Orders of Reaction

Rate = k[A]^m [B]^n

k = rate constant (only changes with temperature or catalyst)

m = order w.r.t. A  |  n = order w.r.t. B  |  Overall order = m + n

⚠ Orders are determined EXPERIMENTALLY — NEVER from balanced equation!

ORDER

EFFECT & GRAPH SHAPE

0th order w.r.t. A

Rate does NOT change when [A] changes. Rate = k (constant). Graph of Rate vs [A] = horizontal line. Graph of [A] vs time = straight line (constant decrease).

1st order w.r.t. A

Rate ∝ [A]. Double [A] → double rate. Graph of Rate vs [A] = straight line through origin. Graph of [A] vs time = exponential decay curve. Half-life is CONSTANT (used in radioactive decay).

2nd order w.r.t. A

Rate ∝ [A]². Double [A] → QUADRUPLE rate. Graph of Rate vs [A] = upward curve (parabola). Graph of Rate vs [A]² = straight line.



13.4  Arrhenius Equation

k = A × e^(−Ea/RT)

Taking natural log: ln k = ln A − Ea/RT

Graph of ln k vs 1/T: straight line, gradient = −Ea/R, intercept = ln A

As temperature increases, Ea/RT decreases, so e^(−Ea/RT) increases → k increases → faster rate. This gives the exponential dependence of rate on temperature.

💡 EXAM TIP

CATALYST vs TEMPERATURE comparison (JAMB favourite): CATALYST: Provides lower-energy pathway (lowers Ea). Does NOT change ΔH, Kc, or equilibrium yield. Increases rate of BOTH forward AND reverse reactions equally. TEMPERATURE: Gives more molecules sufficient KE to overcome the SAME Ea barrier. For EXOTHERMIC reactions, raising T actually REDUCES equilibrium yield (by Le Chatelier), even though it speeds up the reaction. Both increase reaction rate, but by completely different mechanisms.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Biological enzymes are nature's catalysts — they lower activation energies so dramatically that reactions that would take thousands of years at room temperature happen in milliseconds in your body! The enzyme carbonic anhydrase converts CO₂ + H₂O ⇌ H₂CO₃ about 10 million times per second — one of the fastest enzyme-catalysed reactions known. Without it, removing CO₂ from your blood would be 10 million times slower and you would die instantly.

FACTORS AFFECTING RATE:

1. Concentration: more particles, more collisions, faster rate

2. Temperature: more particles exceed Ea (~doubles per 10°C rise)

3. Surface area: powders react faster than lumps

4. Catalyst: lowers Ea without being consumed

5. Pressure (gases only): increases effective concentration

RATE EXPRESSION: Rate = k[A]^m[B]^n

k = rate constant (T-dependent) | m, n = orders (found experimentally, NOT from equation)

Overall order = m + n

ACTIVATION ENERGY (Ea): Minimum energy for successful collision.

Arrhenius: k = Ae^(-Ea/RT) | Arrhenius plot: ln k vs 1/T, slope = -Ea/R

💡 EXAM TIP:  Catalysts provide alternative pathway with LOWER Ea. They do NOT change dH or equilibrium position — only increase rate of reaching equilibrium.

B.  is the highest energy point on the reaction coordinate

C.  is the product

D.  is the reactant

  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Higher T: more molecules have Ea or more. More SUCCESSFUL COLLISIONS. Faster rate.



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CATALYST: alternative pathway with LOWER ACTIVATION ENERGY. More molecules react at given T.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate = change in concentration/time = mol dm-3 s-1.



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate doubles per 10C. 20 to 50 = 3 steps. 2^3=8x.



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  More surface area = more exposed particles = more collisions = FASTER RATE.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Catalyst: LOWER HUMP. Same reactant and product energy levels.



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Order is EXPERIMENTALLY determined. NOT from stoichiometry.



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate proportional to [B]2. [B] to 3[B]: rate increases 3^2=9x.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  FIRST-ORDER: t1/2=0.693/k. CONSTANT regardless of initial concentration.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  COLLISION THEORY: need (1) energy >= Ea AND (2) correct orientation. Both = effective collision.



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Increasing P = decreasing V = HIGHER CONCENTRATION of gas = more collisions = faster rate.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Units of k depend on order. Zero order: mol dm-3 s-1. First: s-1. Second: dm3 mol-1 s-1.



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Mg POWDER: greater surface area. More Mg exposed to HCl. Faster reaction.



Question 14

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate measured: (1) volume CO2 per unit time, OR (2) mass loss per unit time (CO2 escapes).



Question 15

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Second order: rate=k[A]2. k=rate/[A]2 = dm3 mol-1 s-1.



Question 16

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ENZYMES: biological protein catalysts. Highly SPECIFIC. Active site binds substrate. pH and T sensitive.



Question 17

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate=2 x (0.5)^2=2 x 0.25=0.5 mol dm-3 s-1.



Question 18

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Zero order: rate=k (constant). INDEPENDENT of [A]. Doubling [A] = NO CHANGE.



Question 19

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  MnO2 is CATALYST. Lowers Ea, speeds decomposition, NOT consumed. Same mass at end.



Question 20

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  HETEROGENEOUS CATALYST: different phase from reactants. MnO2 (solid), H2O2 (liquid/aqueous).



Question 21

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  RATE-DETERMINING STEP: SLOWEST step in multi-step mechanism. Bottleneck.



Question 22

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  RADICAL CHAIN. Initiation: UV=2Cl*. Propagation: Cl*+H2=HCl+H*; H*+Cl2=HCl+Cl*.



Question 23

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  60 min=3 half-lives. [A]=0.8 x (1/2)^3=0.8/8=0.1 mol/dm3.



Question 24

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  TEMPERATURE: affects rate (always increases when raised) AND equilibrium (shifts endothermic direction).



Question 25

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Catalyst LOWERS Ea (moves threshold line left). More molecules exceed Ea. Distribution unchanged.



Question 26

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Overall order = sum of exponents = 0+1 = 1 (FIRST ORDER).



Question 27

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  POWDERED CaCO3: enormously greater surface area than chips. Much faster rate.



Question 28

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  HETEROGENEOUS: reactants ADSORB onto catalyst surface. Bonds weakened. React. Products DESORB.



Question 29

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  First order: rate=k[A]. k=rate/[A]=(mol dm-3 s-1)/(mol dm-3)=s-1.



Question 30

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  THIOSULPHATE CLOCK: S precipitates (colloidal). Flask over cross on paper. Time until cross DISAPPEARS.



Question 31

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  A=PRE-EXPONENTIAL FACTOR: related to collision frequency and probability of correct orientation.



Question 32

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate doubles per 10C. 25 to 55 = 3 steps of 10C. 2^3=8x.



Question 33

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Slowest: LOW T (fewer molecules with Ea), LOW CONCENTRATION (fewer collisions), NO CATALYST.



Question 34

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Area under entire M-B distribution = TOTAL NUMBER OF MOLECULES.



Question 35

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate doubles when [A] doubles = rate proportional to [A]^1 = FIRST ORDER.



Question 36

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  CATALYST: (1)NOT consumed (2)Does NOT change DH (3)Provides LOWER Ea pathway.



Question 37

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  DYNAMIC EQUILIBRIUM: rate of forward = rate of reverse. Both continue at equal rates.



Question 38

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SURFACE AREA key for heterogeneous catalysts. More surface = more adsorption sites.



Question 39

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  DECREASING CONCENTRATION reduces collisions per unit time. SLOWER rate.



Question 40

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  For SOLIDS and LIQUIDS: molecular spacing minimal. Pressure doesn't change concentration significantly.



Question 41

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Catalyst speeds BOTH forward and reverse equally. Equilibrium reached FASTER. Position and Kc UNCHANGED.



Question 42

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate law exponents are EXPERIMENTALLY determined. Do not equal stoichiometric coefficients generally.



Question 43

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ENZYME CATALYSIS: enzyme and substrate both in aqueous solution = HOMOGENEOUS catalysis. Active site specificity makes it unique.



Question 44

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Higher temperature gives molecules more kinetic energy, increasing collision frequency and ensuring more collisions exceed Ea.



Question 45

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Catalysts work by providing an alternative reaction mechanism with lower activation energy, increasing rate without being consumed.



Question 46

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate = change in concentration of reactant or product per unit time (mol/L/s or mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹).



Question 47

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Colour does not affect reaction rate. Temperature, concentration, catalyst, surface area, and light (for photochemical reactions) do.



Question 48

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Collision theory: reactant molecules must collide with energy ≥ Ea AND with the correct orientation for bonds to break and form.



Question 49

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  More surface area = more exposed reactive sites = more frequent collisions = faster reaction rate.



Question 50

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Overall order = sum of powers in rate expression = 1 + 2 = 3 (third order).



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  For first-order reactions, t½ = 0.693/k — it is constant and independent of initial concentration.



Question 52

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Second-order rate constant has units of mol⁻¹ dm³ s⁻¹ (or L mol⁻¹ s⁻¹).



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Enzymes are proteins that catalyse biological reactions. They are highly specific — each enzyme acts on a specific substrate.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Higher concentration of H⁺ ions means more frequent collisions with CaCO3 surface, increasing reaction rate.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Reaction order must be determined experimentally (from rate data). It cannot be deduced from the balanced equation alone.



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Zero-order reaction: Rate = k. Rate is independent of reactant concentration.



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  NO (gas) catalyses SO2 oxidation in the same gas phase — homogeneous catalyst. MnO2, Fe, V2O5 are heterogeneous (different phase from reactants).



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Arrhenius equation: k = Ae^(−Ea/RT). It shows rate constant k increases exponentially with temperature.



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Photochemical reactions (e.g. H2 + Cl2 with UV light) are initiated when photons provide energy to form reactive radicals.



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate ∝ [NO2]ⁿ; doubling [NO2] quadruples rate: 2ⁿ = 4; n = 2. Second order with respect to NO2.



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  A reaction mechanism describes the elementary steps (bond breaking and forming) by which reactants convert to products.



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  The rate-determining step is the slowest elementary step in a reaction mechanism. It controls the overall rate.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  MnO2 is a heterogeneous catalyst for H2O2 decomposition: 2H2O2 → 2H2O + O2↑. It dramatically increases rate.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Surface area is relevant when one reactant is solid. Smaller particles have more surface area for more frequent collisions.



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate of reaction = Δ[concentration]/Δtime = mol dm⁻³ s⁻¹.



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Pressure affects gas-phase reactions by increasing concentration of gas molecules, increasing collision frequency.



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  At higher T, the distribution shifts right — more molecules have kinetic energy exceeding the activation energy.



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Inhibitors slow reactions by blocking active sites (enzyme inhibitors) or removing chain-carrying radicals (anti-oxidants).



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Photosynthesis uses light energy to drive the reaction: 6CO2 + 6H2O → C6H12O6 + 6O2. It is photochemical.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Zinc powder has greater surface area than granules, providing more reactive surface — increasing rate.



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Initial rate is measured at the very beginning (t = 0) when concentrations are known and haven't changed significantly.



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Temperature rises 30°C = 3 intervals of 10°C. Rate = 1 × 2³ = 8.



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Reaction rate can be followed by measuring changes in concentration (via pH, conductance, colour, pressure/gas volume, mass).



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  In autocatalysis, a product of the reaction catalyses the same reaction, causing the rate to accelerate as product accumulates.



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Rate ∝ [H2]; halving [H2] halves the rate. Rate = k × (½[H2]) × [I2] = ½ original rate.



Question 76

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Coal dust (powder) has enormously greater surface area than lumps, dramatically increasing combustion rate. Coal dust explosions are a real industrial hazard.



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The transition state is the unstable, highest-energy configuration along the reaction pathway — at the peak of the activation energy barrier.



Question 78

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  A catalyst changes (usually increases) reaction rate and is not permanently consumed. It may be a positive or negative catalyst.



Question 79

✅ ANSWER: C

Question 80

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Iron (with K2O and Al2O3 promoters) is the catalyst in the Haber Process for ammonia synthesis: N2 + 3H2 ⇌ 2NH3.





14

⚖️ CHEMICAL EQUILIBRIUM

Dynamic Equilibrium · Le Chatelier · Kc · Industrial Processes



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Imagine a revolving door between two rooms. People are constantly going IN and OUT at the same time. At EQUILIBRIUM, the number entering = the number leaving, so the total in each room stays CONSTANT. The door is still spinning (it is DYNAMIC — not static!). Chemical equilibrium works the same way: both forward and reverse reactions keep happening, but their rates are equal so concentrations stay constant. Equilibrium is ACTIVE, not frozen.



14.1  Features of Dynamic Equilibrium

Forward reaction and reverse reaction occur SIMULTANEOUSLY at all times

Rate of forward reaction = Rate of reverse reaction at equilibrium

Concentrations of all species remain CONSTANT (not necessarily equal to each other)

Only occurs in a CLOSED SYSTEM where nothing enters or leaves

Macroscopic properties (colour, pressure, concentration) appear unchanged

Observable sign: a colour or property that stops changing despite ongoing reaction

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

One of the most beautiful demonstrations of dynamic equilibrium uses the reaction: 2NO₂(g, brown) ⇌ N₂O₄(g, colourless). When you cool the sealed tube, it turns paler (equilibrium shifts toward N₂O₄). When heated, it turns darker brown (equilibrium shifts toward NO₂). The gas is constantly interconverting even at equilibrium — the colour just reflects which side is favoured at that temperature.



14.2  Le Chatelier's Principle — Complete Guide

LE CHATELIER'S PRINCIPLE

When a system at equilibrium is disturbed (stressed), it shifts in the direction

that OPPOSES the disturbance and re-establishes equilibrium.



CHANGE APPLIED

EFFECT ON EQUILIBRIUM

Increase [REACTANT]

System shifts RIGHT (forward) — uses up added reactant to oppose the increase. More product is formed.

Increase [PRODUCT]

System shifts LEFT (reverse) — uses up added product. More reactant is regenerated.

Remove PRODUCT continuously

Equilibrium continuously shifts RIGHT — keeps making more product. Used industrially to increase yield!

INCREASE PRESSURE

Shifts toward side with FEWER moles of gas (reduces volume to oppose pressure increase). If equal moles on both sides: no shift in position.

DECREASE PRESSURE

Shifts toward side with MORE moles of gas (increases volume).

INCREASE TEMPERATURE

Shifts in ENDOTHERMIC direction (absorbs the added heat to oppose T rise). This INCREASES Kc if reaction is endothermic; DECREASES Kc if reaction is exothermic.

DECREASE TEMPERATURE

Shifts in EXOTHERMIC direction (releases heat to replace what was removed). Yield of exothermic products INCREASES at lower temperature.

Add CATALYST

NO SHIFT in equilibrium position. Only reaches equilibrium FASTER. Kc is UNCHANGED.



14.3  Equilibrium Constant Kc

For: aA + bB ⇌ cC + dD

Kc = [C]^c [D]^d ÷ ([A]^a [B]^b)

Kc > 1: products favoured at equilibrium

Kc < 1: reactants favoured at equilibrium

Kc = 1: roughly equal amounts of reactants and products

Kc changes ONLY when TEMPERATURE changes. NOT with pressure, concentration, or catalyst.

Pure solids and pure liquids are NOT included in the Kc expression.

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Write Kc for: N₂(g) + 3H₂(g) ⇌ 2NH₃(g) Kc = [NH₃]² ÷ ([N₂][H₂]³)

📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

At equilibrium: [H₂] = 0.04, [I₂] = 0.04, [HI] = 0.32 mol/dm³. H₂ + I₂ ⇌ 2HI. Kc = [HI]² ÷ ([H₂][I₂]) = (0.32)² ÷ (0.04 × 0.04) = 0.1024 ÷ 0.0016 = 64



14.4  The Haber Process — Full Equilibrium Analysis

THE HABER PROCESS — Industrial Ammonia Production

  N₂ (from liquid air)  ──────────────────────────────────────┐

                                                               │

  H₂ (from natural gas) ──────────────────────────────────────┤

  CH₄ + H₂O → CO + 3H₂ (steam reforming)                     │

                                                               ▼

                              ┌────────────────────────────────┐

                              │   REACTION VESSEL              │

                              │   N₂ + 3H₂ ⇌ 2NH₃            │

                              │   Temp: 450°C (compromise)     │

                              │   Press: 200–250 atm (high)    │

                              │   Catalyst: Iron (Fe)          │

                              │   ~15% conversion per pass     │

                              └─────────────┬──────────────────┘

                                            │

                                            ▼

                          ┌─────────────────────────────────┐

                          │        CONDENSER                │

                          │   NH₃ liquefied and removed     │

                          │   N₂ + H₂ recycled back ──────►│

                          └────────────────┬────────────────┘

                                           │

                                           ▼

                                    LIQUID NH₃

                               (Fertilisers, HNO₃, Nylon)



N₂(g) + 3H₂(g) ⇌ 2NH₃(g)    ΔH = −92 kJ/mol (EXOTHERMIC)

Conditions: 450°C  |  200–250 atm  |  Iron (Fe) catalyst + K₂O and Al₂O₃ promoters

~15% conversion per pass  |  NH₃ liquefied and removed  |  Gases recycled



CONDITION

REASONING (Le Chatelier + Kinetics)

Why 450°C? (Compromise temperature)

THERMODYNAMICS: Reaction is exothermic → Le Chatelier → LOWER temperature increases yield (shifts right). At 100°C, yield would be ~98% — excellent, but rate is impossibly slow. KINETICS: Low temperature = very slow rate even with catalyst. COMPROMISE: 450°C gives ~15–25% conversion at commercially viable speed. Below 400°C: even with Fe catalyst, rate is too slow for industrial use.

Why 200–250 atm?

Mole count: 4 mol gas (reactants) → 2 mol gas (products). FEWER moles on right. Le Chatelier: high pressure favours fewer moles → shifts RIGHT → more NH₃. High P also increases concentration → faster rate (double benefit). Limited by engineering: very thick, expensive pressure vessels required. 200 atm is economic optimum.

Why Iron (Fe) catalyst?

Speeds up both forward and reverse reactions equally. Equilibrium reached in SECONDS not hours at 450°C. Does NOT change: Kc, ΔH, equilibrium position, or maximum yield. K₂O (potassium oxide) = electronic promoter — increases electron density on Fe. Al₂O₃ = structural promoter — prevents Fe crystals sintering/fusing.

Recycling and continuous removal

Only ~15% of N₂ + H₂ converts to NH₃ per pass. NH₃ is cooled to liquid (boiling point −33°C) and continuously removed. Removing product → Le Chatelier shifts RIGHT → more NH₃ forms. Unreacted N₂ + H₂ recycled back. Overall conversion much higher than 15% in practice.



14.5  Contact Process — Sulfuric Acid (H₂SO₄)

STEP 1: S + O₂ → SO₂  (or: 4FeS₂ + 11O₂ → 2Fe₂O₃ + 8SO₂  roasting pyrite ore)

STEP 2 (KEY EQUILIBRIUM): 2SO₂(g) + O₂(g) ⇌ 2SO₃(g)   ΔH = −197 kJ/mol   [V₂O₅ catalyst, 450°C, ~1 atm, ~98% conversion]

STEP 3: SO₃ absorbed in concentrated H₂SO₄ → H₂S₂O₇ (oleum). Then: H₂S₂O₇ + H₂O → 2H₂SO₄

WHY NOT ABSORB SO₃ DIRECTLY IN WATER? Forms a dense, dangerous acid MIST (fine H₂SO₄ droplets that cannot be separated from gas phase). Absorbing in concentrated H₂SO₄ first is much safer.

💡 EXAM TIP

HABER vs CONTACT — Memorise the key differences: HABER: Product = NH₃ | Catalyst = IRON (Fe) | Pressure = HIGH (200 atm) | Temp = 450°C CONTACT: Product = H₂SO₄ | Catalyst = VANADIUM PENTOXIDE (V₂O₅) | Pressure = LOW (~1 atm) | Temp = 450°C OSTWALD: Product = HNO₃ | Catalyst = PLATINUM/RHODIUM | Temp = 850°C Confusing V₂O₅ with Fe is the most common JAMB error in this topic.

LE CHATELIER'S PRINCIPLE: When equilibrium is disturbed, system shifts to minimise disturbance.

Increase [reactant] -> shifts right | Increase pressure -> fewer gas moles side

Increase temperature -> endothermic direction | Catalyst -> no shift, faster equilibrium

EQUILIBRIUM CONSTANT Kc: For aA + bB <-> cC + dD:

Kc = [C]^c[D]^d / [A]^a[B]^b

Kc > 1 = products favoured | Kc < 1 = reactants favoured

Kc changes ONLY with temperature

HABER PROCESS: N2 + 3H2 <-> 2NH3, dH = -92 kJ/mol

Conditions: 450°C (compromise), 200 atm, Fe catalyst (with K2O/Al2O3 promoters)

4 mol gas -> 2 mol gas: high pressure favours NH3 production

💡 EXAM TIP:  CONTACT PROCESS (H2SO4): 2SO2 + O2 <-> 2SO3, catalyst V2O5 at 450°C. High pressure favours SO3 but is not used due to cost -- 98% conversion achieved at ~1 atm with recycling.
