Q80.  (JAMB 1983)  

For equilibrium: 2HI(g) ⇌ H2(g) + I2(g) at 440°C, Kc = 0.02. This means

A.  very little HI dissociates (reactants favoured)

B.  mostly H2 and I2 form

C.  Kc = 1 so equal amounts

D.  equilibrium lies far right







  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  LE CHATELIER: when equilibrium disturbed, system SHIFTS to OPPOSE the change.



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc=[products]/[reactants]=[C][D]/[A][B]. Each raised to stoichiometric coefficient.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  4 mol gas to 2. Increasing P: shifts toward FEWER moles gas (right). More NH3.



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  LARGE Kc (>1): products predominate. SMALL Kc (<1): reactants predominate.



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc changes ONLY with TEMPERATURE. Others shift equilibrium but NOT Kc.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  EXOTHERMIC forward. Increasing T shifts BACKWARDS. LESS NH3. Smaller Kc.



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Catalyst increases BOTH forward and reverse rates EQUALLY. Faster equilibrium. Position and Kc UNCHANGED.



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Adding Cl2: Le Chatelier shifts LEFT. More PCl5 formed. [PCl5] INCREASES.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dn=moles gaseous products - moles gaseous reactants.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc=[SO3]2/([SO2]2[O2]). Products/reactants, each to stoichiometric power.



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  DYNAMIC EQUILIBRIUM: both reactions continue at equal rates. Concentrations constant. NOT static.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc=[HI]2/([H2][I2])=(2.0)2/(0.5x0.5)=4/0.25=16.



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Exothermic: K DECREASES with T (equilibrium shifts backwards, fewer products).



Question 14

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Contact: lower T favours SO3 (exothermic). High P: more SO3 (fewer moles gas). V2O5 catalyst. Compromise T=450C.



Question 15

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ZIEGLER-NATTA: TiCl4+AlR3. Makes stereoregular polymers.



Question 16

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Water ionisation ENDOTHERMIC. Increasing T shifts right. LARGER Kw. Neutral water pH < 7 at higher T.



Question 17

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Q: calculated using CURRENT concentrations. Q<Kc = forward. Q>Kc = reverse. Q=Kc = equilibrium.



Question 18

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  REMOVING PRODUCT (water): Le Chatelier shifts RIGHT. More ester. Higher yield.



Question 19

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  1 mol gas to 2. Increasing P: shifts LEFT (fewer moles). MORE N2O4, LESS NO2.



Question 20

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  COMPROMISE: lower T = better yield (exothermic) but too slow. Higher T = faster but less yield. 450C balances.



Question 21

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Adding O2 = common reactant. Le Chatelier shifts RIGHT. More SO3 formed.



Question 22

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  HETEROGENEOUS: more than one phase. CaCO3(s)=CaO(s)+CO2(g) has solid and gas.



Question 23

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Pure solids NOT included (activity=1). Only CO2 (gas): Kc=[CO2].



Question 24

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Endothermic forward. Increasing T: more NO2, less N2O4. Kc INCREASES.



Question 25

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc depends ONLY on TEMPERATURE. Concentration, pressure, catalyst changes don't change Kc.



Question 26

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Inert gas at CONSTANT VOLUME: partial pressures of reactants/products UNCHANGED. No shift.



Question 27

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Degree of dissociation: 0 < alpha < 1 for weak acid. Strong acid: alpha=1.



Question 28

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Very SMALL Kc = REACTANTS strongly predominate. Reaction barely proceeds.



Question 29

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  Endothermic: increasing T = more products (higher Kc) AND faster rate. Catalyst also increases rate.



Question 30

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Exothermic equilibrium: increasing T shifts BACKWARD (toward reactants). Kc DECREASES.



Question 31

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Concentration changes SHIFT position but DO NOT change Kc. Kc changes only with T.



Question 32

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2O5 catalyses 2SO2+O2=2SO3. Increases RATE of equilibrium attainment. Does not change Kc.



Question 33

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DH>0 = ENDOTHERMIC. Increasing T: Le Chatelier shifts right. Kc INCREASES.



Question 34

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Ka=[products]/[reactants]=[CH3COO-][H+]/[CH3COOH]. Acid dissociation constant.



Question 35

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NaCl provides Cl- (common ion). Q increases above Ksp. AgCl precipitates. Solubility decreases.



Question 36

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Decreasing P: Le Chatelier shifts toward MORE MOLES GAS (right). More PCl3 and Cl2. Less PCl5.



Question 37

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DYNAMIC EQUILIBRIUM: reactions still occur at equal forward and reverse rates. No NET change. NOT static.



Question 38

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NH3 is LIQUEFIED (BP=-33C) and removed as liquid. Unreacted N2 and H2 recycled. Continuously shifts equilibrium right.



Question 39

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc=[C]2/([A][B])=100. [C]2=100x0.1x0.1=1. [C]=1.0 mol/dm3.



Question 40

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Q=[H2][I2]/[HI]2=(0.02x0.02)/(0.25)=0.0016. Kc=0.02. Q<Kc. System shifts FORWARD.



Question 41

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  LE CHATELIER guides industrial optimisation: P, T, concentration to maximise yield economically.



Question 42

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BUFFER CAPACITY: ability to resist pH change. Higher concentrations of acid+base = MORE reserve = better capacity.



Question 43

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Very LARGE Kc = products overwhelmingly predominate. Reaction goes essentially to COMPLETION. Practically irreversible.



Question 44

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Le Chatelier's Principle: a system at equilibrium will shift in the direction that opposes the imposed change.



Question 45

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Increasing pressure favours the side with fewer moles of gas: left has 4 mol, right has 2 mol → equilibrium shifts right.



Question 46

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc = [products]^stoich / [reactants]^stoich = [C]ᶜ[D]ᵈ / [A]ᵃ[B]ᵇ.



Question 47

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Pure solids and liquids have constant activity = 1 and are excluded from the Kc expression.



Question 48

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Large Kc → products are favoured at equilibrium. Small Kc → reactants favoured.



Question 49

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  A catalyst increases rate of both forward and reverse reactions equally, reaching equilibrium faster but not changing the equilibrium position or Kc.



Question 50

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Increasing temperature for an exothermic reaction: Le Chatelier shifts equilibrium left to absorb heat, Kc decreases.



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kp (and Kc) are constant at constant temperature. Changing pressure or concentration does not change K.



Question 52

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  High pressure increases yield and rate; low temperature increases yield but slows rate. 450°C is a compromise for acceptable rate and yield.



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Adding reactant increases reaction quotient Q below Kc, so equilibrium shifts right to restore Kc.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  If Q < Kc: forward reaction proceeds; Q > Kc: reverse reaction proceeds; Q = Kc: at equilibrium.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Increasing pressure shifts toward fewer moles of gas. PCl5 ⇌ PCl3 + Cl2: left = 1 mol gas, right = 2 mol → shifts left.



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  For reactions where products have fewer moles of gas, increasing pressure increases both yield (shifts equilibrium right) and rate (more frequent collisions).



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Units of Kc = (mol/L)^Δn, where Δn = moles of gaseous products − moles of gaseous reactants.



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Both CaCO3 and CaO are solids (excluded from Kc). Kc = [CO2] only.



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dynamic equilibrium: both forward and reverse reactions continue but at equal rates, so net concentrations don't change.



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kw = [H⁺][OH⁻] = 10⁻⁷ × 10⁻⁷ = 10⁻¹⁴ mol² dm⁻⁶ at 25°C.



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  If Kc increases with temperature, the forward reaction is endothermic (raising T favours endothermic direction).



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Key step: 2SO2 + O2 ⇌ 2SO3 (using V2O5 catalyst). SO3 is then absorbed in H2SO4 to form oleum.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Catalytic oxidation of NH3 over platinum: 4NH3 + 5O2 ⇌ 4NO + 6H2O is the key step in the Ostwald Process.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Adding Cl⁻ (common ion) shifts: NaCl(s) ⇌ Na⁺ + Cl⁻ to the left → more NaCl precipitates.



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc is constant at constant temperature. It does not change with concentration, pressure, or catalyst.



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Δn = 2−3 = −1; Kc = (mol/L)^(−1) = L mol⁻¹.



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc >> 1 means products >> reactants at equilibrium (equilibrium lies to the right).



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Removing product decreases Q below Kc, driving the forward reaction to restore equilibrium (Le Chatelier's principle).



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The Haber process achieves only ~10–15% conversion per pass. Unreacted gases are recycled to improve overall ammonia yield economically.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Homogeneous equilibrium: all reactants and products are in the same phase (e.g. all gases or all in solution).



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Adding a catalyst does NOT shift the equilibrium position — it only speeds up attainment of equilibrium.



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc = [products]^stoich / [reactants]^stoich = [HI]² / ([H2][I2]).



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  The forward reaction is exothermic. Increasing T shifts equilibrium left (Le Chatelier) → less N2O4, more NO2.



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  At equilibrium, both forward and reverse reactions continue, but at equal rates — so net concentrations are constant.



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Reversing a reaction inverts the equilibrium expression: Kc(reverse) = 1/Kc(forward).



Question 76

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SO2 + ½O2 → SO3 is exothermic. Very high T shifts equilibrium left (less SO3), so 450°C balances yield and rate.



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Biological processes require precise pH (e.g. blood pH 7.35–7.45). Buffer systems (HCO3⁻/CO2) maintain this despite metabolic acid production.



Question 78

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Haber process: ~150–300 atm pressure, ~450°C, iron catalyst (+ K2O/Al2O3 promoters).



Question 79

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Partial pressure PA = xA × Ptotal, where xA is the mole fraction of gas A (Dalton's law of partial pressures).



Question 80

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Kc = 0.02 << 1 means the equilibrium lies well to the left — most HI remains undissociated.





15

🧪 NON-METALS

Halogens · Sulfur Allotropes · Carbon Allotropes · Noble Gases



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

The halogens (Group 17) are like 7 electrons in their outer shell — they desperately need just ONE MORE to complete their shell and become stable like noble gases. Fluorine is at the top, closest to its nucleus, pulling hardest for that extra electron (most reactive). Iodine at the bottom has its outer shell so far from the nucleus that the pull is much weaker (least reactive). They get less greedy as you go down!



15.1  The Halogens (Group 17) — Full Coverage

PROPERTY

DETAILS

PHYSICAL PROPERTIES summary

F₂: pale yellow gas | Cl₂: yellow-green gas | Br₂: red-brown liquid (only non-metal liquid at room T) | I₂: dark grey-purple solid (sublimes to violet vapour) All diatomic molecules (X₂). Melting and boiling points INCREASE down group (stronger van der Waals forces as molecules get larger).

REACTIVITY trend

F > Cl > Br > I (decreases down group). WHY: Atomic size increases → outer shell farther from nucleus → nuclear attraction weaker → less able to attract extra electron from another species. F is the most electronegative element (3.98 on Pauling scale).

HALOGEN

PROPERTIES AND TESTS

Fluorine (F₂)

Most reactive element. Never found free in nature. Reacts with almost everything including noble gases (XeF₂, XeF₄)! Always has oxidation number −1 (never positive). Cannot be stored in glass (reacts with SiO₂). Always in plastic containers.

Chlorine (Cl₂)

Yellow-green, toxic gas. Smell of swimming pools. TEST: Turns moist blue litmus RED then BLEACHES it white (HOCl formed). Disinfection: Cl₂ + H₂O ⇌ HCl + HOCl (hypochlorous acid kills bacteria). Bleaching: HOCl oxidises colour-causing chromophores in dyes. Displaces Br⁻ and I⁻ from their salts.

Bromine (Br₂)

Red-brown liquid with pungent smell. Toxic vapour. TEST for alkenes: orange bromine water decolourised → colourless. Displaces I⁻ only (not Cl⁻). Cannot displace Cl⁻ from NaCl. Brought to starch paper: no colour change (unlike I₂).

Iodine (I₂)

Dark grey/purple solid. Sublime to violet vapour. TEST: BLUE-BLACK colour with starch (iodide-starch complex). Least reactive halogen. Antiseptic (2% I₂ in KI solution). Required by thyroid gland (iodised salt in Nigeria prevents goitre).



15.2  Halogen Displacement Reactions

A more reactive halogen displaces a less reactive halide ion from solution. This directly demonstrates the reactivity order.

REACTION

OBSERVATION & EXPLANATION

Cl₂ + 2KBr(aq)

→ 2KCl(aq) + Br₂ Solution turns ORANGE/BROWN (Br₂ produced) Cl₂ is MORE reactive than Br₂ → displaces Br⁻

Cl₂ + 2KI(aq)

→ 2KCl(aq) + I₂ Solution turns DARK BROWN; blue-black with starch Cl₂ displaces I⁻ (Cl₂ more reactive than I₂)

Br₂ + 2KI(aq)

→ 2KBr(aq) + I₂ Solution turns dark brown; starch turns blue-black Br₂ can displace I⁻ but NOT Cl⁻

I₂ + KCl(aq)

NO REACTION (I₂ less reactive than Cl₂)

I₂ + KBr(aq)

NO REACTION (I₂ less reactive than Br₂)

Chlorine Chemistry in Detail

BLEACHING ACTION: Cl₂ + H₂O ⇌ HCl + HOCl. Hypochlorous acid (HOCl) is a powerful oxidising agent. It oxidises and destroys the chromophore groups in dye molecules, removing colour. Bleaching is by CHEMICAL OXIDATION, not a physical process.

PREPARATION of Cl₂ in lab: MnO₂ + 4HCl(conc) → MnCl₂ + Cl₂↑ + 2H₂O (heat)

PREPARATION of HCl: NaCl(s) + H₂SO₄(conc) → NaHSO₄ + HCl↑ (gentle heat)

DISPROPORTIONATION in cold NaOH: Cl₂ + 2NaOH → NaCl + NaOCl + H₂O. Cl₂ is simultaneously oxidised (+0 → +1 in NaOCl) AND reduced (+0 → −1 in NaCl). This is DISPROPORTIONATION.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Iodine is essential for human health — your thyroid gland uses it to make the hormones thyroxine (T4) and triiodothyronine (T3) that regulate metabolism, growth, and brain development. Iodine deficiency is still common in landlocked parts of northern Nigeria far from the sea. The Nigerian government mandated iodisation of all table salt to combat this — about 15–20 mg of potassium iodide (KI) per kg of salt. Without this programme, children develop goitre (swollen thyroid) and intellectual disabilities.



15.3  Sulfur — Allotropes and Chemistry

CONCEPT/TYPE

DESCRIPTION

ALLOTROPY definition

Existence of an element in TWO OR MORE different physical forms in the SAME physical state, which differ in physical properties but give IDENTICAL chemical reactions and products.

Rhombic sulfur (α-S)

Stable BELOW 96°C. Octahedral yellow crystals. Most stable form at room temperature. Density 2.07 g/cm³. Made of S₈ ring molecules.

Monoclinic sulfur (β-S)

Stable 96–119°C. Needle-shaped amber crystals. Less dense (1.96 g/cm³). Also made of S₈ ring molecules. Converts to rhombic below 96°C (transition temperature).

CONCENTRATED H₂SO₄ (sulphuric acid) — triple role:

STRONG DIPROTIC ACID: H₂SO₄ → H⁺ + HSO₄⁻ → 2H⁺ + SO₄²⁻

DEHYDRATING AGENT: C₁₂H₂₂O₁₁ (sugar) + conc H₂SO₄ → 12C + 11H₂O (black char forms). Also: removes H₂O from alcohols to give alkenes.

OXIDISING AGENT (hot, conc only): Cu + 2H₂SO₄(hot conc) → CuSO₄ + SO₂↑ + 2H₂O



15.4  Carbon Allotropes — Diamond, Graphite, Fullerene

CARBON ALLOTROPES — Structure Comparison

  DIAMOND                      GRAPHITE

  Each C bonds to 4 others     Each C bonds to 3 others

  (tetrahedral, sp³)           (hexagonal layers, sp²)



     C ─── C ─── C               C ═ C ═ C

     │     │     │              //   \   //

     C ─── C ─── C           C       C

     │     │     │              \\   //\\

     C ─── C ─── C               C ═ C ═ C

                               ─────────────

                               (weak van der Waals

                                between layers)



  ALL 4 e⁻ in bonds          3 in bonds + 1 DELOCALISED

  → NO free electrons        → FREE electrons → CONDUCTS

  → NON-CONDUCTOR            → SOFT (layers slide)

  → HARDEST substance        → LUBRICANT, ELECTRODE

  → Diamond tip in drills    → Pencil 'lead', batteries



ALLOTROPE

STRUCTURE, PROPERTIES & USES

DIAMOND

sp³ hybridisation. Each C bonded to 4 others (perfect tetrahedral network). All valence electrons in covalent bonds → NO free electrons → ELECTRICAL INSULATOR. Hardest natural substance (10 Mohs). Very high mp (3550°C). Transparent. High refractive index (sparkles). USES: Cutting tools, drill bits, jewellery, heat sinks in electronics.

GRAPHITE

sp² hybridisation. Each C bonded to 3 others in flat hexagonal sheets. ONE delocalised electron per C → FREE ELECTRONS between layers → GOOD CONDUCTOR. Layers held by weak van der Waals forces → layers SLIDE over each other → SOFT, SLIPPERY. Black, opaque, grey streak. USES: Electrodes (cells, electrolysis), lubricant, pencil 'lead', lithium-ion battery anodes.

FULLERENE (C₆₀, 'Buckyball')

60 C atoms in spherical cage (12 pentagons + 20 hexagons = football pattern). Discovered 1985, Nobel Prize 1996. Semiconductor. Soluble in organic solvents. RESEARCH: Drug delivery (hollow cage), superconductors, nanotechnology.

CARBON NANOTUBES

Graphene sheet rolled into seamless cylinder. Strength: 100× stronger than steel at 1/6th the weight. Electrical: can be metallic conductor or semiconductor. RESEARCH: Reinforcing composites, nanoscale electronics, medical sensors.

💡 EXAM TIP

DIAMOND vs GRAPHITE CONDUCTIVITY: DIAMOND: sp³ → ALL 4 valence electrons in covalent bonds → NO free electrons → NON-CONDUCTOR. GRAPHITE: sp² → 3 electrons in bonds + 1 DELOCALISED → FREE electrons → CONDUCTOR. This is one of the most tested JAMB comparisons. Both are pure carbon but completely different structures and properties.



15.5  Noble Gases (Group 18)

He, Ne, Ar, Kr, Xe, Rn. All have FULL OUTER SHELLS (2 for He; 8 for the rest). Very low reactivity — virtually inert under normal conditions (Xe can form compounds with F under extreme conditions).

NOBLE GAS

USES & NOTES

Helium (He)

Lightest noble gas (after H₂). Used in balloons, airships (NON-FLAMMABLE unlike H₂). Liquid He for MRI machines, superconductors (−4 K = coldest stable liquid).

Neon (Ne)

Famous red-orange glow in neon signs when electrically excited. Other colours from different noble gas mixtures.

Argon (Ar)

Most abundant noble gas (0.93% of air). Used in light bulbs (prevents tungsten filament oxidising), welding (inert shield), double-glazing insulation.

Xenon (Xe)

High-intensity camera flash lamps. Anaesthetic properties. Xe used in ion thrusters on spacecraft.



16

🔩 METALS

Reactivity Series · Extraction · Alloys · Blast Furnace · Hall-Héroult



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

The reactivity series is like a job application queue. The most reactive metals (K, Na, Ca at the top) are desperately eager to give away their electrons — they will react with cold water, air, even acids with explosive results. The least reactive metals (Cu, Ag, Au at the bottom) are so comfortable with their electrons they barely react with anything. Gold at the bottom is like a CEO who never needs to do anything — it is found free in nature as nuggets!



REACTIVITY SERIES OF METALS

K  (Potassium)   ■■■■■■■■■■  Most reactive | Reacts violently with cold water

Na (Sodium)      ■■■■■■■■■   Reacts vigorously with cold water

Ca (Calcium)     ■■■■■■■■    Reacts steadily with cold water

Mg (Magnesium)   ■■■■■■■     Reacts with steam; burns in O₂

Al (Aluminium)   ■■■■■■      Protected by Al₂O₃ layer (passive)

Zn (Zinc)        ■■■■■       Reacts with steam and dilute acids

Fe (Iron)        ■■■■        Reacts slowly with steam; rusts

Sn (Tin)         ■■■         Reacts slowly with acids

Pb (Lead)        ■■          Reacts slowly with acids

H  (Hydrogen)    ──────────  REFERENCE LINE

Cu (Copper)      ·           Does NOT react with acids or water

Ag (Silver)      ·           Very unreactive

Au (Gold)        ·           Least reactive — found as free metal



16.1  General Properties of Metals

PROPERTY

EXPLANATION

Electrical conductivity

Sea of delocalised electrons flows freely when voltage is applied. Cu is best conductor (after Ag). Used in wiring, cables, circuit boards.

Thermal conductivity

Delocalised electrons rapidly transfer kinetic energy. Metals feel cold because they conduct heat away from your warm hand.

Malleability (beaten into sheets)

Metal ions slide over each other when force is applied. Electron sea rearranges to maintain bonding. No bonds permanently broken.

Ductility (drawn into wire)

Same mechanism. Cu is most ductile. A 1 kg block of Cu can be drawn into about 100 km of wire.

High melting/boiling points

Strong electrostatic attraction between positive ions and delocalised electron sea requires lots of energy to overcome.

Metallic lustre

Free electrons absorb and re-emit light across all visible wavelengths, giving characteristic shine.

Form basic oxides

Metal oxides react with acids: CuO + H₂SO₄ → CuSO₄ + H₂O. Some (Al, Zn) are AMPHOTERIC (react with both acids and bases).



16.2  Reactions of Metals with Water and Acid

METAL/GROUP

REACTIONS WITH WATER AND ACID

K, Na (very reactive)

React VIOLENTLY with cold water: 2K + 2H₂O → 2KOH + H₂↑ (K ignites, zips around, purple flame) 2Na + 2H₂O → 2NaOH + H₂↑ (Na melts, moves, may ignite)

Ca

Reacts steadily with cold water: Ca + 2H₂O → Ca(OH)₂ + H₂↑ (steady bubbling, solution turns milky)

Mg

Reacts very slowly with cold water (MgO surface layer). Burns brightly in steam: Mg + H₂O(steam) → MgO + H₂↑

Al, Zn, Fe

React with STEAM or dilute acids (not cold water). Fe + 3H₂O(steam) → Fe₃O₄ + 4H₂↑ (iron scale) Zn + H₂SO₄ → ZnSO₄ + H₂↑ (vigorous)

Cu, Ag, Au

DO NOT react with water or dilute acids. Cu reacts with CONCENTRATED or HOT acids only: Cu + 2H₂SO₄(hot conc) → CuSO₄ + SO₂ + 2H₂O

Displacement from salt solutions

More reactive displaces less reactive: Fe + CuSO₄(aq) → FeSO₄ + Cu↓ (blue → green, brown Cu deposits) Zn + 2AgNO₃(aq) → Zn(NO₃)₂ + 2Ag↓ (silver crystals form)

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Pure aluminium should be highly reactive (it is high in the reactivity series between Mg and Zn). But normal aluminium looks unreactive because it instantly forms a thin, transparent, extremely hard layer of aluminium oxide (Al₂O₃) on its surface when exposed to air. This layer (only nanometres thick!) seals the metal from further reaction. If you scratch it in NaOH solution (which dissolves Al₂O₃), the aluminium below reacts vigorously: Al + NaOH + H₂O → NaAlO₂ + H₂↑ — pouring NaOH on aluminium foil and observing bubbles is a striking demonstration.



16.3  Extraction of Metals from Ores

METAL GROUP

EXTRACTION METHOD & REASON

Very reactive metals (K, Na, Ca, Mg, Al)

Compounds too stable for carbon reduction. Must use ELECTROLYSIS of molten compound. Al: Hall-Héroult process (molten Al₂O₃ in cryolite). Na: Down's Cell (molten NaCl).

Moderately reactive (Zn, Fe, Sn, Pb)

REDUCTION with carbon (coke) or CO at high temperature. Fe: Blast furnace (Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂). Zn: ZnO + C → Zn + CO (zinc distils off at 907°C).

Least reactive (Cu, Ag, Au)

Cu: smelting of CuFeS₂ (chalcopyrite) ore by roasting and reduction, or electrolytic refining. Ag, Au: found as free metals or concentrated by cyanide leaching.

The Blast Furnace — Iron Extraction

Raw materials loaded at the top: iron ore (Fe₂O₃, haematite) + coke (C) + limestone (CaCO₃). Hot air blast enters at the bottom.

COMBUSTION: C + O₂ → CO₂  (coke burns, provides heat 1900°C)

CO FORMATION: CO₂ + C → 2CO  (in hotter, excess coke zone — CO is the real reducing agent)

REDUCTION of ore: Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂  (at ~700°C, molten iron sinks to bottom)

LIMESTONE DECOMPOSITION: CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂  (lime forms at high temperature)

SLAG FORMATION: CaO + SiO₂ → CaSiO₃  (calcium silicate slag, removes sandy gangue impurity)

Pig iron (from blast furnace) contains ~4% C — brittle. Converted to STEEL by blasting pure O₂ to oxidise excess carbon to CO₂.



16.4  Alloys — Why Mixtures are Better

An ALLOY is a mixture of a metal with one or more elements (metals or non-metals). Adding atoms of different size to a metal lattice disrupts the regular layers, preventing them from sliding as easily — the alloy is HARDER and STRONGER than the pure metal.

ALLOY

COMPOSITION & USES

Steel (Fe + C + others)

Plain carbon steel: Fe + 0.1–1.5% C. Much harder than pure Fe. Stainless steel: 18% Cr + 8% Ni. Cr forms self-healing Cr₂O₃ layer. USES: Buildings (Third Mainland Bridge!), cars, cutlery, surgical instruments.

Brass (Cu + 30–40% Zn)

Harder than Cu. Golden colour. Corrosion resistant. USES: Musical instruments (trumpet, trombone, saxophone), door handles, fittings.

Bronze (Cu + 8–12% Sn)

Resistant to corrosion and wear. Dark brown colour. USES: Statues, bearings, bells, coins. Nigeria's Benin bronzes are this alloy!

Solder (Pb + Sn, 50:50)

Low melting point (~183°C). Flows easily when melted. USES: Joining electronic circuit board components.

Duralumin (Al + Cu + Mg)

Light (2.8 g/cm³) yet very strong. Density much less than steel (7.9 g/cm³). USES: Aircraft bodies, car parts, bicycle frames.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The famous Benin City bronze plaques and sculptures — produced between the 13th and 19th centuries by craftsmen of the Benin Kingdom (present-day Edo State) — are made of BRASS (copper-zinc alloy), not pure bronze. They are among the greatest works of art in world history. Over 3,000 were looted by British forces in 1897. The University of Aberdeen became the first British institution to return their Benin bronzes to Nigeria in 2021, and many others have since followed. This history is literally connected to the chemistry of copper alloys you are studying!



17

🌱 ORGANIC CHEMISTRY

Homologous Series · Functional Groups · Reactions · Polymers



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Carbon atoms are like LEGO bricks with 4 connection points. You can chain them in straight lines (n-alkanes), branch them (iso-alkanes), form rings (cyclohexane, benzene), or attach special 'add-on accessories' (functional groups). The functional group is the key — it completely determines how the molecule behaves chemically, just like the accessory on a LEGO character determines what job it can do.



ORGANIC CHEMISTRY — Key Functional Groups

  CLASS        FUNCTIONAL     EXAMPLE        TEST

               GROUP

  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Alkane        C–C only       CH₄, C₂H₆     Burns cleanly, no reaction

                               (methane,      with bromine water

                               ethane)



  Alkene        C=C            C₂H₄ (ethene) Decolourises bromine water

                               C₃H₆ (propene) (orange → colourless) ✓



  Alcohol       –OH            C₂H₅OH        Reacts with Na, oxidised

                               (ethanol)      to acid or aldehyde



  Carboxylic    –COOH          CH₃COOH       Reacts with Na₂CO₃ → CO₂

  acid                         (acetic acid)  Litmus turns red



  Aldehyde      –CHO           CH₃CHO        Fehling's → BRICK RED ppt

                               (ethanal)      Tollens' → SILVER MIRROR



  Ketone        C=O (chain)    CH₃COCH₃      No reaction with Fehling's

                               (propanone/    or Tollens' — KEY DIFFERENCE

                               acetone)       from aldehydes



  Ester         –COO–          CH₃COOC₂H₅   Fruity/sweet smell

                               (ethyl         Formed with H₂SO₄ catalyst

                               ethanoate)



17.1  Why Carbon is Special

Carbon forms FOUR covalent bonds (tetravalent) — maximum versatility

Carbon can bond to other carbon atoms in chains and rings (CATENATION)

C–C bonds are strong but not too strong — reactions occur at reasonable conditions

Carbon forms stable bonds with H, O, N, S, halogens — huge variety of molecules

Over 10 million organic compounds known; new ones discovered/made every day



17.2  Homologous Series and Naming

A HOMOLOGOUS SERIES is a family of organic compounds sharing: (1) Same functional group. (2) Same general formula. (3) Consecutive members differ by CH₂ (14 g/mol). (4) Similar chemical behaviour. (5) Gradually changing physical properties.

SERIES

FORMULA, EXAMPLES & KEY REACTIONS

ALKANES  (CnH2n+2)

Saturated (C–C single bonds only). No functional group. CH₄ methane | C₂H₆ ethane | C₃H₈ propane | C₄H₁₀ butane Low reactivity. Free radical substitution with halogens (UV). Fuels (LPG, petrol, diesel, natural gas).

ALKENES  (CnH2n)

Unsaturated (contains one C=C double bond). C₂H₄ ethene | C₃H₆ propene | C₄H₈ butene MORE REACTIVE than alkanes due to C=C. Electrophilic addition reactions. TEST: decolourises orange bromine water.

ALKYNES  (CnH2n-2)

Unsaturated (one C≡C triple bond). C₂H₂ ethyne (acetylene). Very reactive. Oxy-acetylene welding (burns at 3100°C).

ALCOHOLS  (–OH group)

Primary: R–CH₂–OH | Secondary: R₂CH–OH | Tertiary: R₃C–OH CH₃OH methanol (poisonous!) | C₂H₅OH ethanol (drinks) Reactions: oxidation, esterification, dehydration, react with Na.

CARBOXYLIC ACIDS  (–COOH)

HCOOH methanoic (formic) acid | CH₃COOH ethanoic (acetic) acid/vinegar. Weak acids. Esterification with alcohols (+ H₂SO₄ catalyst). TEST: Reacts with Na₂CO₃ → CO₂ gas (effervescence).

ESTERS  (–COO–)

Formed from alcohol + carboxylic acid + H₂SO₄ catalyst (reversible). FRUITY SMELLS. Ethyl ethanoate (pear/nail polish). Hydrolysis reverses esterification. Saponification (NaOH) → soap.

ALDEHYDES  (–CHO)

From oxidation of PRIMARY alcohol. CH₃CHO ethanal. Sharp smell. TESTS: Fehling's solution → BRICK-RED precipitate. Tollens' reagent (ammoniacal silver nitrate) → SILVER MIRROR on glass.

KETONES  (–CO– in chain)

From oxidation of SECONDARY alcohol. CH₃COCH₃ propanone (acetone, nail polish remover). NO reaction with Fehling's or Tollens' — KEY difference from aldehydes!



17.3  Key Reactions to Master

Addition Reactions of Alkenes

HYDROGENATION: C=C + H₂ → C–C  (Ni catalyst, 150°C). Makes MARGARINE from liquid vegetable oils (liquid unsaturated → solid saturated fats).

HALOGENATION: C=C + Br₂ → CBr–CBr  (decolourises orange bromine water → colourless). Standard JAMB TEST for alkenes.

HYDRATION: C=C + H₂O → C(OH)–C  (H₃PO₄ catalyst, 300°C, high pressure). Industrial ethanol production.

HYDROHALOGENATION: C=C + HX → C–C(X). Markovnikov's rule: H adds to C with MORE H atoms.

Free Radical Substitution of Alkanes

CH₄ + Cl₂ → CH₃Cl + HCl  (UV light required)

Mechanism: INITIATION (Cl₂ + hν → 2Cl•) → PROPAGATION (Cl• + CH₄ → HCl + CH₃•; CH₃• + Cl₂ → CH₃Cl + Cl•) → TERMINATION (two radicals combine to end the chain)

Oxidation of Alcohols

Primary alcohol → Aldehyde → Carboxylic acid  (using KMnO₄ or K₂Cr₂O₇ as oxidising agent in acid)

Colour changes: KMnO₄ purple → colourless; K₂Cr₂O₇ orange → green

Esterification

CH₃COOH + C₂H₅OH ⇌ CH₃COOC₂H₅ + H₂O  (conc H₂SO₄ catalyst, heat, REVERSIBLE)

Name the ester: first part from alcohol (ethyl…), second from acid (…ethanoate) = ethyl ethanoate

💡 EXAM TIP

FUNCTIONAL GROUP TESTS SUMMARY — MEMORISE ALL: • Decolourises bromine water → ALKENE (C=C present) • Fehling's → brick-red precipitate → ALDEHYDE • Tollens' → silver mirror → ALDEHYDE • Na₂CO₃ → CO₂ bubbles → CARBOXYLIC ACID • Fruity smell + H₂SO₄ + alcohol + heat → ESTER • Burns with sooty flame → AROMATIC or HIGH CARBON compound • Burns cleanly, blue flame → lower alkane or alcohol



17.4  Polymers

TYPE

MECHANISM, EXAMPLES & USES

ADDITION POLYMERISATION

Alkene monomers join via opening of C=C double bond. NO BY-PRODUCT formed. EXAMPLES: n(CH₂=CH₂) → (–CH₂–CH₂–)n  polyethylene (carrier bags, bottles) n(CH₂=CHCl) → PVC (pipes, cables, records) n(CH₂=CH₃) → poly(propene) (woven polypropylene sacks used in Nigeria)

CONDENSATION POLYMERISATION

Two DIFFERENT functional groups react, releasing small molecule (H₂O or HCl) each time. NYLON-6,6: hexane-1,6-diamine (H₂N–) + hexanedioic acid (–COOH) → nylon + nH₂O POLYESTER (Terylene): ethane-1,2-diol (–OH) + benzene-1,4-dicarboxylic acid (–COOH) → polyester + nH₂O Natural examples: proteins (amino acids), DNA (nucleotides), cellulose (glucose units).

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Every woven polypropylene bag used to carry grain, garri, and rice in Nigerian markets is made from ADDITION POLYMERISATION of propene (a petroleum by-product). Nigeria imports millions of tonnes of polymer resins and manufactures bags locally — the chemistry of polymerisation feeds millions of Nigerians by providing affordable food packaging. The nylon in fishing nets used by artisanal fishermen on the Niger Delta rivers is made by CONDENSATION POLYMERISATION of nylon-6,6.



18

🏭 INDUSTRIAL CHEMISTRY

Haber · Contact · Ostwald · Solvay · Chlor-alkali · Cement · Glass



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

Industrial chemistry is laboratory chemistry scaled up by a factor of a million. All the principles — equilibrium, rates, energy — apply at the factory scale. The Haber process, for example, is just Le Chatelier's Principle applied to nitrogen and hydrogen in a very big vessel. Understanding the chemistry tells you exactly WHY engineers chose 450°C, 200 atm, and iron catalyst — every condition has a chemical reason.



18.1  Haber Process — Ammonia Production

THE HABER PROCESS — Industrial Ammonia Production

  N₂ (from liquid air)  ──────────────────────────────────────┐

                                                               │

  H₂ (from natural gas) ──────────────────────────────────────┤

  CH₄ + H₂O → CO + 3H₂ (steam reforming)                     │

                                                               ▼

                              ┌────────────────────────────────┐

                              │   REACTION VESSEL              │

                              │   N₂ + 3H₂ ⇌ 2NH₃            │

                              │   Temp: 450°C (compromise)     │

                              │   Press: 200–250 atm (high)    │

                              │   Catalyst: Iron (Fe)          │

                              │   ~15% conversion per pass     │

                              └─────────────┬──────────────────┘

                                            │

                                            ▼

                          ┌─────────────────────────────────┐

                          │        CONDENSER                │

                          │   NH₃ liquefied and removed     │

                          │   N₂ + H₂ recycled back ──────►│

                          └────────────────┬────────────────┘

                                           │

                                           ▼

                                    LIQUID NH₃

                               (Fertilisers, HNO₃, Nylon)



N₂(g) + 3H₂(g) ⇌ 2NH₃(g)    ΔH = −92 kJ/mol  (EXOTHERMIC)

Conditions: 450°C  |  200–250 atm  |  Fe catalyst (+ K₂O and Al₂O₃ promoters)

~15% conversion/pass  |  NH₃ liquefied (bp −33°C) and removed  |  Gases recycled



ASPECT

DETAILS

Raw Materials

NITROGEN: from fractional distillation of liquid air. HYDROGEN: from steam reforming of natural gas: CH₄ + H₂O → CO + 3H₂  (Ni catalyst, 750–850°C) CO + H₂O → CO₂ + H₂  (CO shift reaction) CO₂ removed by absorption in K₂CO₃ solution.

Why 450°C?

COMPROMISE between yield and rate. Exothermic reaction: Le Chatelier → lower T gives higher yield. But low T = impossibly slow rate (even with catalyst). 450°C: ~15–25% conversion at viable commercial rate. This is the 'Haber compromise temperature'.

Why 200 atm?

4 mol gas → 2 mol gas (fewer moles on product side). Le Chatelier: high P favours fewer moles → shifts right → more NH₃. Also increases rate (higher concentration). Limited by engineering cost of high-pressure vessels.

Role of Fe catalyst

Lowers Ea so equilibrium reached in SECONDS at 450°C. Does NOT change: Kc, ΔH, equilibrium position, or yield. K₂O = electronic promoter. Al₂O₃ = structural promoter (prevents sintering).

Recycling

Only ~15% converts per pass. NH₃ cooled and liquefied → removed (Le Chatelier: removing product shifts RIGHT). Unreacted N₂ + H₂ recycled back → overall conversion much higher.

Uses of NH₃

80% → fertilisers (NH₄NO₃, (NH₄)₂SO₄, urea CO(NH₂)₂). Also: Ostwald process (HNO₃), nylon, explosives, cleaning agents, refrigerant.

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Before the Haber process was invented in 1908 by Fritz Haber and commercialised by Carl Bosch, the world's nitrogen supply for fertilisers came entirely from guano (seabird droppings) from South America and Chilean saltpetre (NaNO₃). These sources could support a maximum of about 4 billion people. Today's population of 8 billion is ONLY possible because of the Haber process — about half of all nitrogen atoms in your body came through a Haber plant at some point. The Notore Chemical Industries at Onne Port, Rivers State uses natural gas from the Niger Delta as the H₂ source for Nigerian fertiliser production.



18.2  Contact Process — Sulfuric Acid (H₂SO₄)

STEP 1: S + O₂ → SO₂  (burning molten sulfur)  OR  4FeS₂ + 11O₂ → 2Fe₂O₃ + 8SO₂  (roasting pyrite)

STEP 2 (KEY EQUILIBRIUM): 2SO₂(g) + O₂(g) ⇌ 2SO₃(g)   ΔH = −197 kJ/mol CONDITIONS: V₂O₅ catalyst, 450°C, ~1–2 atm. Achieves ~98% conversion.

WHY V₂O₅? Vanadium(V) oxide works at relatively low temperature. It alternates between V₅⁺ and V₄⁺ — it provides an alternate redox pathway with lower Ea.

STEP 3: SO₃ + H₂SO₄(conc) → H₂S₂O₇ (oleum)  THEN  H₂S₂O₇ + H₂O → 2H₂SO₄

WHY NOT ABSORB SO₃ IN WATER DIRECTLY? Forms a dense, persistent acid MIST (tiny droplets) that cannot be separated from the gas phase. Environmental disaster. Oleum method is safe and efficient.

CONDITION

REASONING

Low pressure (~1 atm)

2SO₂ + O₂ → 2SO₃: 3 mol gas → 2 mol gas. Le Chatelier: high P would increase yield. But: SO₂/SO₃ are not corrosive enough to justify expensive high-pressure equipment when 98% yield achievable at 1 atm anyway.

450°C (like Haber)

Exothermic reaction: lower T would give higher yield but rate too slow. Higher T gives faster rate but poor yield. 450°C with V₂O₅ gives 98% conversion (much better than Haber's 15%!).



18.3  Ostwald Process — Nitric Acid (HNO₃)

Starting material: ammonia (NH₃) from the Haber process. Product: HNO₃ for fertilisers and explosives.

4NH₃(g) + 5O₂(g) → 4NO(g) + 6H₂O(g)   [Pt/Rh gauze catalyst, 850°C, 8–10 atm]

2NO(g) + O₂(g) → 2NO₂(g)   [cooling, no catalyst needed, spontaneous at lower T]

4NO₂(g) + O₂(g) + 2H₂O(l) → 4HNO₃(aq)   [absorbed in water → 60–68% HNO₃]

USES of HNO₃: Ammonium nitrate (NH₄NO₃) fertiliser (NH₃ + HNO₃ → NH₄NO₃). TNT and other explosives. Dyes, pharmaceuticals, etching metals.



18.4  Solvay Process — Sodium Carbonate (Na₂CO₃)

Also called AMMONIA-SODA PROCESS. Na₂CO₃ (soda ash / washing soda) used in glass, detergents, paper, water softening.

STAGE

DETAILS

Raw materials

NaCl (brine) + CaCO₃ (limestone) + NH₃ (recycled — makes process very economical and environmentally efficient)

Key reaction

Brine saturated with NH₃ then CO₂ bubbled through: NaCl + NH₃ + CO₂ + H₂O → NaHCO₃↓ + NH₄Cl NaHCO₃ precipitates (least soluble salt present). Filtered off.

Decompose NaHCO₃

2NaHCO₃ → Na₂CO₃ + H₂O + CO₂  (CO₂ recycled back)

Recover NH₃ (recycled!)

2NH₄Cl + Ca(OH)₂ → CaCl₂ + 2NH₃↑ + 2H₂O NH₃ recycled back to start. Only by-product is CaCl₂ (used for de-icing roads).



18.5  Chlor-alkali Process

Electrolysis of concentrated NaCl (brine) in a membrane cell or diaphragm cell produces three important industrial chemicals simultaneously:

ELECTRODE / PRODUCT

REACTION & USES

CATHODE: 2H⁺ + 2e⁻ → H₂↑

HYDROGEN gas. USES: Fuel (H₂ fuel cells), making NH₃ (Haber), margarine (hydrogenation), HCl manufacture.

ANODE: 2Cl⁻ → Cl₂↑ + 2e⁻

CHLORINE gas. USES: PVC production, bleach (NaOCl), water disinfection, HCl, pesticides, solvents.

Solution: NaOH remains

SODIUM HYDROXIDE solution. USES: Soap/detergent making, paper/pulp production, food processing (NaOH thins cocoa butter), aluminium extraction (dissolves Al₂O₃ in Bayer process).



18.6  Cement and Glass — Nigeria's Key Industries

CEMENT PRODUCTION

Raw materials: Limestone (CaCO₃) + Clay (SiO₂, Al₂O₃, Fe₂O₃).

Crushed limestone and clay mixed in correct proportions

Heated in a ROTARY KILN at 1450°C (CLINKERING temperature): CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂  (calcination) CaO + SiO₂ → CaSiO₃  (calcium silicate, main binding compound) CaO + Al₂O₃ → CaAl₂O₄  (calcium aluminate)

Product (CLINKER) ground to fine powder

Mixed with gypsum (CaSO₄·2H₂O) → PORTLAND CEMENT

SETTING: Cement + water → complex hydration reactions → interlocking calcium silicate hydrate crystals → hard, rigid concrete. Exothermic process (concrete gets warm as it sets).

GLASS PRODUCTION

SODA-LIME GLASS (most common): SiO₂ (silica/sand) + Na₂CO₃ (soda ash) + CaCO₃ (limestone). Melted together at ~1500°C. Na₂CO₃ + SiO₂ → Na₂SiO₃ + CO₂. Cooled to form amorphous (non-crystalline) solid.

COLOURED GLASS: Adding metal oxides: Co²⁺ = blue | Fe²⁺ = green | Fe³⁺ = yellow | Cu²⁺ = blue-green | Mn²⁺ = purple

BOROSILICATE GLASS (Pyrex): Add B₂O₃ → resists thermal shock. Used for laboratory glassware and cooking dishes.



18.7  Industrial Chemistry — Master Summary

PROCESS

PRODUCT

KEY CONDITIONS

HABER PROCESS

NH₃ (ammonia)

Fe catalyst | 450°C | 200–250 atm

CONTACT PROCESS

H₂SO₄ (sulfuric acid)

V₂O₅ catalyst | 450°C | ~1 atm

OSTWALD PROCESS

HNO₃ (nitric acid)

Pt/Rh catalyst | 850°C | 8–10 atm

SOLVAY PROCESS

Na₂CO₃ (soda ash)

No catalyst | ~70°C | ~1 atm

CHLOR-ALKALI

Cl₂ + H₂ + NaOH

Electrolysis | Room T | Atmospheric

HALL-HÉROULT

Aluminium (Al)

Electrolysis | 900°C (melt) | in cryolite

BLAST FURNACE

Iron (Fe / pig iron)

Coke + limestone + hot air | 1900°C

🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Dangote Cement Plc, based in Lagos, is the LARGEST cement company in Africa and one of the largest in the world. The Obajana plant in Kogi State has an annual production capacity of 16.25 million tonnes — the single largest cement plant in sub-Saharan Africa. Every sack of Dangote cement represents the chemical reactions you studied here: CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂ in a rotary kiln at 1450°C, consuming enormous amounts of limestone from local quarries. Nigeria is also the largest cement producer in West Africa.

19

🏷️ IUPAC NAMING OF ORGANIC COMPOUNDS

Step-by-step method · Alkanes · Alkenes · Alkynes · Alcohols · Acids · Esters · Aldehydes · Ketones · Amines · Halogenoalkanes



🎯 SIMPLE ANALOGY — Think of it this way:

IUPAC naming is like writing an ADDRESS for a molecule. Every molecule has a unique address that tells you exactly: (1) HOW MANY carbon atoms are in the main road (chain length prefix), (2) WHAT TYPE of road it is (suffix for the functional group), and (3) WHERE the house numbers are (position numbers for branches and groups). Once you learn the street names (prefixes) and road types (suffixes), you can read or write ANY organic name!



19.1  The Two Building Blocks of Every IUPAC Name

Every IUPAC name is built from just TWO parts stuck together:



IUPAC NAME = PREFIX + SUFFIX

PREFIX

+

SUFFIX



PART

MEANING & LIST

PREFIX — tells you the chain length (how many carbons in the longest chain)

METH = 1 carbon ETH  = 2 carbons PROP = 3 carbons BUT  = 4 carbons PENT = 5 carbons HEX  = 6 carbons HEPT = 7 carbons OCT  = 8 carbons NON  = 9 carbons DEC  = 10 carbons

SUFFIX — tells you the functional group (what type of compound it is)

-ANE   = alkane (single bonds only) -ENE   = alkene (one C=C double bond) -YNE   = alkyne (one C≡C triple bond) -OL    = alcohol (–OH group) -AL    = aldehyde (–CHO group) -ONE   = ketone (C=O in chain) -OIC ACID = carboxylic acid (–COOH) -OATE  = ester (–COO–) -AMINE = amine (–NH₂) -YL HALIDE = halogenoalkane



SIMPLE RULE:  Name = [chain length prefix] + [functional group suffix]

Example:  PROP  +  ANE  =  PROPANE  (3 carbons, all single bonds)

Example:  ETH   +  OL   =  ETHANOL  (2 carbons, with –OH group)

Example:  BUT   +  ONE  =  BUTANONE (4 carbons, with ketone C=O)



19.2  Carbon Chain Prefixes — Memorise These!

The prefix tells you how many carbon atoms are in the LONGEST CHAIN. Think of it like naming a road by its length — a 'methyl road' has 1 carbon, an 'ethyl road' has 2, and so on.



CHAIN LENGTH PREFIXES — THE 10 YOU MUST KNOW

  Prefix   Carbons   Memory Trick

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────

  METH-       1        METHANE = natural gas (1 C)

  ETH-        2        ETHANOL = drinking alcohol (2 C)

  PROP-       3        PROPANE = cooking gas (3 C)

  BUT-        4        BUTANE = lighter fuel (4 C)

  PENT-       5        PENTA = pentagon has 5 sides

  HEX-        6        HEXA = hexagon has 6 sides

  HEPT-       7        HEPTA = heptagon has 7 sides

  OCT-        8        OCTA = octopus has 8 legs

  NON-        9        NONA = nonagon has 9 sides

  DEC-       10        DECA = decade = 10 years



🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The prefix 'meth-' comes from the Greek word 'methy' meaning wine, and 'hyle' meaning wood — because methanol (CH₃OH) was originally made by the destructive distillation of wood. The prefix 'eth-' comes from the Greek 'aither' (upper air). These ancient Greek roots are used in every chemistry laboratory and industrial plant in the world today!



19.3  The 5-Step IUPAC Naming Method — Works Every Time

Follow these 5 steps in ORDER for every molecule, and you will ALWAYS get the correct IUPAC name:



THE 5-STEP IUPAC NAMING METHOD

  STEP 1:  Find the LONGEST CARBON CHAIN that contains the

           principal functional group. Count its carbons → gives the PREFIX.



  STEP 2:  Identify the PRINCIPAL FUNCTIONAL GROUP (highest priority).

           This gives the SUFFIX (–ol, –al, –one, –oic acid, etc.)



  STEP 3:  NUMBER the chain from the END NEAREST to the principal

           functional group (give it the LOWEST possible number).



  STEP 4:  Identify and name all SUBSTITUENTS (branches, extra groups).

           Use multiplying prefixes: di=2, tri=3, tetra=4.



  STEP 5:  Assemble the name:

           [substituents in alphabetical order]-[chain prefix][suffix]

           Separate numbers from letters with a hyphen (-).

           Separate numbers from numbers with a comma (,).



19.4  Naming ALKANES (suffix: -ane)

Alkanes contain ONLY carbon–carbon single bonds and carbon–hydrogen bonds. They are SATURATED. Suffix = –ane.



STRAIGHT-CHAIN ALKANES — Simplest Case

  CH₄                     →  methane       (1 carbon, -ane suffix)

  CH₃–CH₃                 →  ethane        (2 carbons)

  CH₃–CH₂–CH₃             →  propane       (3 carbons)

  CH₃–CH₂–CH₂–CH₃         →  butane        (4 carbons)

  CH₃–(CH₂)₃–CH₃           →  pentane       (5 carbons)

  CH₃–(CH₂)₄–CH₃           →  hexane        (6 carbons)



Branched Alkanes — Substituents (Alkyl Groups)

When an alkane has a BRANCH (a smaller carbon group attached to the main chain), we name the branch as an ALKYL GROUP: methyl (CH₃–), ethyl (C₂H₅–), propyl (C₃H₇–), butyl (C₄H₉–), etc.



BRANCHED ALKANE NAMING — Step by Step

  Example 1:  CH₃–CH(CH₃)–CH₃



  STEP 1: Longest chain? Count the main chain ignoring the branch:

          Main chain = C–C–C = 3 carbons → PREFIX = prop-

  STEP 2: Functional group? None special → SUFFIX = -ane

  STEP 3: Number from end nearest branch:

          C1–C2–C3, branch is on C2

  STEP 4: Branch = CH₃ = methyl group, on carbon 2

  STEP 5: Name = 2-methylpropane



  Example 2:  CH₃–CH(CH₃)–CH₂–CH₃



  STEP 1: Longest chain = 4 carbons → PREFIX = but-

  STEP 2: No functional group → SUFFIX = -ane

  STEP 3: Number so branch gets LOWEST number:

          From left: branch on C2. From right: branch on C3.

          Use numbering that gives C2 (lowest) → branch at position 2

  STEP 4: Branch = CH₃ = methyl, at carbon 2

  STEP 5: Name = 2-methylbutane



  Example 3:  CH₃–C(CH₃)₂–CH₂–CH₃  (two methyl branches on same carbon)



  Longest chain = 4 C → but-, two methyls on C2

  Name = 2,2-dimethylbutane   (di = two, same carbon 2)



📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Name this compound:  CH₃–CH(C₂H₅)–CH₂–CH₂–CH₃  STEP 1: What is the longest chain? Option A: Go straight: C–C–C–C–C = 5 carbons Option B: Go through the branch: C–C–C₃ gives 5 (but must count through branch too). Actual longest continuous chain = 6 carbons (go through the ethyl branch!)  STEP 2: All single bonds → suffix = -ane. 6 carbons → hex- STEP 3: Number from end nearest the branch: branch at C3 STEP 4: Remaining CH₃ is a methyl branch at C3 STEP 5: Name = 3-methylhexane



💡 EXAM TIP

LONGEST CHAIN TRAP: Always trace every possible path through the molecule and pick the LONGEST one. Many students stop at a chain of 5 when there is actually a chain of 6 or 7 going through a branch. Try all routes!



19.5  Naming ALKENES (suffix: -ene)

Alkenes contain ONE C=C double bond. They are UNSATURATED. Suffix = –ene. The position of the double bond gets a number.



ALKENE NAMING — Position of the Double Bond

  RULE: Number the chain so the double bond gets the LOWEST number.

        Use the number of the FIRST carbon of the double bond.



  CH₂=CH₂                     →  ethene        (no number needed — only one position)

  CH₂=CH–CH₃                 →  propene       (no number — only one position)

  CH₂=CH–CH₂–CH₃             →  but-1-ene     (double bond starts at C1)

  CH₃–CH=CH–CH₃             →  but-2-ene     (double bond starts at C2)

  CH₂=CH–CH₂–CH₂–CH₃       →  pent-1-ene    (5 C, double bond at C1)

  CH₃–CH=CH–CH₂–CH₃       →  pent-2-ene    (5 C, double bond at C2)

  CH₂=C(CH₃)–CH₃             →  2-methylpropene  (branch on C2 of propene)



BUT-1-ENE vs BUT-2-ENE — Position Numbers Matter!

C¹H₂=C²H–C³H₂–C₄H₃ but-1-ene (=bond on C1)

vs

C¹H₃–C²H=C³H–C₄H₃ but-2-ene (=bond on C2)



⚠️ COMMON MISTAKE

For but-2-ene, numbering from the RIGHT gives the double bond at C2. Numbering from the LEFT also gives C2. When both ends give the same number, use the end that gives substituents a LOWER number. For but-1-ene, numbering from the LEFT gives C1, but from the right it would be C3 — ALWAYS use the lower number (C1 wins). So but-1-ene is correct, not but-3-ene!



19.6  Naming ALKYNES (suffix: -yne)

Alkynes contain ONE C≡C TRIPLE bond. Suffix = –yne. Named exactly like alkenes but use –yne.



Structural / Condensed Formula

IUPAC Name

Common Name

Key Feature

HC≡CH

ethyne

acetylene

Triple bond, 2 C

HC≡C–CH₃

propyne

(propyne)

Triple bond, 3 C

HC≡C–CH₂–CH₃

but-1-yne

—

Triple bond at C1

CH₃–C≡C–CH₃

but-2-yne

—

Triple bond at C2

HC≡C–(CH₂)₂–CH₃

pent-1-yne

—

Triple bond at C1, 5 C



19.7  Naming ALCOHOLS (suffix: -ol)

Alcohols contain the –OH (hydroxyl) group. Suffix = –ol. The carbon bearing the –OH gets the LOWEST possible number.



ALCOHOL NAMING WITH POSITION NUMBERS

  RULE: Number the chain from the end NEAREST to the –OH group.

        –OH group ALWAYS gets the lowest possible number.



  CH₃OH                       →  methanol       (1 C)

  CH₃CH₂OH                   →  ethanol        (2 C, OH on C1 from either end)

  CH₃CH₂CH₂OH               →  propan-1-ol   (OH on C1)

  CH₃CH(OH)CH₃              →  propan-2-ol   (OH on C2)

  CH₃CH₂CH₂CH₂OH           →  butan-1-ol    (OH on C1)

  CH₃CH₂CH(OH)CH₃          →  butan-2-ol    (OH on C2)

  (CH₃)₃COH                  →  2-methylpropan-2-ol  (tertiary, OH on C2)



PRIMARY, SECONDARY, TERTIARY ALCOHOLS

PRIMARY (1°) –OH on C with 2 H atoms E.g. ethanol CH₃CH₂OH

SECONDARY (2°) –OH on C with 1 H atom E.g. propan-2-ol CH₃CH(OH)CH₃

TERTIARY (3°) –OH on C with 0 H atoms E.g. 2-methylpropan-2-ol (CH₃)₃COH



📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Name:  CH₃–CH(OH)–CH₂–CH₂–CH₃  STEP 1: Longest chain containing –OH = 5 carbons → pentan- STEP 2: Functional group = –OH → suffix = -ol STEP 3: Number from the end nearest –OH:          From left: OH on C2. From right: OH on C4.          Use LOWER number → C2 wins. STEP 4: No other substituents STEP 5: Name = pentan-2-ol



🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

Ethanol (CH₃CH₂OH) is the alcohol in palm wine, burukutu, ogogoro, beer, and all fermented drinks. It is named 'ethanol' because it has 2 carbons (ETH-) and an –OH group (-OL). Methanol (CH₃OH, 1 carbon) looks and smells identical to ethanol but is EXTREMELY TOXIC — just 10 mL can cause permanent blindness and 30 mL can kill! Unscrupulous producers sometimes add methanol to local gin to increase volume. This has caused many deaths in Nigeria. IUPAC naming helps distinguish these two lethal look-alikes: methanol (1 C) vs ethanol (2 C).



19.8  Naming ALDEHYDES (suffix: -al)

Aldehydes have the –CHO group at the END of the carbon chain. Suffix = –al. The CHO carbon is ALWAYS carbon 1 — you never need to give it a number because it is always at the end.



ALDEHYDE NAMING — CHO is always C1

  HCHO                         →  methanal       (1 C, also called formaldehyde)

  CH₃CHO                       →  ethanal        (2 C, also called acetaldehyde)

  CH₃CH₂CHO                   →  propanal       (3 C)

  CH₃CH₂CH₂CHO               →  butanal        (4 C)

  CH₃CH(CH₃)CHO              →  2-methylpropanal  (branch on C2)



  KEY POINT: The CHO carbon is ALWAYS numbered C1.

  So you NEVER say '1-ethanal' — just 'ethanal' (C1 is implied).

  You may need to number the chain to place substituents.



TOPIC

DETAILS

TEST for Aldehydes

Fehling's solution: brick-red Cu₂O precipitate Tollens' reagent: silver mirror on inside of tube Benedict's solution: brick-red (used in medical tests) Ketones give NONE of these tests.

Oxidation state

Aldehyde is mid-way between alcohol and acid: Primary alcohol → [oxidise] → Aldehyde → [oxidise] → Carboxylic acid CH₃CH₂OH → CH₃CHO → CH₃COOH (using K₂Cr₂O₇/H⁺ or KMnO₄/H⁺ as oxidising agents)



19.9  Naming KETONES (suffix: -one)

Ketones have the C=O group IN THE MIDDLE of the carbon chain (not at the end). Suffix = –one. The carbonyl carbon must be given the LOWEST possible number.



KETONE NAMING — C=O in the middle

  CH₃–CO–CH₃               →  propan-1-one?  NO! — propan-2-one  (C=O on C2)

                            Also called: acetone or propanone

  CH₃–CO–CH₂–CH₃         →  butan-2-one    (C=O on C2, 4 carbons)

  CH₃–CH₂–CO–CH₂–CH₃   →  pentan-3-one   (C=O on C3, 5 carbons)



  RULE: Number from the end that gives C=O the LOWEST number.

  For butan-2-one:  numbering from left gives C=O on C2.

                    numbering from right gives C=O on C3.

                    C2 < C3, so number from LEFT → butan-2-one ✓



⚠️ COMMON MISTAKE

The smallest possible ketone is PROPAN-2-ONE (3 carbons). You CANNOT have a ketone on carbon 1 — that would be an aldehyde (CHO). The C=O in a ketone is always flanked by two carbon atoms, so the smallest ketone has 3 carbons with C=O in the middle (C2).



19.10  Naming CARBOXYLIC ACIDS (suffix: -oic acid)

Carboxylic acids have the –COOH group at the END of the chain. The COOH carbon is ALWAYS C1. Suffix = –oic acid.



Structural / Condensed Formula

IUPAC Name

Common Name

Key Feature

HCOOH

methanoic acid

formic acid

COOH only, 1 C

CH₃COOH

ethanoic acid

acetic acid

2 C, vinegar

CH₃CH₂COOH

propanoic acid

(propanoic)

3 C

CH₃CH₂CH₂COOH

butanoic acid

butyric acid

4 C, rancid butter smell

CH₃(CH₂)₄COOH

hexanoic acid

caproic acid

6 C

CH₃(CH₂)₁₆COOH

octadecanoic acid

stearic acid

18 C, solid fat

CH₃CH(CH₃)COOH

2-methylpropanoic acid

isobutyric acid

branch on C2



NAMING BRANCHED CARBOXYLIC ACIDS

  Example: (CH₃)₂CH–COOH



  STEP 1: Longest chain including COOH = 3 carbons → propan-

          Wait — is there a longer path? No. 3 C total including COOH.

          Actually count again: COOH is C1, CH is C2, two CH₃ branch.

          Main chain = 3 C if we go COOH–CH–CH₃ = propanoic

          Branch = CH₃ on C2 = methyl

  STEP 2: Functional group = COOH → -oic acid

  STEP 3: COOH is always C1 (start from COOH end)

  STEP 4: Methyl branch on C2

  STEP 5: Name = 2-methylpropanoic acid



19.11  Naming ESTERS (suffix: -oate)

Esters form from an ALCOHOL + CARBOXYLIC ACID reaction. The name has TWO PARTS: the ALKYL part (from the alcohol) + the CARBOXYLATE part (from the acid). Suffix = –oate.



ESTER NAME =  [alkyl group from alcohol]  +  [acid name changed to -oate]



Acid: ethanoic acid  +  Alcohol: methanol  →  METHYL ETHANOATE

      (from ethanoic)          (from methanol)    (methyl + ethanoate)



Acid: propanoic acid  +  Alcohol: ethanol  →  ETHYL PROPANOATE

Acid: methanoic acid  +  Alcohol: propanol →  PROPYL METHANOATE

Acid: butanoic acid   +  Alcohol: ethanol  →  ETHYL BUTANOATE  (pineapple smell!)



STRUCTURAL FORMULA → ESTER NAME

  General structure of ester:   R–COO–R'

                                │       │

                           acid part   alcohol part



  CH₃–COO–CH₃      →  methyl ethanoate

  ^─acid(ethanoic)  ^─alcohol(methanol → methyl)



  CH₃CH₂–COO–C₂H₅  →  ethyl propanoate

  ^─acid(propanoic)   ^─alcohol(ethanol → ethyl)



  HCOO–CH₂CH₂CH₃  →  propyl methanoate

  ^─acid(methanoic)   ^─alcohol(propanol → propyl)



  NOTE: Ester name = ALKYL first, then CARBOXYLATE second.

  (Opposite order from how they are drawn in the structure!)



TOPIC

DETAILS

Common Esters and Their Smells

Ethyl ethanoate — pear/nail polish remover Amyl acetate (pentyl ethanoate) — banana Ethyl butanoate — pineapple Propyl ethanoate — pear Methyl salicylate — wintergreen Octyl acetate — orange

Formation (Esterification)

R–COOH  +  HO–R'  ⇌  R–COO–R'  + H₂O (conc. H₂SO₄ catalyst, heat, reversible) Hydrolysis (acid/base) REVERSES esterification. Saponification (NaOH) makes soaps from fats.



🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The fruity flavours in sweets, soft drinks, and perfumes are mostly artificial esters! The 'strawberry' flavour in strawberry sweets is ethyl methanoate. The 'banana' flavour in banana candy is amyl acetate (pentyl ethanoate). The 'pineapple' flavour is ethyl butanoate. These esters are made cheaply and in large quantities by combining the right alcohol and acid with a drop of sulfuric acid — the same reaction you study in JAMB chemistry. The essential oil in Nigerian peppermint leaves contains menthyl esters that give the characteristic cool smell.



19.12  Naming AMINES (suffix: -amine or -yl amine)

Amines contain the –NH₂ group (primary amine). They are WEAK BASES. Named as alkylamines.



Structural / Condensed Formula

Key Feature

19.13  Naming HALOGENOALKANES (Haloalkanes)

When a halogen (F, Cl, Br, I) replaces a hydrogen on an alkane, the compound is a HALOGENOALKANE. The halogen is named as a SUBSTITUENT with a number: fluoro–, chloro–, bromo–, iodo–.



HALOGENOALKANE NAMING

  RULE: Halogen is treated as a SUBSTITUENT (like a branch).

        Name: [position]-[halogen prefix]-[alkane name]

        fluoro = F  |  chloro = Cl  |  bromo = Br  |  iodo = I



  CH₃Cl                   →  chloromethane    (Cl on C1, only 1 C)

  CH₃CH₂Cl               →  chloroethane     (Cl on C1 of 2-C chain)

  CH₃CHClCH₃             →  2-chloropropane  (Cl on C2 of 3-C chain)

  CH₃CH₂CH₂Br           →  1-bromopropane   (Br on C1)

  CH₂BrCH₂Br             →  1,2-dibromoethane (two Br, on C1 and C2)

  CHCl₃                  →  trichloromethane (chloroform — 3 Cl on C1)

  CCl₄                   →  tetrachloromethane (4 Cl on C1)

  CH₂=CHCl               →  chloroethene     (vinyl chloride — monomer for PVC!)



19.14  Functional Group Priority Order

When a molecule has MORE THAN ONE functional group, the GROUP WITH THE HIGHEST PRIORITY becomes the principal functional group and gives the SUFFIX. Lower-priority groups are named as PREFIXES.



FUNCTIONAL GROUP PRIORITY (HIGHEST to LOWEST)

  HIGHEST PRIORITY (suffix group — named at the END of the name):

  1.  –COOH   carboxylic acid  (–oic acid)

  2.  –COO–   ester            (–oate)

  3.  –CHO    aldehyde         (–al)

  4.  C=O     ketone           (–one)

  5.  –OH     alcohol          (–ol)

  6.  –NH₂    amine            (–amine)

  LOWEST PRIORITY (named as prefix — do NOT give suffix):

  7.  C=C     alkene           (add 'en' before suffix, e.g. but-3-en-1-ol)

  8.  C≡C    alkyne           (add 'yn' before suffix)

  9.  Halogens (F, Cl, Br, I)  (fluoro–, chloro–, bromo–, iodo–)

  10. Alkyl branches          (methyl–, ethyl–, etc.)



📝 WORKED EXAMPLE

Name: CH₂=CH–CH₂–OH  (contains both C=C and –OH)  Priority: –OH (rank 5) > C=C (rank 7) → –OH gives the suffix Chain = 3 C → prop- Suffix = -ol (from –OH) C=C is lower priority → named as 'en' within the name –OH on C1 (number from OH end), C=C between C2 and C3 Name: prop-2-en-1-ol   (also known as allyl alcohol)



19.15  Complete Worked Examples — From Structure to Name



WORKED EXAMPLES — Practice These Patterns

  Q1.  CH₃–CH₂–CH₂–CH₂–CH₂–CH₃

       6 carbons, all single bonds, no functional group

       Answer: hexane



  Q2.  CH₃–CH=CH–CH₂–CH₃

       5 carbons, one C=C on C2

       Answer: pent-2-ene



  Q3.  CH₃–CH₂–CH(OH)–CH₂–CH₃

       5 carbons, –OH on C3 (same from either end)

       Answer: pentan-3-ol



  Q4.  CH₃–CO–CH₂–CH₂–CH₃

       5 carbons, ketone C=O on C2

       Answer: pentan-2-one



  Q5.  CH₃–CH(CH₃)–CH(Cl)–CH₃

       Longest chain: 4 C → but-

       Cl on C2, methyl branch on C3 (number from Cl end for lowest)

       Wait — recheck: Cl side gives Cl=C2,branch=C3.

               Other end: Cl=C3, branch=C2. Alphabetical: Cl before methyl?

               Give lowest locant set: {2,3} either way. Then alphabetical.

       Answer: 2-chloro-3-methylbutane



  Q6.  CH₃–CH₂–COO–CH₂–CH₃

       This is an ester: acid part = CH₃CH₂COO– (propanoic acid)

       Alcohol part = –CH₂CH₃ (ethanol → ethyl)

       Answer: ethyl propanoate



  Q7.  CH₃–CH(NH₂)–CH₂–CH₃

       4 carbons, –NH₂ on C2

       Answer: butan-2-amine



19.16  Reverse: From Name Back to Structure

JAMB also asks you to DRAW the structure from a given IUPAC name. Use the reverse of the 5-step method:



NAME → STRUCTURE: The Reverse Method

  STEP 1: Read the PREFIX → draw a carbon chain of that length.

  STEP 2: Read the SUFFIX → add the functional group.

  STEP 3: Read the LOCANT NUMBER → place the group on the correct carbon.

  STEP 4: Read SUBSTITUENT names/numbers → add branches/groups.

  STEP 5: Fill in all remaining bonds with hydrogen atoms.



  Example: Draw 2-methylbutan-1-ol

  STEP 1: 'butan' = 4 C chain: C–C–C–C

  STEP 2: '–ol' = –OH group

  STEP 3: '1' = OH on C1: HO–C–C–C–C

  STEP 4: '2-methyl' = CH₃ branch on C2: HO–CH₂–CH(CH₃)–CH₂–CH₃

  STEP 5: Add H: HOCH₂CH(CH₃)CH₂CH₃  ✓



  Example: Draw 3-ethylpent-1-ene

  STEP 1: 'pent' = 5 C chain

  STEP 2: 'ene' = C=C double bond

  STEP 3: '1' = double bond starts at C1: C=C–C–C–C

  STEP 4: '3-ethyl' = C₂H₅ on C3: CH₂=CH–CH(C₂H₅)–CH₂–CH₃

  STEP 5: Already done above ✓



19.17  Common JAMB Mistakes — Avoid These!



MISTAKE

CORRECT APPROACH

❌ MISTAKE 1: Not finding the LONGEST chain

ALWAYS trace every possible path. The longest chain may go THROUGH a branch. E.g., in CH₃–CH(C₂H₅)–CH₃, trace through the ethyl branch: Longest chain = 5 C (not 3). Answer: 3-methylpentane... actually just pentane with methyl = 3-methylpentane. Recount carefully every time.

❌ MISTAKE 2: Wrong numbering direction

ALWAYS give the principal functional group (or first substituent) the LOWEST number. CH₃–CH(OH)–CH₂–CH₃: OH is on C2 from left OR C3 from right. C2 < C3, so number from left: butan-2-ol ✓ (NOT butan-3-ol ✘)

❌ MISTAKE 3: Forgetting to name the position

Once a chain is longer than 3 carbons and a group can be in more than one position, you MUST give the position number. but-1-ene ≠ but-2-ene. They are DIFFERENT compounds. butan-1-ol ≠ butan-2-ol. Always include the locant!

❌ MISTAKE 4: Aldehydes numbered as anything but C1

The –CHO carbon is ALWAYS C1. Never write '1-ethanal' — just 'ethanal'. You number other substituents from the CHO end.

❌ MISTAKE 5: Confusing ketone with aldehyde

Propan-1-one does NOT EXIST — that would be an aldehyde (propanal). The smallest ketone is propan-2-one. The C=O in a ketone must have C on BOTH sides.

❌ MISTAKE 6: Ester name order reversed

Ethyl ethanoate ≠ ethanoic ethyl. The ALKYL (from alcohol) always comes FIRST, the CARBOXYLATE comes SECOND. ethyl ethanoate ✓  |  propyl methanoate ✓



19.18  Quick Reference Summary — Cut Out and Keep!



IUPAC NAMING — FULL QUICK REFERENCE

  PREFIXES:  meth=1  eth=2  prop=3  but=4  pent=5  hex=6  hept=7  oct=8  non=9  dec=10



  SUFFIXES (by functional group):

  –ane   = alkane (single bonds)      –ene  = alkene (C=C)

  –yne   = alkyne (C≡C)               –ol   = alcohol (–OH)

  –al    = aldehyde (–CHO, at end)     –one  = ketone (C=O, in middle)

  –oic acid = carboxylic acid (–COOH) –oate = ester (–COO–)

  –amine = amine (–NH₂)



  SUBSTITUENT PREFIXES (used when group is NOT the principal one):

  methyl–(CH₃)  ethyl–(C₂H₅)  propyl–(C₃H₇)  butyl–(C₄H₉)

  fluoro–(F)    chloro–(Cl)    bromo–(Br)    iodo–(I)

  hydroxy–(–OH)  oxo–(C=O)   amino–(–NH₂)



  MULTIPLYING PREFIXES: di=2  tri=3  tetra=4  penta=5  hexa=6



  5-STEP METHOD: (1) Find longest chain → (2) Identify principal group →

  (3) Number from end nearest principal group → (4) Name substituents →

  (5) Assemble: [substituents alphabetically]-[prefix][suffix]



  RULES: Numbers-to-letters: hyphen  |  Numbers-to-numbers: comma

         Alphabetise substituents (ignore di/tri/tetra when alphabetising)

         Lowest locant set wins. If tied, alphabetical order of substituents.



🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

IUPAC stands for 'International Union of Pure and Applied Chemistry'. It was founded in 1919 and is headquartered in Zurich, Switzerland. Before IUPAC naming was standardised, the same compound could have dozens of different names in different countries — causing enormous confusion in science and industry. Today, IUPAC names are used by every chemist, pharmacist, and chemical engineer in the world. When a Nigerian pharmacist reads the label 'propan-2-ol' on a disinfectant, they know exactly what chemical it is because of IUPAC's universal system.



🌟 DID YOU KNOW?

The world's longest systematic IUPAC name belongs to Titin, a protein found in human muscle. Its full IUPAC name has 189,819 letters and takes over 3 hours to pronounce! It begins with 'methionylthreonylthreonylglutaminylarginyl...' This is why proteins are usually given short common names. Even so, for smaller molecules like drugs and solvents, IUPAC names are essential — the common pain-killer paracetamol has the IUPAC name 'N-(4-hydroxyphenyl)acetamide'. Every tablet you take was manufactured using IUPAC-named chemicals.

HALOGENS (Group 17): F > Cl > Br > I reactivity. Each displaces less reactive halide from solution.

All form HX (hydrohalic acids): HI strongest acid, HF weakest (strongest H-F bond).

INDUSTRIAL PROCESSES:

Haber: N2 + 3H2 <-> 2NH3 (Fe, 450°C, 200 atm)

Contact: 2SO2 + O2 <-> 2SO3 (V2O5, 450°C) -> H2SO4

Ostwald: 4NH3 + 5O2 -> 4NO (Pt/Rh, 850°C) -> HNO3

Solvay: NaCl + NH3 + CO2 + H2O -> NaHCO3 -> Na2CO3

REACTIVITY SERIES (high to low):

K > Na > Ca > Mg > Al > Zn > Fe > Ni > Sn > Pb > H > Cu > Ag > Au

Metals above H displace H2 from dilute acids. Extraction by electrolysis (reactive metals) or reduction (less reactive).

ORGANIC CHEMISTRY:

Alkanes (CnH2n+2): free radical substitution with halogens (UV)

Alkenes (CnH2n): electrophilic addition; Markovnikov's rule for HX

Alkynes (CnH2n-2): addition reactions

Alcohols (-OH): oxidation, esterification, dehydration to alkene

Carboxylic acids (-COOH): esterification (with H2SO4 catalyst), neutralization

Esters: hydrolysis (acid + alcohol) or saponification (soap making)

💡 EXAM TIP:  HOMOLOGOUS SERIES: Members differ by CH2. Same general formula. Similar chemical properties. Physical properties change gradually with chain length.

Q255.  (JAMB 1983)  

The main use of chlorine from the chlor-alkali industry is

A.  bleaching only

B.  making PVC, disinfecting water, and making bleach

C.  making HCl only

D.  making NaOH







  📋  ANSWERS & DETAILED EXPLANATIONS  



Question 1

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2+H2O=HCl+HOCl. HOCl OXIDISES/DECOLOURISES dyes. HOCl is the bleaching agent.



Question 2

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2 test: moist blue litmus = RED (acidic) then WHITE (bleaching/oxidising). Definitive test.



Question 3

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  F: smallest atom, high nuclear charge (9 protons), electrons very close. Greatest pull on electrons. EN=4.0.



Question 4

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Down Group VII: atomic radius increases. Electron gained farther from nucleus. DECREASES reactivity. F>Cl>Br>I.



Question 5

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  I- most easily OXIDISED. Down group: easier to lose electron. Reducing power: I->Br->Cl->F-.



Question 6

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  S+O2=SO2 (sulphur dioxide). Direct burning gives SO2. SO3 requires catalyst.



Question 7

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Carbon allotropes: DIAMOND, GRAPHITE, FULLERENES (C60). Most commonly tested.



Question 8

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2+2KI=2KCl+I2. I2+starch=BLUE-BLACK. Test for Cl2.



Question 9

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  C2H5OH=(conc H2SO4, 170C)=C2H4+H2O. Dehydration of ethanol gives ETHENE.



Question 10

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NaCl+H2SO4(conc)=NaHSO4+HCl on gentle heating. Standard lab preparation.



Question 11

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALLOTROPY: same element in two or more different PHYSICAL FORMS. E.g. diamond/graphite/fullerene.



Question 12

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2+2NaOH=NaCl+NaOCl+H2O (cold dilute). Cl: 0 to -1(NaCl) AND 0 to +1(NaOCl). NaOCl=BLEACH.



Question 13

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  GRAPHITE: each C forms 3 bonds. 4th electron per C is DELOCALISED between layers. Mobile electrons = conducts.



Question 14

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Dry cell (Leclanche): electrolyte=NH4Cl PASTE. Zinc anode. Carbon+MnO2 cathode. 1.5V.



Question 15

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  N=N: bond energy=945 kJ/mol. One of strongest bonds. Enormous energy to break.



Question 16

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  SO2 test: turns moist ORANGE K2Cr2O7 paper GREEN. SO2 reduces Cr6+(orange) to Cr3+(green).



Question 17

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  DIAMOND: hardest natural material (10 Mohs). Each C: 4 strong covalent bonds in 3D network.



Question 18

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  GRAPHITE: SOFTEST (1-2 Mohs). Layers held by weak van der Waals. Layers slide. Lubricant, pencil lead.



Question 19

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BLEACHING POWDER=Ca(OCl)Cl. Made: Ca(OH)2+Cl2=Ca(OCl)Cl+H2O. OCl- is bleaching agent.



Question 20

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2O2: O is -1. Can be OXIDISED (-1 to 0, gives O2) or REDUCED (-1 to -2, gives H2O).



Question 21

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  WHITE PHOSPHORUS: spontaneously IGNITES IN AIR at ~34C. Stored under WATER to prevent O2 contact.



Question 22

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  FRASCH PROCESS: molten sulphur pumped from underground using superheated steam. 99.5% pure sulphur.



Question 23

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Haber: ~15% conversion per pass. Unreacted gases RECYCLED after NH3 liquefied and removed.



Question 24

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2+6NaOH(hot,conc)=5NaCl+NaClO3+3H2O. Hot conc NaOH gives SODIUM CHLORATE.



Question 25

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2S: (1) ROTTEN EGG smell. (2) Turns lead acetate paper BLACK: Pb(CH3COO)2+H2S=PbS(black).



Question 26

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  SO2: ANTIOXIDANT (prevents oxidation) and ANTIMICROBIAL (kills bacteria). E220. Wines, dried fruits.



Question 27

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2+H2O=HCl+HOCl. HOCl (hypochlorous acid) is active BLEACHING agent.



Question 28

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Diamond: ALL strong C-C covalent bonds in 3D network. ALL must be broken to melt. Very high MP (3550C).



Question 29

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  AgNO3+KI=AgI (YELLOW precipitate). Insoluble in dilute HNO3 and in NH3. Identifies I-.



Question 30

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  LIMITED O2: 2C+O2=2CO (incomplete combustion). Excess O2: C+O2=CO2 (complete).



Question 31

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SiO2=GIANT COVALENT: each Si bonded to 4 O. 3D network. Very high MP (~1710C). Found as quartz, sand.



Question 32

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Cl2 water is acidic (HCl+HOCl). Universal indicator turns RED. Then HOCl bleaches the indicator WHITE.



Question 33

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  H2 test: SQUEAKY POP when burning splint applied. 2H2+O2=2H2O. Rapid combustion.



Question 34

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BROMINE: only halogen that is LIQUID at RT. BP=59C, MP=-7C. Reddish-brown liquid.



Question 35

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Al forms THIN PROTECTIVE Al2O3 layer (passivation). Prevents further oxidation.



Question 36

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  HALL-HEROULT: bauxite purified to Al2O3, dissolved in cryolite, electrolysed ~950C.



Question 37

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  STEEL: Fe + C (0.1-2% carbon). STAINLESS STEEL: Fe+Cr+Ni.



Question 38

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALUMINIUM: most abundant metal in Earth's crust (~8% by mass).



Question 39

✅ ANSWER: D

📝 EXPLANATION:  IRON: Fe2O3+3CO=2Fe+3CO2 (blast furnace). Na, K, Al: too reactive, electrolysis needed.



Question 40

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BLAST FURNACE produces CAST/PIG IRON: 4% carbon + Si, Mn, S, P impurities. Brittle.



Question 41

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  GALVANISATION: Zn acts as SACRIFICIAL ANODE if coating damaged. Fe protected.



Question 42

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BRONZE=Cu+Sn. BRASS=Cu+Zn.



Question 43

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ELECTROLYTIC REFINING: impure Cu anode dissolves. Pure Cu deposits on cathode.



Question 44

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALKANES: CnH2n+2. C4H8=CnH2n=ALKENE formula. Not an alkane.



Question 45

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALCOHOLS contain HYDROXYL group (-OH).



Question 46

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ESTERIFICATION: RCOOH+R'OH=RCOOR'+H2O. Catalyst: concentrated H2SO4. Heat under reflux.



Question 47

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  C6H12O6=2C2H5OH+2CO2. Yeast/zymase enzyme. Anaerobic. 25-37C.



Question 48

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  Complete combustion: CnH(2n+2)+O2(excess)=nCO2+(n+1)H2O.



Question 49

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALKENES decolourise BROMINE WATER (C=C+Br2=CBrCBr). ALKANES do NOT react at RT.



Question 50

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  SAPONIFICATION: RCOOR'+NaOH=RCOONa+R'OH. Ester+NaOH=soap+alcohol.



Question 51

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALKENES: one C=C double bond. General formula CnH2n.



Question 52

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  nCH2=CH2=(-CH2-CH2-)n = POLYETHENE (POLYTHENE). Addition polymer.



Question 53

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CH3CH2OH: 2 carbons+OH. eth=2 carbons. ETHANOL.



Question 54

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CRACKING: long-chain alkane=(heat/catalyst)=shorter alkanes+ALKENES+H2.



Question 55

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NYLON: condensation polymer. Diamine+dicarboxylic acid=polyamide+H2O.



Question 56

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  C2H5OH=(conc H2SO4, 170C)=C2H4+H2O. DEHYDRATION gives ETHENE.



Question 57

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BROMINE WATER: alkene DECOLOURISES brown Br2(aq) to colourless. Addition across C=C.



Question 58

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  BENZENE: 6 pi electrons delocalised over ring. Aromatic stabilisation. Prefers substitution not addition.



Question 59

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  C2H5OH=(K2Cr2O7/H2SO4)=CH3CHO=(further)=CH3COOH (ethanoic acid).



Question 60

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  GLYCEROL=propane-1,2,3-triol. THREE -OH groups. TRIHYDRIC alcohol. Component of fats.



Question 61

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  ESTER: contains -COO- group. CH3COOC2H5=ethyl ethanoate (ethyl acetate).



Question 62

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  PROPANOIC ACID: 3 carbon chain with -COOH. C2H5COOH=CH3CH2COOH.



Question 63

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  DoU=(2x4+2-6)/2=4/2=2. Two degrees: e.g. two double bonds (butadiene) or one triple bond.



Question 64

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CH3COOH+NaHCO3=CH3COONa+H2O+CO2. EFFERVESCENCE. Carboxylic acid test.



Question 65

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Hydration: CH2=CH2+H2O=(H3PO4 catalyst, heat)=CH3CH2OH. Industrial ethanol.



Question 66

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALDEHYDES: (1)TOLLENS: Ag(NH3)2++RCHO=Ag (silver mirror). (2)FEHLING: Cu2+(blue)+RCHO=Cu2O(red).



Question 67

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  NYLON-6,6: hexanedioic acid (6C)+hexane-1,6-diamine (6C)=polyamide+H2O.



Question 68

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  NATURAL RUBBER (polyisoprene): polymer of ISOPRENE (CH2=C(CH3)-CH=CH2).



Question 69

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  ALKANE+Cl2=(UV)=chloroalkane+HCl. FREE RADICAL SUBSTITUTION.



Question 70

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  AMINO ACIDS: BOTH -NH2 (amino) and -COOH (carboxyl). Form proteins by condensation (peptide bonds).



Question 71

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  Both C4H10 but different carbon chain: butane (linear) vs methylpropane (branched). CHAIN isomerism.



Question 72

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  SUCROSE=glucose+fructose=DISACCHARIDE. Glucose and fructose are monosaccharides. Starch=polysaccharide.



Question 73

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  INDUSTRIAL ETHANOL: hydration of ethene. H3PO4 catalyst, 300C, 60 atm. Continuous process.



Question 74

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  PVC: addition polymer of vinyl chloride (CH2=CHCl). Repeat unit: (-CH2-CHCl-)n.



Question 75

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CH3COOH+C2H5OH=(conc H2SO4)=CH3COOC2H5+H2O. ESTERIFICATION.

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  CONTACT PROCESS: S=SO2=SO3(V2O5)=oleum=H2SO4. Makes SULPHURIC ACID.



Question 77

✅ ANSWER: A

📝 EXPLANATION:  SOLVAY (Na2CO3): NaCl+NH3+CO2+H2O. NH3 recovered and recycled.



Question 78

✅ ANSWER: C

📝 EXPLANATION:  HABER PROCESS: N2+3H2=2NH3 (Fe catalyst, 450C, 200 atm). Makes AMMONIA.



Question 79

✅ ANSWER: C

✅ ANSWER: C

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  V2O5 catalyses 2SO2+O2=2SO3. Best at ~450C.



Question 83

✅ ANSWER: B

📝 EXPLANATION:  UREA: M=60. N=28. %N=46.7%. HIGHEST nitrogen content of common solid fertilisers.



Question 84

✅ ANSWER: B
